<GlossaryHero
  badge="THE B.A.D. GLOSSARY · FIELD REFERENCE"
  title="Threat Behavior & Adversary Reference"
  subParts={[
    { strong: "B" }, "ehaviors. ",
    { strong: "A" }, "dversaries. ",
    { strong: "D" }, "amage.",
  ]}
>
  A working reference to the language of compromise: what attackers do, who they are, and the damage they leave behind. The techniques, the actors, the campaigns, and the artifacts an analyst will name out loud during triage. The technique sections follow an intrusion from first access to data theft; vulnerabilities, malware, actors and campaigns come after. Each technique, malware and actor card ends with a triage signal (what the behavior looks like in the evidence), and each campaign card ends with the lesson it holds for triage.

{/* ============================================================
     1. CORE ADVERSARIAL TECHNIQUES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="core" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><circle cx="12" cy="12" r="10"/><circle cx="12" cy="12" r="6"/><circle cx="12" cy="12" r="2"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Core adversarial techniques</h2>
      <p>Foundational behaviors, attacker techniques, and broad offensive concepts.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/alert/">Alert</a> · <a href="/docs/uncover/mitre/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="advanced-persistent-threat-apt" class="bad-term"><h3>Advanced Persistent Threat (APT)</h3><p>A sophisticated, well-resourced adversary, often state-sponsored, that gains and maintains prolonged, covert unauthorized access to a network while avoiding detection. The term names the actor; the campaign it runs is APT activity. Vendors track the same group under different names: numbered APT groups for well-established actors, and provisional labels such as Mandiant's UNC clusters or Microsoft's Storm- names for activity not yet tied to a known group.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Attribution is rarely a triage question. What you can see is the tradecraft: long dwell time, valid accounts, edge-device exploitation and living-off-the-land tools. Record the observed behavior and leave the name to threat intelligence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/advanced_persistent_threat" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="ai-agent-abuse" class="bad-term"><h3>AI Agent Abuse</h3><p>Hijacking or misusing an AI agent's tool access (mail, files, code execution, APIs) so it acts for the attacker, usually through prompt injection in content it reads, a poisoned tool or MCP server, or stolen agent credentials. The agent's permissions become the attacker's, and its actions look like normal automation.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Agent tool calls or data sends the user did not request, especially right after the agent read external content; new MCP servers, plugins or connectors; and agent tokens used from outside the agent's host. Review the agent's own action log, not only the user's.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://atlas.mitre.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: MITRE ATLAS</a></article>
    <article id="application-shimming" class="bad-term"><h3>Application Shimming</h3><p>Abuses the Windows Application Compatibility Infrastructure (shims) to inject malicious code or maintain persistence. An ATT&amp;CK Persistence and Privilege Escalation technique.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> sdbinst.exe running outside a software install, new .sdb files under C:\Windows\AppPatch\Custom, or new values under the AppCompatFlags Custom and InstalledSDB registry keys.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1546/011/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1546.011</a></article>
    <article id="logic-attack" class="bad-term"><h3>Business Logic Abuse</h3><p>Exploiting flaws in an application's workflow rather than its code: replaying a single-use coupon, skipping a payment step, or abusing a password-reset flow to take over an account. Every request looks valid on its own, so signature-based controls rarely fire; the signal is a sequence that the business process should not allow.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A per-account or per-session sequence the workflow should not allow: the same coupon redeemed many times, an order confirmed with no payment event, or a reset completed from a session that never received the reset link. Application and business-event logs carry it, not the WAF.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://owasp.org/www-community/vulnerabilities/Business_logic_vulnerability" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: OWASP</a></article>
    <article id="collection" class="bad-term"><h3>Collection</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK tactic: gathering the data the attacker came for (file shares, mailboxes, screenshots, keystrokes) before exfiltration. Archive tools such as 7-Zip or WinRAR creating large password-protected archives are a common tell.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> One account reading files across many shares or folders in a short window, mailbox searches or exports (New-MailboxExportRequest, eDiscovery content searches) by someone outside legal or IT, and screenshots or clipboard captures by an unfamiliar process.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0009/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0009</a></article>
    <article id="com-hijacking" class="bad-term"><h3>COM Hijacking</h3><p>A persistence technique that abuses Windows Component Object Model (COM) registry entries to hijack the execution flow of legitimate applications.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> New or changed InprocServer32 or LocalServer32 values under HKCU\Software\Classes\CLSID pointing to a DLL in a user-writable path, overriding a CLSID registered under HKLM (Sysmon events 12 and 13).</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1546/015/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1546.015</a></article>
    <article id="compromised-host" class="bad-term"><h3>Compromised Host</h3><p>A system under attacker control, often used for lateral movement, staging malware, or maintaining persistence within a network.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A conclusion, not an alert: you call a host compromised when the evidence shows attacker-controlled execution on it. Record that evidence and your confidence, because the label drives containment and scoping.</p></article>
    <article id="defense-impairment" class="bad-term"><h3>Defense Impairment</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK tactic: breaking security mechanisms, pipelines, and tooling so defenders cannot see or trust what is happening: disabling security tools such as endpoint detection and response (EDR) agents (EDR killers, BYOVD), modifying firewalls, and tampering with logging and trust controls. Created in ATT&amp;CK v19 (April 2026) when Defense Evasion was split; the old T1562 Impair Defenses techniques now sit here, chiefly as T1685 Disable or Modify Tools. Pre-ransomware EDR killing is among the highest-signal events an analyst can catch.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A security agent that stops reporting, a new Defender exclusion or tamper event (Defender event 5007), a stopped log forwarder, or a cleared log. Silence from a sensor is evidence: find out why the telemetry stopped before you conclude nothing happened.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0112/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0112</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1685/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1685</a></article>
    <article id="discovery" class="bad-term"><h3>Discovery</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK tactic: learning the environment after access (whoami, net group "Domain Admins", nltest, AdFind, BloodHound, cloud role enumeration). A burst of enumeration commands from one account or host is one of the earliest hands-on-keyboard signals.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Several enumeration commands (whoami /all, net group, nltest /domain_trusts, AdFind) from one account within minutes, or a burst of LDAP queries from a workstation, as BloodHound collection produces. Admins run these too, so weigh the account and the host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0007/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0007</a></article>
    <article id="execution" class="bad-term"><h3>Execution</h3><p>The ATT&amp;CK tactic (TA0002) for running attacker-controlled code on a local or remote system: through command interpreters, user-run files and lures, scheduled tasks, WMI, or exploits. It rarely stands alone: attackers execute code to discover, steal, move or persist.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process whose parent does not fit its role: Office or a browser spawning a shell, a script host running from a download folder, or a signed binary with an unusual command line. Process-creation telemetry (Sysmon event 1, Security 4688 with command line) is the evidence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0002</a></article>
    <article id="impact" class="bad-term"><h3>Impact (ATT&amp;CK Tactic)</h3><p>The tactic for the damage itself (TA0040); not to be confused with ASSURED's impact axis, which C.L.E.A.R. defines. Its techniques include Data Encrypted for Impact (T1486), Inhibit System Recovery such as shadow-copy deletion with vssadmin or bcdedit (T1490), Service Stop (T1489), data destruction, and Resource Hijacking such as cryptomining (T1496). Shadow-copy deletion and backup-service kills usually come minutes before encryption.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Shadow-copy deletion (vssadmin delete shadows, wmic shadowcopy delete) or bcdedit recovery changes, backup and database services stopped in a batch, or a sudden spike in file renames. Any one of these on a server needs an immediate look; confirmed, it fires the escalation tripwire.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0040/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0040</a></article>
    <article id="initial-access" class="bad-term"><h3>Initial Access</h3><p>The ATT&amp;CK tactic covering how attackers first gain a foothold inside the environment: phishing, exploited edge devices and public-facing applications, valid or stolen credentials, and exposed remote services. Reconnaissance and Resource Development come before it.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The first event that does not fit: a sign-in from a new device or hosting IP, a child process under an internet-facing service, a user-run file from a download or email path. Record when and how it happened, or that it is still unknown, because the rest of the case hangs off this point.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0001</a></article>
    <article id="initial-access-broker" class="bad-term"><h3>Initial Access Broker (IAB)</h3><p>A criminal who breaks in, or buys infostealer logs, and sells the resulting access (VPN credentials, RDP, a web shell) to ransomware and extortion crews. Explains why the intrusion you are triaging may have started weeks earlier under a different actor.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A gap between first access and hands-on activity: a valid-account or web-shell login weeks before the ransomware stage, often from different infrastructure. When you find a foothold, look back for earlier activity on the same credential or host.</p></article>
    <article id="insider" class="bad-term"><h3>Insider</h3><p>Any person who has or had authorized access to, or knowledge of, an organization's resources: employees, contractors, vendors and partners, including people who have left while their access or knowledge remains. The word, in CISA's definition, says nothing about intent; see Insider Risk and Insider Threat.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Insider status is a fact about the account, not a finding: a valid account and expected access are where an insider case starts. Record what the account did. HR-held context reaches the case only through the insider-risk route, never as analyst-collected notes.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/topics/physical-security/insider-threat-mitigation/defining-insider-threats" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA</a></article>
    <article id="insider-risk" class="bad-term"><h3>Insider Risk</h3><p>The potential for harm from anyone with legitimate access, whether through mistakes, negligence, policy violations, well-meaning workarounds or deliberate misuse. It is the umbrella term, and most insider risk is not malicious. Insider threat is the deliberate subset. Some frameworks, including CISA's, fold the whole spectrum into 'insider threat', so map these labels to your organization's policy vocabulary.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Observable technical activity only: unusual data volume, new destinations, access outside the role, bypassed policy controls. HR-held context reaches the case only through the insider-risk route, never as analyst-collected notes.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/topics/physical-security/insider-threat-mitigation/defining-insider-threats" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA</a></article>
    <article id="insider-risk-management-irm" class="bad-term"><h3>Insider Risk Management (IRM)</h3><p>The program and tool category that watches for insider risk: user and entity behavior analytics (UEBA), data loss prevention (DLP) and privileged access management (PAM), often joined to HR data through connectors. It is deployed only after legal and privacy review (and works-council review where one exists), and it is the route by which HR-held context reaches a case.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> IRM alerts arrive as policy matches on technical activity (data movement, access outside the role). Work them like any alert: record what the account did. HR-held context reaches the case only through the insider-risk route, never as analyst-collected notes.</p></article>
    <article id="insider-threat" class="bad-term"><h3>Insider Threat</h3><p>A person with authorized access who uses it to harm the organization. CISA's definition covers malicious, complacent and unintentional acts; many programs, ASSURED included, reserve 'insider threat' for the malicious subset and use 'insider risk' for the broader set.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The same evidence as any insider case: what the account did, from where, to which data. Intent is not a triage call; record the activity and route it through the insider-risk process.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/topics/physical-security/insider-threat-mitigation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA</a></article>
    <article id="jailbreaking" class="bad-term"><h3>Jailbreaking</h3><p>Two senses. (1) Mobile: removing iOS or Android platform restrictions, usually by the device owner and sometimes by an attacker; for triage, a jailbroken or rooted device fails MDM compliance and cannot be trusted to enforce app sandboxing. (2) AI: prompts crafted to make a large language model ignore its safety policy; related to prompt injection (OWASP LLM01) and catalogued in MITRE ATLAS.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Mobile: an MDM compliance failure for a jailbroken or rooted device that still holds corporate sessions. AI: prompts in the AI gateway or audit log telling the model to ignore its instructions or role-play past its policy, followed by output the policy should have blocked.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: OWASP LLM01</a></article>
    <article id="lateral-movement" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Lateral Movement</h3><p>Moving from the initial foothold to other hosts, identities or tenants to find sensitive data or reach higher-value targets (ATT&amp;CK tactic TA0008). It usually rides legitimate remote-access protocols (RDP, SMB, WinRM, SSH) with stolen credentials, or replays a stolen session into a cloud or SaaS tenant, so the signal is an account authenticating where it does not normally go. Each hop widens the blast radius, and confirmed lateral movement is an escalation criterion.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Network logons (4624 type 3) or RDP logons (type 10) fanning out from one source, remote service creation (7045), or PsExec-style named pipes. The source host is your next scoping target.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0008/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0008</a></article>
    <article id="leaver-window" class="bad-term"><h3>Leaver Window</h3><p>The period around a known departure, roughly the notice period, when insider data theft is most likely: CERT research on insider theft of intellectual property found that many of those who took data did so within about a month of leaving. The departure date reaches the SOC as a date, not as a reason to suspect anyone.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Bulk repository clones, mass downloads, uploads to personal storage or USB writes during the notice period, measured against the person's own baseline and peer baselines. See <a href="/docs/subject/insider-analysis/">Subject: insider analysis</a>.</p></article>
    <article id="lethal-trifecta" class="bad-term"><h3>Lethal Trifecta</h3><p>An AI agent that combines three capabilities: access to private data, exposure to untrusted content, and a way to send data out (links or images it renders, web fetches, email, tool calls). With all three, a prompt injection hidden in the untrusted content can make the agent read the private data and send it to the attacker. Removing any one leg breaks that path. The name is Simon Willison's (June 2025). A triage assistant holds the first two legs by design, so ASSURED's admission gate removes the third; see <a href="/docs/ai-governance/">Governing automated triage</a>.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> In a case involving an AI assistant or agent, list the three legs: what data it could read, what untrusted content it processed, and which outbound channels it could use. An agent with all three that read attacker-reachable content is a possible exfiltration path; check its action log for sends, fetches or rendered links the user did not request.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://simonwillison.net/2025/Jun/16/the-lethal-trifecta/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Simon Willison (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="malware" class="bad-term"><h3>Malware</h3><p>Software designed to disrupt, damage, or gain unauthorized access to systems. Encompasses ransomware, trojans, worms, viruses, spyware, wipers, rootkits, and the other malware types catalogued below.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A detection name tells you the family at best. Establish whether it executed (process, network or persistence evidence), not only whether a file was written. A file that a control blocked or quarantined, with no execution or follow-on evidence, is the contained-by-control path (recorded at P3 or P4, a pattern-entry candidate); malware that ran is a different case.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/malware" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="persistence" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Persistence</h3><p>The ATT&amp;CK tactic for keeping access across reboots, updates and password changes. Mature actors plant several anchors: Run keys, scheduled tasks, services, WMI event subscriptions, and in the cloud, OAuth apps and added MFA methods.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> New Run keys, services (7045), scheduled tasks (4698), WMI subscriptions (Sysmon 19 to 21), and in the cloud new OAuth consents, app credentials or MFA methods. When you find one anchor, hunt for the others: actors rarely plant only one.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0003</a></article>
    <article id="post-exploitation" class="bad-term"><h3>Post-Exploitation</h3><p>Everything the attacker does after the first foothold: exploring the environment, escalating access, exfiltrating data, or setting up long-term control.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Hands-on-keyboard behavior after the first alert: discovery commands, credential access, remote logons. Its presence moves the case from 'was there a foothold?' to 'how far did they get?'.</p></article>
    <article id="privilege-chaining" class="bad-term"><h3>Privilege Chaining</h3><p>The sequential use of multiple smaller privilege escalations or credential compromises to progressively gain more powerful access. Each step may look minor on its own (a misconfigured service, a readable credential file, an over-broad group membership), which is why tools such as BloodHound model attack paths rather than single permissions.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Several small privilege changes on one identity or host in a short span: a new group membership, a token or service abuse, then a logon to a more sensitive tier. Score the chain, not each step.</p></article>
    <article id="privilege-escalation" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Privilege Escalation</h3><p>The ATT&amp;CK tactic for gaining higher permissions: on the endpoint, standard user to admin to SYSTEM; in the directory or cloud, a regular account to domain admin or a privileged cloud role.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A SYSTEM or root process whose parent ran as a standard user, a new member in a privileged group (4728, 4732, 4756), or a privileged cloud role assigned outside change management.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0004</a></article>
    <article id="prompt-injection" class="bad-term"><h3>Prompt Injection</h3><p>Text that an AI system reads as instructions instead of data: typed directly by a user, or hidden in an email, web page or document the model processes (indirect prompt injection). In agentic tools it can trigger actions with the agent's permissions. OWASP ranks it first among LLM risks (LLM01).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> In AI tool logs, an assistant or agent acting on instructions that came from content it read (an email, a page, a document) rather than from the user, especially a tool call, link or data send the user never asked for.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: OWASP LLM01</a></article>
    <article id="reconnaissance" class="bad-term"><h3>Reconnaissance</h3><p>The pre-attack phase where attackers gather intelligence, enumerate exposed services, and identify vulnerabilities before launching an intrusion. ATT&amp;CK v19.2 also tracks attackers querying public AI services to research targets (T1682).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Mostly invisible: it happens on public sources. You see its edges: scanning of external services, account-enumeration probes against sign-in endpoints, and phishing that cites details only an org chart would give.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0043/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0043</a></article>
    <article id="remote-execution" class="bad-term"><h3>Remote Execution</h3><p>Running commands or malicious code on a target system from another host: PsExec and remote service creation, WMI and WinRM, scheduled tasks created remotely, SSH, or an exploit against a network service. Administrators do all of this legitimately, so the account and the source host decide the call.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process on the target whose parent is services.exe, WmiPrvSE.exe or wsmprovhost.exe, preceded by a network logon from another host. Pair the target's process telemetry with the source's logon.</p></article>
    <article id="resource-development" class="bad-term"><h3>Resource Development</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK tactic: building or acquiring what the attack needs before it starts (registering look-alike domains, buying access or infostealer logs, compromising servers or routers to use as relays, staging tools).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Usually outside your telemetry. The traces arrive later: a newly registered look-alike domain in a phishing email, a sign-in from compromised router or VPS infrastructure, or one of your credentials in infostealer logs.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0042/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0042</a></article>
    <article id="sandbox-escape" class="bad-term"><h3>Sandbox Escape</h3><p>An attack that breaks out of a restricted environment (browser sandbox, container, or VM) to reach the underlying host. Most often seen as browser sandbox escapes chained with a renderer bug. ATT&amp;CK T1611 Escape to Host covers both container breakouts and virtual-machine-to-hypervisor escapes (for example, on ESXi).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A browser, container or VM process spawning something it should never spawn (a shell from a browser renderer, a host process from a container), or an unexpected kernel or hypervisor crash on the host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1611/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1611</a></article>
    <article id="sql-injection" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>SQL Injection</h3><p>Code injection where crafted input changes the SQL query an application sends to its database, letting the attacker read or change data, bypass logins, and sometimes run commands on the database server. Prevented by parameterized queries; web application firewalls catch common payloads but not all of them.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> SQL syntax in request parameters (quotes, UNION SELECT, sleep or WAITFOR DELAY), database errors returned to the client, or response sizes that jump for one parameter. Check whether the database logged matching queries before you call it successful.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://community.owasp.org/attacks/SQL_Injection" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: OWASP</a></article>
    <article id="staged-payloads" class="bad-term"><h3>Staged Payloads</h3><p>A two-phase delivery process where a lightweight stager downloads and executes a more complex secondary payload. Payload deployment, the delivery and installation of the attacker's tools, usually works this way: a dropper, script or stager fetches and runs the real implant.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A small first-stage process (script host, mshta, rundll32, a loader DLL) making an outbound request, then a new process or injected memory within seconds. Capture the second-stage URL: it often names the C2.</p><span id="payload-deployment" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="stealth" class="bad-term"><h3>Stealth</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK tactic: hiding and concealing actions so they appear as normal behavior, through obfuscation, masquerading, process injection, and indicator removal. In ATT&amp;CK v19 (April 2026), Defense Evasion was split: Stealth kept the TA0005 ID, and the new Defense Impairment (TA0112) took the defense-breaking techniques. Rules written against 'Defense Evasion' still match TA0005, but not Defense Impairment.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Something trusted behaving in an untrusted way: a system binary with an odd command line or path, a signed process loading an unsigned DLL, encoded scripts. The name looks normal; the context does not.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0005</a></article>
    <article id="system-tampering" class="bad-term"><h3>System Tampering</h3><p>The unauthorized modification of system configurations, binaries, or behaviors to support attacker goals or disrupt operations: changing boot settings, replacing or patching system files, editing the hosts file or firewall rules, or weakening security settings. In ATT&amp;CK terms most of it falls under Defense Impairment or Impact.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Configuration changes with no change record: bcdedit, netsh advfirewall, hosts-file writes, registry edits to security settings, or system binaries that fail signature checks.</p></article>
    <article id="toxic-combination" class="bad-term"><h3>Toxic Combination</h3><p>An access-governance term: multiple individually benign roles or permissions that together create excessive, dangerous access (the classic separation-of-duties conflict). A condition attackers hunt for and access reviews exist to catch.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An access review or cloud entitlement tool flagging one identity that can both create and approve a payment, or both write and deploy code. In a case, check whether the activity under review used such a combination.</p></article>
    <article id="uac-bypass" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>UAC Bypass</h3><p>Circumventing Windows User Account Control prompts so a process run by a local administrator reaches high integrity without the consent prompt. It does not elevate a standard user.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A high-integrity process spawned by an auto-elevating binary (fodhelper.exe, eventvwr.exe, computerdefaults.exe) right after a registry change under HKCU\Software\Classes\ms-settings or mscfile.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1548/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1548.002</a></article>
    <article id="wmi-obfuscation" class="bad-term"><h3>WMI Event Subscription</h3><p>A filter, consumer and binding stored in the WMI repository that runs attacker code when an event fires (logon, a timer). It is fileless and survives reboots. A Persistence technique; for WMI used to run commands, see WMI Abuse under Execution.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Sysmon events 19, 20 and 21 (WMI filter, consumer and binding), WMI-Activity event 5861, or Get-CimInstance against root\subscription returning a CommandLineEventConsumer or ActiveScriptEventConsumer you cannot explain.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1546/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1546.003</a></article>
    <article id="zero-day" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Zero Day</h3><p>A vulnerability with no available patch, either because the vendor does not yet know about it or has not yet shipped a fix. Exploitation is not required for the term; when attackers exploit it before a fix exists, that is a zero-day attack (the NIST definition cited here). Once a patch exists but has not been applied, the bug is an n-day.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> No signature exists yet, so detection is behavioral: an edge or server process doing something new (spawning a shell, writing files to web directories, new outbound connections). Check vendor advisories and the CISA KEV catalog against your exposed versions.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/zero_day_attack" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Lateral Movement animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An attacker hops from host-1 to host-2, host-3 and host-4, widening the blast radius with each hop." class="bad-anim bad-anim--lateral">
        <defs>
          <linearGradient id="hostGrad" x1="0" y1="0" x2="0" y2="1">
            <stop offset="0%" stop-color="#7f1d1d"/>
            <stop offset="100%" stop-color="#450a0a"/>
          </linearGradient>
        </defs>
        {/* four hosts */}
        <g stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" fill="url(#hostGrad)">
          <rect x="20"  y="60" width="50" height="40" rx="6"/>
          <rect x="110" y="60" width="50" height="40" rx="6"/>
          <rect x="200" y="60" width="50" height="40" rx="6"/>
          <rect x="290" y="60" width="50" height="40" rx="6"/>
        </g>
        {/* host labels */}
        <g fill="#fecaca" font-size="9" text-anchor="middle">
          <text x="45"  y="120">host-1</text>
          <text x="135" y="120">host-2</text>
          <text x="225" y="120">host-3</text>
          <text x="315" y="120">host-4</text>
        </g>
        {/* path */}
        <path id="lateral-path" d="M45 80 L135 80 L225 80 L315 80" stroke="#7f1d1d" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3,3" fill="none"/>
        {/* animated marker */}
        <circle r="7" fill="#ef4444" class="bad-anim__dot">
          <animateMotion dur="6s" repeatCount="indefinite" rotate="auto">
            <mpath href="#lateral-path"/>
          </animateMotion>
        </circle>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Lateral Movement</h3>
      <p>Adversary traversal across an environment after initial access. Each hop expands the blast radius and adds new entities for Subject to map. In real intrusions the movement is rarely linear and often piggybacks on legitimate authentication.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Privilege Escalation */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 280 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Privileges climb a ladder from user to admin to SYSTEM to domain admin." class="bad-anim bad-anim--escalate">
        {/* ladder rails */}
        <line x1="80"  y1="20" x2="80"  y2="180" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3" stroke-linecap="round"/>
        <line x1="200" y1="20" x2="200" y2="180" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3" stroke-linecap="round"/>
        {/* rungs with labels */}
        <g class="bad-anim__rungs">
          <g class="bad-anim__rung bad-anim__rung--1">
            <line x1="80" y1="155" x2="200" y2="155" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3"/>
            <text x="220" y="159" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">user</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__rung bad-anim__rung--2">
            <line x1="80" y1="115" x2="200" y2="115" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3"/>
            <text x="220" y="119" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">admin</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__rung bad-anim__rung--3">
            <line x1="80" y1="75" x2="200" y2="75" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3"/>
            <text x="220" y="79" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">SYSTEM</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__rung bad-anim__rung--4">
            <line x1="80" y1="35" x2="200" y2="35" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3"/>
            <text x="220" y="39" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">domain admin</text>
          </g>
        </g>
        {/* climbing marker */}
        <circle r="10" fill="#ef4444" filter="drop-shadow(0 0 6px #ef4444)" class="bad-anim__climber">
          <animate attributeName="cx" values="140;140;140;140" dur="6s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
          <animate attributeName="cy" values="170;130;90;50;50" keyTimes="0;0.25;0.5;0.75;1" dur="6s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </circle>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Privilege Escalation</h3>
      <p>The climb from standard user to admin to SYSTEM to domain admin. Local escalation (user to admin to SYSTEM) is noisy on the endpoint; the jump to domain or cloud admin often happens through stolen credentials and shows up only in identity logs (a new privileged role assignment, an IAM role chained to another). Watching both planes is what catches the climb in progress, and that is the difference between a contained incident and a full domain compromise.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Persistence */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Four persistence anchors light up in turn: a Run key, a daily scheduled task, an auto-start service and a WMI event subscription that fires at logon." class="bad-anim bad-anim--persist">
        {/* four persistence locations */}
        <g class="bad-anim__slot bad-anim__slot--1">
          <rect x="20" y="40" width="120" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="80" y="62" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">Run key</text>
          <text x="80" y="78" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">HKLM\...\Run</text>
        </g>
        <g class="bad-anim__slot bad-anim__slot--2">
          <rect x="180" y="40" width="120" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="240" y="62" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">Scheduled task</text>
          <text x="240" y="78" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">at 02:00 daily</text>
        </g>
        <g class="bad-anim__slot bad-anim__slot--3">
          <rect x="20" y="115" width="120" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="80" y="137" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">Service</text>
          <text x="80" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">auto-start</text>
        </g>
        <g class="bad-anim__slot bad-anim__slot--4">
          <rect x="180" y="115" width="120" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="240" y="137" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">WMI event sub</text>
          <text x="240" y="153" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">on logon</text>
        </g>
        {/* check marks appearing on each */}
        <g class="bad-anim__checks" fill="#22c55e" font-size="20" font-weight="800">
          <text x="125" y="65" class="bad-anim__check bad-anim__check--1">✓</text>
          <text x="285" y="65" class="bad-anim__check bad-anim__check--2">✓</text>
          <text x="125" y="140" class="bad-anim__check bad-anim__check--3">✓</text>
          <text x="285" y="140" class="bad-anim__check bad-anim__check--4">✓</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Persistence</h3>
      <p>Why removing the malware is rarely enough. Mature adversaries plant multiple anchors (registry Run keys, scheduled tasks, services, WMI event subscriptions) so reboots, password resets, and even partial cleanups leave at least one foothold intact. The same logic holds in the identity plane (a new OAuth app grant, an extra multi-factor authentication (MFA) method, a mailbox forwarding rule, a federated-domain change) and on Linux and macOS (cron or systemd units, <code>authorized_keys</code>, LaunchAgents). Defenders need to enumerate every place persistence might hide before they can call a host or an account clean.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: SQL Injection */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="The login input ' OR '1'='1' -- is concatenated into the SQL query, which becomes a tautology that returns every row of the users table." class="bad-anim bad-anim--sqli">
        <rect x="20" y="30" width="300" height="34" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="30" y="52" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">login.php?user=</text>
        <text x="155" y="52" font-size="11" fill="#ef4444" class="bad-anim__sqli-payload">' OR '1'='1' -- </text>
        <text x="30" y="85" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">↓ becomes SQL:</text>
        <rect x="20" y="95" width="300" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="30" y="115" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">SELECT * FROM users WHERE name='</text>
        <text x="30" y="132" font-size="10" fill="#ef4444">' OR '1'='1' -- ' AND pw='...'</text>
        <text x="170" y="170" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">→ tautology returns all rows</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>SQL Injection</h3>
      <p>Untrusted input concatenated straight into a SQL query. The classic payload <code>' OR '1'='1' -- </code> (the space after the dashes matters in MySQL) turns the query into a tautology and returns every row, and the rest of the original query becomes a comment: <code>SELECT * FROM users WHERE name='' OR '1'='1' -- ' AND pw='...'</code>. The same characters get parsed as syntax instead of data. The fix is parameterized queries (prepared statements), which keep input as data.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Zero Day */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A day counter stuck at zero days to prepare: the CVE is still pending while the exploit is already live." class="bad-anim bad-anim--zero">
        <circle cx="160" cy="100" r="60" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3"/>
        <circle cx="160" cy="100" r="60" fill="none" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="4" stroke-dasharray="377" class="bad-anim__zero-ring" pathLength="377"/>
        <text x="160" y="92" text-anchor="middle" font-size="22" font-weight="800" fill="#fecaca" class="bad-anim__zero-counter">0</text>
        <text x="160" y="115" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">days to prepare</text>
        <text x="160" y="180" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">CVE-pending · exploit live</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Zero Day</h3>
      <p>The counter sits at zero: defenders have had no days to prepare. The vendor has not shipped a fix and may not yet know the bug exists. Until a fix ships, exploitation outpaces mitigation. Once a patch drops, attackers diff it and mass exploitation of unpatched systems often follows within days (at that point the bug is an n-day). The most valuable zero-days are never publicly disclosed at all.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: UAC Bypass */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A UAC elevation prompt is skipped through an auto-elevating Windows binary such as fodhelper or sdclt." class="bad-anim bad-anim--uac">
        {/* shield */}
        <path d="M160 25 L210 50 L210 110 Q210 150 160 175 Q110 150 110 110 L110 50 Z" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2" class="bad-anim__shield"/>
        <text x="160" y="95" text-anchor="middle" font-size="20" font-weight="800" fill="#fecaca">UAC</text>
        <text x="160" y="115" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">elevate?</text>
        {/* crack */}
        <path d="M160 30 L155 80 L165 120 L150 170" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="2" fill="none" class="bad-anim__crack" stroke-dasharray="200" stroke-dashoffset="200"/>
        <text x="160" y="195" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">bypassed via auto-elevating binary (fodhelper, sdclt...)</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>UAC Bypass</h3>
      <p>Windows User Account Control should prompt before elevation. Bypass techniques abuse auto-elevating Windows binaries that read attacker-writable HKCU registry keys (<code>fodhelper.exe</code>'s ms-settings handler is the classic; <code>computerdefaults.exe</code> and <code>sdclt.exe</code> have similar variants, while the old <code>eventvwr.exe</code> mscfile hijack was fixed in Windows 10 1703). The consent prompt never fires. UAC is a defense-in-depth feature, not a security boundary, and Microsoft does not service bypasses as vulnerabilities. These bypasses only work for an account that is already a local administrator, running at medium integrity with UAC set below 'Always notify'; they lift the process to high integrity without the consent prompt. A standard user gets a credential prompt that these tricks do not skip. So a confirmed UAC bypass also tells you the compromised account holds local admin rights, which is itself a finding.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

</section>

{/* ============================================================
     2. INITIAL ACCESS TECHNIQUES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="access" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M13 4h3a2 2 0 0 1 2 2v14"/><path d="M2 20h3"/><path d="M13 20h9"/><path d="M10 12v.01"/><path d="M13 4.562v16.157a1 1 0 0 1-1.242.97L5 20V5.562a2 2 0 0 1 1.515-1.94l4-1A2 2 0 0 1 13 4.561Z"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Initial access techniques</h2>
      <p>Entry vectors attackers use to gain a first foothold.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/alert/validation/">Alert</a> · <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="callback-phishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Callback Phishing</h3><p>An email with no link or attachment (a fake invoice or subscription renewal) that tells the victim to call a phone number. The call center then talks them into installing remote-access software. Defeats link and attachment scanning entirely.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An email with an invoice or renewal and a phone number but no link, then a remote-access tool installed by the user within the hour. Search all mailboxes for the same sender or phone number.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566.004</a></article>
    <article id="credential-stuffing" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Credential Stuffing</h3><p>Automated injection of stolen username-password pairs into login forms, relying on credential reuse.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many accounts each tried once or twice from rotating IPs, a high failure rate on a login or API endpoint, and successes for accounts later reported as taken over.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1110/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1110.004</a></article>
    <article id="deepfake-impersonation" class="bad-term"><h3>Deepfake Impersonation</h3><p>Using AI-generated voice or video to pose as a real person (an executive, IT staff, a job candidate) on a call or video meeting. In early 2024 an employee of the engineering firm Arup in Hong Kong paid out about US$25 million after a video call on which every other participant was a deepfake. ATT&amp;CK v19.2 tracks the content creation as Generate Content: Audio-Visual Content (T1683.002).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A payment, reset or access request made on a call or video meeting that skips the normal approval path, often with urgency and secrecy. Recognizing a voice or face is not proof of identity: verify through a known, separate channel.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1683/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1683.002</a></article>
    <article id="dprk-it-workers" class="bad-term"><h3>DPRK Remote IT Workers</h3><p>North Korean operatives who get hired as remote IT contractors under stolen or fabricated identities (Microsoft: Jasper Sleet), then earn salaries for the regime, steal data, and sometimes extort the employer. A hiring and insider-risk scenario as much as an intrusion. See Lazarus Group.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A corporate laptop shipped to a forwarding address, remote-control tools or IP-KVM devices IT never deployed, sign-ins whose geolocation or network does not match where the laptop should be, and identity, payroll and HR records that disagree.</p></article>
    <article id="drive-by-download" class="bad-term"><h3>Drive-by Download</h3><p>Compromise triggered by visiting a malicious or compromised site. The classic form was a silent browser exploit served by an exploit kit. Today it is usually a fake browser update or fake CAPTCHA that persuades the user to download or paste-and-run something (SocGholish, ClickFix). Fully silent exploit chains still exist but are rare and mostly used in targeted watering-hole attacks.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A browser spawning a script host or installer, a fake-update file (JS or MSI) in Downloads, or a paste-and-run command right after a site visit.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1189/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1189</a></article>
    <article id="email-attachment-exploit" class="bad-term"><h3>Email Attachment Exploit</h3><p>Malware or exploit code embedded in an email attachment that runs when the user opens it, either through a document vulnerability (Follina, the Equation Editor bug) or by persuading the user to enable content or run a file. ATT&amp;CK files it under Spearphishing Attachment (T1566.001).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An Office, PDF or archive process spawning a shell, script host or msdt shortly after a mail client opened the attachment. Find the message and check who else received it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566.001</a></article>
    <article id="exploit-public-facing-application" class="bad-term"><h3>Exploit Public-Facing Application</h3><p>Targets vulnerabilities in externally exposed applications (web servers, APIs, file-transfer platforms, VPN and other edge appliances) to gain unauthorized access or remote code execution (ATT&amp;CK T1190). Edge devices and file-transfer platforms have been its most exploited targets in recent years.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A web or application server process spawning a shell, new files in web directories, and odd requests or error spikes in WAF and application logs just before. Check the exposed version against the CISA KEV catalog.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1190/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1190</a></article>
    <article id="rdp-pivoting" class="bad-term"><h3>Exposed Remote Services (RDP, VPN, Citrix)</h3><p>Logging in through internet-facing remote services (RDP, VPN, Citrix, other edge appliances) with stolen, bought or guessed credentials, or by exploiting the appliance itself. A leading initial-access vector that often leaves no endpoint telemetry, so VPN and appliance logs are the evidence. Using RDP from inside the network to move between hosts is Lateral Movement (T1021.001).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Successful VPN or remote-gateway sign-ins from new countries, hosting providers or residential proxies, especially without MFA, followed by internal RDP or SMB from the VPN address pool.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1133/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1133</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1021.001</a></article>
    <article id="help-desk-social-engineering" class="bad-term"><h3>Help-Desk Social Engineering</h3><p>Calling the IT help desk while posing as an employee to get a password reset, MFA reset or new MFA device registered. Scattered Spider's signature entry path.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An MFA method change or reset shortly followed by a sign-in from a new device or location.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-320a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-320A</a></article>
    <article id="malvertising" class="bad-term"><h3>Malvertising</h3><p>Paid ads, especially search ads, that impersonate legitimate software downloads and lead to trojanized installers (often loaders or infostealers). Sibling technique: SEO poisoning (T1608.006), which games organic search results instead of buying ads.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A search-ad click to a look-alike download site, then an installer for a common tool from that domain, signed by an unfamiliar publisher or not signed at all.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1583/008/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1583.008</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1608/006/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1608.006</a></article>
    <article id="phishing" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Phishing</h3><p>Deceptive messages (email, SMS, QR codes, chat apps, phone) that impersonate a trusted sender to steal credentials or session tokens or to get malware run. Modern kits proxy the real login page (adversary-in-the-middle) to capture the MFA-authenticated session cookie, so MFA alone does not stop them. Some lures skip credentials and get the user to paste a command into the Run dialog or a terminal. See AiTM Phishing, Device Code Phishing, Illicit Consent Grant and ClickFix.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A reported or detected message, then what came after it: a credential entered (proxy logs, a sign-in from a new IP), a file opened, a command pasted and run, or a call made. Any user who reached the credential step is credential-exposed until the password is reset, sessions are revoked and MFA methods are reviewed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566</a></article>
    <article id="quishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Quishing (QR-Code Phishing)</h3><p>Phishing that hides the link in a QR code, often in an email or a printed notice, so the victim opens it on a phone outside corporate web filtering and email link scanning.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Emails carrying a QR-code image with little text, often about MFA or document signing, followed by sign-ins from mobile devices or IPs the user never used.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566</a></article>
    <article id="smishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Smishing (SMS Phishing)</h3><p>Phishing by text message: fake delivery notices, toll bills, or "IT support" texts that lead to a credential-harvesting page or a malicious app.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> User reports of texts about deliveries, tolls or IT support, then sign-ins or MFA registrations from mobile carriers or new devices. SMS bypasses your mail gateway, so user reports are the main source.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1660/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1660 (Mobile)</a></article>
    <article id="social-engineering" class="bad-term"><h3>Social Engineering</h3><p>Manipulates people into performing actions or revealing information by exploiting trust, fear, urgency, or authority. Phishing, vishing and help-desk impersonation are all forms of it.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A request that uses urgency, authority or secrecy to skip a normal check: a password or MFA reset, a payment change, a remote session. Verify through a known channel, not the one the request came from.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1684/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1684</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/news/avoiding-social-engineering-and-phishing-attacks" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA</a></article>
    <article id="spear-phishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Spear Phishing</h3><p>Targeted phishing customized for a specific individual or organization to increase success rate.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A message that cites real projects, colleagues or vendors and comes from a look-alike or newly registered domain. Search for other recipients: targeted campaigns rarely hit one person.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566</a></article>
    <article id="supply-chain-compromise" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Supply Chain Compromise</h3><p>Targets third-party software or service providers to insert malicious code or access downstream victims: a vendor's build or update process (SolarWinds, 3CX), an open-source package, or a managed service provider's access. Trusted software or access then delivers the attack.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A trusted, signed application or vendor account acting unlike its baseline: new child processes, new outbound domains, or actions outside its normal scope. Vendor advisories and package-registry alerts often arrive first.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1195</a></article>
    <article id="typosquatting" class="bad-term"><h3>Typosquatting</h3><p>Registers look-alike domains (or package names in npm and PyPI) to trick users into visiting malicious sites or installing malicious code, often to harvest credentials or deliver malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> DNS lookups or links to newly registered domains one character off from yours or your vendors', and packages with names close to popular ones installed in builds.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1583/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1583.001</a></article>
    <article id="usb-removable-media-infection" class="bad-term"><h3>USB / Removable Media Infection</h3><p>Malware spread on USB drives, usually as a disguised shortcut (LNK) or executable the user is tricked into opening. Autorun from removable media is disabled by default on modern Windows, but some organizations re-enable it via legacy Group Policy. Devices that pose as keyboards (BadUSB, Rubber Ducky) execute without user action and fall under Hardware Additions (T1200).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Removable-media events (6416, Partition Diagnostic 1006), LNK or EXE files executed from a removable drive, and a new keyboard device that types at machine speed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1091/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1091</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1200/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1200</a></article>
    <article id="valid-accounts" class="bad-term"><h3>Valid Accounts</h3><p>Logging in with real credentials that were stolen (often from infostealer logs), bought from an access broker, or left at defaults. Nothing is exploited, so the only signal is behavior: new device, new location, impossible travel, unusual hours or access. The Snowflake customer-account thefts were this, at scale.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A correct password used from a new device, location or ASN, at unusual hours, then access the user does not normally make. Check whether the credential appears in infostealer logs.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1078</a></article>
    <article id="vishing-voice-phishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Vishing (Voice Phishing)</h3><p>Phone calls or voicemails impersonating authority figures or institutions to extract credentials or prompt unsafe actions.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A user report of a call from 'IT' or 'the bank', then a password reset, MFA change or remote-access install within minutes. Line up the call time with identity and endpoint logs.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566.004</a></article>
    <article id="watering-hole-attack" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Watering Hole Attack</h3><p>Compromises a website frequently visited by a target group, embedding malware to infect visitors.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Several users from the same team or sector visiting the same site, then the same suspicious script or download on their hosts. Unfamiliar JavaScript on a known-good site is the tell.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1189/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1189</a></article>
    <article id="whaling" class="bad-term"><h3>Whaling</h3><p>Spear phishing targeting high-profile individuals like executives, often for financial fraud (business email compromise) or data theft.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Messages to executives or their assistants about payments, legal matters or board business from look-alike domains or compromised partner accounts. Verify payment-change requests out of band.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1566</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Phishing hook animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A lure among the fish leads from a click to stolen credentials to compromise." class="bad-anim bad-anim--phish">
        {/* ground line */}
        <line x1="0" y1="160" x2="320" y2="160" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        {/* email envelope as bait */}
        <g class="bad-anim__bait">
          <rect x="140" y="60" width="40" height="28" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <path d="M140 60 L160 78 L180 60" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        </g>
        {/* fishing line */}
        <line x1="160" y1="20" x2="160" y2="60" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1" class="bad-anim__line"/>
        {/* victims */}
        <g class="bad-anim__victims" font-size="20">
          <text x="40" y="160" text-anchor="middle">🐟</text>
          <text x="100" y="155" text-anchor="middle">🐠</text>
          <text x="220" y="158" text-anchor="middle">🐡</text>
          <text x="280" y="155" text-anchor="middle">🐟</text>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="190" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">click → credentials → compromise</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Phishing</h3>
      <p>Deceptive messages that lure users into clicking, opening, or entering credentials. The bait is the message (email, SMS, a QR code, a chat ping, a phone call); the line is the trust the sender impersonates; the catch is initial access. Modern kits proxy the real login page (adversary-in-the-middle), so the catch is often a live session cookie rather than a password, and MFA alone does not stop them.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Watering Hole */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A payload is implanted on an industry news site, and every target who visits gets a drive-by infection." class="bad-anim bad-anim--water">
        <rect x="120" y="60" width="100" height="60" rx="10" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        <text x="170" y="83" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">industry-news.example</text>
        <text x="170" y="100" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">+ implanted payload</text>
        <g class="bad-anim__visitors" font-size="18">
          <text x="20"  y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--1">👤</text>
          <text x="60"  y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--2">👤</text>
          <text x="100" y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--3">👤</text>
          <text x="240" y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--4">👤</text>
          <text x="280" y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--5">👤</text>
          <text x="320" y="155" class="bad-water-visitor bad-water-visitor--6">👤</text>
        </g>
        <text x="170" y="40" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">site every target reads</text>
        <text x="170" y="185" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">arrive · drink · drive-by infection</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Watering Hole Attack</h3>
      <p>Do not go to the targets. Wait at the website they all visit (an industry news source, a community forum, a vendor portal), implant a payload there, and infect everyone who shows up. The name began as a metaphor; the technique is now mature espionage tradecraft, and criminal crews use it too, usually via fake browser-update prompts injected into the compromised site.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Supply Chain Compromise */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A compromised vendor ships a trojanized update to customers A, B and C: one breach upstream, many victims downstream." class="bad-anim bad-anim--supply">
        <rect x="20" y="40" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ef4444" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="70" y="58" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" font-weight="700" fill="#fff">vendor</text>
        <text x="70" y="72" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fff">compromised</text>
        <g class="bad-anim__updates">
          <g class="bad-supply-update bad-supply-update--1"><rect x="0" y="-10" width="50" height="20" rx="3" fill="#facc15" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1"/><text x="25" y="4" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#1a0606" font-weight="700">update</text><animateMotion dur="6s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M120 60 L240 60"/></g>
          <g class="bad-supply-update bad-supply-update--2"><rect x="0" y="-10" width="50" height="20" rx="3" fill="#facc15" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1"/><text x="25" y="4" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#1a0606" font-weight="700">update</text><animateMotion dur="6s" begin="2s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M120 100 L240 100"/></g>
          <g class="bad-supply-update bad-supply-update--3"><rect x="0" y="-10" width="50" height="20" rx="3" fill="#facc15" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1"/><text x="25" y="4" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#1a0606" font-weight="700">update</text><animateMotion dur="6s" begin="4s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M120 140 L240 140"/></g>
        </g>
        <g>
          <rect x="240" y="40"  width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="280" y="64" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">customer A</text>
          <rect x="240" y="85"  width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="280" y="109" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">customer B</text>
          <rect x="240" y="130" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="280" y="154" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">customer C</text>
        </g>
        <text x="170" y="190" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">one breach upstream · thousands compromised downstream</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Supply Chain Compromise</h3>
      <p>The vendor ships the malware for you. A trusted update, package or integration flows through normal channels to thousands of customers. Espionage operators then often activate only a chosen few, so the distribution radius and the exploitation radius differ. SolarWinds, ShadowHammer, 3CX and M.E.Doc did it through signed vendor updates. XZ Utils did it through a hijacked open-source maintainer role, tj-actions through a CI dependency, and the 2025 Shai-Hulud worm through hundreds of npm packages. Different names, same shape.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Credential Stuffing */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Leaked username and password pairs from another site are replayed against a target login page; a tiny hit rate is enough for account takeover at scale." class="bad-anim bad-anim--stuff">
        <rect x="20" y="30" width="120" height="140" rx="8" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="80" y="50" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">leaked DB (sourced)</text>
        <g font-size="10" fill="#fecaca" class="bad-anim__pwlist2">
          <text x="35" y="75">alice@x:hunter2</text>
          <text x="35" y="93">bob@y:dragons</text>
          <text x="35" y="111">eve@z:starwars1</text>
          <text x="35" y="129">jim@w:p@ssw0rd</text>
          <text x="35" y="147">kim@v:summer22</text>
          <text x="35" y="165">tia@u:Welcome!</text>
        </g>
        <path d="M140 100 L220 100" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3" fill="none"/>
        <g>
          <rect x="220" y="70" width="100" height="60" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="270" y="93" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">target site</text>
          <text x="270" y="110" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">checks each pair</text>
        </g>
        <text x="270" y="155" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">tiny hit rate</text>
        <text x="270" y="170" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">enough for ATO at scale</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Credential Stuffing</h3>
      <p>The attacker is not guessing. They have leaked credentials from somewhere else. They replay them at scale against your login page. Password reuse across sites is what makes the economics work: reuse is common enough that even a tiny per-attempt success rate pays off across millions of attempts. MFA stops most of it, ideally phishing-resistant MFA such as passkeys. Per-IP rate limits alone do not, because attackers spread attempts across residential proxies. Bot detection and screening passwords against breach corpora at signup and reset close more of the gap.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     3. EXECUTION AND INJECTION
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="exec" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="m18 2 4 4"/><path d="m17 7 3-3"/><path d="M19 9 8.7 19.3c-1 1-2.5 1-3.4 0l-.6-.6c-1-1-1-2.5 0-3.4L15 5"/><path d="m9 11 4 4"/><path d="m5 19-3 3"/><path d="m14 4 6 6"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Execution and injection</h2>
      <p>Code execution, injection, and process manipulation techniques.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/alert/parsing/">Alert</a> · <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="clickfix" class="bad-term"><h3>ClickFix (fake CAPTCHA / paste-and-run)</h3><p>A lure that tells the user to press Win+R (or open a terminal) and paste an attacker command, usually mshta or PowerShell, to "verify they are human" or "fix" an error; the user executes it themselves.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> RunMRU registry entries and explorer.exe spawning powershell or mshta.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1204.004</a></article>
    <article id="code-injection" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Code Injection</h3><p>Untrusted input that an application's interpreter executes as code (eval, template engines, dynamic SQL or shell construction). In malware-analysis usage the same phrase often means process injection; see Process Injection. When it happens in an internet-facing application, ATT&amp;CK maps the intrusion to T1190 Exploit Public-Facing Application.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Interpreter syntax in application inputs (template expressions, eval payloads, shell metacharacters) in WAF or application logs, then the application process spawning a shell or making new outbound connections.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/94.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CWE-94</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://community.owasp.org/attacks/Code_Injection" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: OWASP</a></article>
    <article id="command-and-scripting-interpreter" class="bad-term"><h3>Command and Scripting Interpreter</h3><p>Using legitimate command-line shells or scripting environments to execute commands: PowerShell and cmd.exe on Windows, bash and zsh on Linux and macOS, Python, JavaScript, and AppleScript.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> PowerShell script-block logs (4104) with encoded or downloaded content, cmd or bash launched by an unexpected parent (Office, a browser, a web server), and interpreters running from user download paths.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1059</a></article>
    <article id="dll-injection" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>DLL Injection</h3><p>Injecting a Dynamic Link Library into a target process to execute malicious code within that process's context.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process opening another with write and create-thread rights, a remote thread starting at LoadLibrary (Sysmon event 8), and a DLL from a user-writable path loaded into a system process (Sysmon event 7).</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055.001</a></article>
    <article id="living-off-the-land" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Living Off The Land</h3><p>Abusing legitimate tools already on the host (on Windows: PowerShell, certutil, rundll32, mshta, bitsadmin; on Linux/macOS: curl, python, osascript). See LOLBAS, GTFOBins and LOOBins.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Built-in tools with unusual arguments or parents: certutil with -urlcache, rundll32 loading a DLL from AppData, mshta with a URL, bitsadmin downloads. The binary is trusted; the command line is the evidence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://lolbas-project.github.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: LOLBAS project</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://gtfobins.github.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: GTFOBins</a></article>
    <article id="macro-based-execution" class="bad-term"><h3>Macro-based Execution</h3><p>Malicious VBA (or legacy Excel 4.0/XLM) macros in Office files that run when the user enables content, or automatically from a Trusted Location. Since 2022, Office on Windows blocks macros by default in files carrying Mark of the Web (downloads and email attachments), so attackers moved to container files (ISO, LNK), HTML smuggling (T1027.006) and paste-and-run lures (T1204.004). When a macro alert fires today, ask how the file got past the block: user Unblock, missing MOTW, a Trusted Location, or a policy exception.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> WINWORD.EXE or EXCEL.EXE spawning cmd, powershell, wscript, mshta or rundll32, and Office trust records showing content enabled on a downloaded file.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1059.005</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1204.002</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-365-apps/security/internet-macros-blocked" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft Learn</a></article>
