    <article id="process-doppelganging" class="bad-term"><h3>Process Doppelgänging</h3><p>Overwriting a legitimate executable inside a Transactional NTFS (TxF) transaction, creating a process from the modified image, then rolling the transaction back, so the file on disk never changes.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Transactional NTFS APIs used by a process that is not an installer, and a process whose image on disk does not match its memory. Rare; most EDRs flag it as process tampering.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/013/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055.013</a></article>
    <article id="process-hollowing" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Process Hollowing</h3><p>A legitimate process is created suspended, its memory replaced with a malicious executable image, then resumed under the guise of a trusted process.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A trusted process (svchost, explorer, RegAsm) created suspended by an unusual parent, with memory that does not match its image on disk, then making network connections it normally would not.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/012/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055.012</a></article>
    <article id="cross-process-injection" class="bad-term"><h3>Process Injection</h3><p>Running code inside another live process's memory (DLL injection, process hollowing, thread hijacking, and similar sub-techniques) so it inherits that process's identity and trust. Sometimes loosely called memory injection, a phrase that can also mean fileless execution (see Fileless Malware).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process writing to another process's memory and starting execution there (Sysmon Event 8, CreateRemoteThread), or executable memory not backed by a file.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055</a><span id="memory-injection" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="reflective-dll-injection" class="bad-term"><h3>Reflective DLL Injection</h3><p>A DLL that maps itself into memory with its own loader instead of the Windows loader, so it never appears as a file on disk or in the normal loaded-module list. EDR catches it as executable memory not backed by any file (T1620; remote-process variants fall under T1055.001).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Memory scans or EDR alerts for unbacked executable memory containing a PE header, often in a process that just made a network connection. Capture memory before any reboot: the evidence lives nowhere else.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1620/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1620</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055.001</a></article>
    <article id="side-loading" class="bad-term"><h3>Side-loading</h3><p>Planting a malicious DLL next to a legitimate (often signed) program that loads a DLL of that name from its own folder, so trusted code runs the payload. Search-order hijacking is the sibling variant under the same ATT&amp;CK ID, T1574.001 "DLL" (see <a href="/docs/glossaries/clear/#D">DLL Search Order Hijacking</a>).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A signed vendor EXE running from a user-writable path such as AppData or ProgramData alongside an unsigned DLL.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1574/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1574.001</a></article>
    <article id="thread-injection" class="bad-term"><h3>Thread Execution Hijacking</h3><p>Suspending an existing thread in another process, writing code into that process, pointing the thread's context (instruction pointer) at it with SetThreadContext, and resuming it. Unlike CreateRemoteThread-style injection, no new thread is created.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> EDR alerts on SuspendThread, SetThreadContext and ResumeThread against another process, with the thread's instruction pointer in unbacked memory. No new thread appears, so CreateRemoteThread-based rules miss it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1055/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1055.003</a></article>
    <article id="wmi-abuse" class="bad-term"><h3>WMI Abuse</h3><p>Using Windows Management Instrumentation to run commands and query system data on the local host or on remote hosts. Access is through PowerShell (Get-CimInstance, Invoke-CimMethod), COM/DCOM, or the deprecated wmic.exe (see the WMIC entry in the CLEAR glossary). WMI is native and looks like admin activity, so it blends in with legitimate management traffic. WMI event subscriptions used for persistence are a separate technique, T1546.003.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> WmiPrvSE.exe spawning cmd or powershell, a network logon followed by a WmiPrvSE child process on the target, and wmic process call create in command lines.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1047/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1047</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Process Hollowing animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 180" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="notepad.exe starts suspended, its memory is replaced with payload.exe, and it resumes still looking like notepad." class="bad-anim bad-anim--hollow">
        {/* legitimate shell */}
        <rect x="30" y="50" width="120" height="80" rx="8" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="5,3"/>
        <text x="90" y="38" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">notepad.exe (suspended)</text>
        {/* innards swapped */}
        <g class="bad-anim__inner">
          <rect x="44" y="64" width="92" height="52" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d"/>
          <text x="90" y="95" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">payload.exe</text>
        </g>
        {/* arrows */}
        <g stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5" fill="none" marker-end="url(#arr)">
          <defs>
            <marker id="arr" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" refX="5" refY="3" orient="auto">
              <path d="M0,0 L6,3 L0,6 z" fill="#ef4444"/>
            </marker>
          </defs>
          <path d="M180 80 L235 80"/>
        </g>
        {/* resumed process */}
        <rect x="240" y="50" width="60" height="80" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        <text x="270" y="38" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">resumed</text>
        <text x="270" y="95" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">looks like</text>
        <text x="270" y="108" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">notepad</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Process Hollowing</h3>
      <p>A legitimate process is launched in a suspended state, its memory is replaced with malicious code, and execution resumes. To observers (and many EDR tools) the process still looks like the trusted binary: same name, same path, same parent. The shell stays the same while the contents are swapped. EDR catches it from the API sequence: a suspended launch, NtUnmapViewOfSection, cross-process writes, then SetThreadContext/ResumeThread.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: DLL Injection */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="evil.dll is loaded into explorer.exe next to its normal libraries and runs with the target's privileges and trust." class="bad-anim bad-anim--dll">
        {/* target process */}
        <rect x="120" y="50" width="180" height="100" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        <text x="210" y="38" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">target process (explorer.exe)</text>
        {/* process internals */}
        <g font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="135" y="75">kernel32.dll</text>
          <text x="135" y="95">user32.dll</text>
          <text x="135" y="115">ole32.dll</text>
          {/* injected DLL appears here */}
          <text x="135" y="135" fill="#ef4444" class="bad-anim__injected" font-weight="700">+ evil.dll</text>
        </g>
        {/* DLL flying in from left */}
        <g class="bad-anim__dllbox">
          <rect x="20" y="105" width="60" height="30" rx="4" fill="#ef4444" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="50" y="124" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fff" font-weight="700">evil.dll</text>
        </g>
        {/* arrow */}
        <path d="M85 120 L115 120" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5" fill="none" marker-end="url(#dll-arr)"/>
        <defs>
          <marker id="dll-arr" markerWidth="6" markerHeight="6" refX="5" refY="3" orient="auto">
            <path d="M0,0 L6,3 L0,6 z" fill="#ef4444"/>
          </marker>
        </defs>
        <text x="210" y="175" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">runs with target's privileges &amp; trust</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>DLL Injection</h3>
      <p>A malicious DLL is loaded into another process's address space. The injected code now runs with the target's privileges and trust, inherits its network handles, and survives in the loaded-modules list of a legitimate binary. EDR catches the injection moment; static tools see only a benign process.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Living Off The Land */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Signed Windows binaries used maliciously: powershell.exe -enc, certutil.exe -urlcache, rundll32.exe evil.dll,Start, wmic.exe process call create, mshta.exe with a URL, and bitsadmin.exe /transfer." class="bad-anim bad-anim--lotl">
        <text x="160" y="28" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">trusted binaries used maliciously</text>
        <g font-size="11">
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--1">
            <rect x="20" y="45" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="80" y="60" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">powershell.exe</text>
            <text x="80" y="73" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ -enc base64...</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--2">
            <rect x="180" y="45" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="240" y="60" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">certutil.exe</text>
            <text x="240" y="73" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ -urlcache evil.bin</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--3">
            <rect x="20" y="90" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="80" y="105" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">rundll32.exe</text>
            <text x="80" y="118" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ evil.dll,Start</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--4">
            <rect x="180" y="90" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="240" y="105" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">wmic.exe</text>
            <text x="240" y="118" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ process call create</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--5">
            <rect x="20" y="135" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="80" y="150" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">mshta.exe</text>
            <text x="80" y="163" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ http://evil/...</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-lotl bad-lotl--6">
            <rect x="180" y="135" width="120" height="34" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
            <text x="240" y="150" text-anchor="middle" fill="#fecaca">bitsadmin.exe</text>
            <text x="240" y="163" text-anchor="middle" fill="#ef4444" font-size="8">→ /transfer evil.dat</text>
          </g>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="185" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">LOLBins: signed, trusted, present by default on Windows</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Living Off The Land</h3>
      <p>Why install attacker tooling when Windows already ships with everything you need? PowerShell, certutil, rundll32, mshta, bitsadmin: every one Microsoft-signed and present by default, so there is no attacker binary to hash or block by reputation. The payload may still land on disk (certutil and bitsadmin downloads do), but the tool doing the work is trusted. Some, such as mshta, are on Microsoft's own recommended block list, which is why hardened environments restrict them. WMIC is the exception: Microsoft removed it from all supported Windows 11 versions in the August-September 2026 updates (no Feature on Demand reinstall), so wmic.exe running on a current Windows 11 host is itself anomalous. It still ships on older Windows Server releases. Attackers now reach WMI through PowerShell CIM cmdlets (Invoke-CimMethod, Get-CimInstance) instead.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Code Injection */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A web app passes form input to eval, and a crafted name makes the interpreter run an operating-system command." class="bad-anim bad-anim--codeinj">
        {/* legit code */}
        <rect x="20" y="40" width="280" height="148" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="160" y="28" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">vulnerable web app</text>
        <g font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="35" y="65">name = request.form['name']</text>
          <text x="35" y="85">return eval("greet_" + name + "()")</text>
          <text x="35" y="102">↑</text>
        </g>
        <g class="bad-anim__inject" font-size="10" fill="#ef4444">
          <text x="35" y="122">name = "admin() if 0 else</text>
          <text x="35" y="137">  __import__('os').system('id') #"</text>
          <text x="35" y="158" font-size="9"># comments out the trailing ()</text>
          <text x="35" y="176" font-size="9">↑ injected: the interpreter runs attacker code</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Code Injection</h3>
      <p>Input that becomes code. Whether it is SQL, JavaScript, shell, or Python <code>eval</code>, the pattern is the same: untrusted text concatenates with logic and the interpreter cannot tell where the data stops and the program begins. Benign input and malicious input travel the same path; the difference shows up only at runtime, in what the interpreter actually does with it. Here the payload <code>admin() if 0 else __import__('os').system('id') #</code> turns the evaluated string into a valid expression that runs a shell command, and the trailing <code>#</code> comments out the <code>()</code> the app appends. The fix is to never pass input to an interpreter: look the function up in an allowlist instead.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     4. CREDENTIAL ACCESS AND ABUSE
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="creds" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M2.586 17.414A2 2 0 0 0 2 18.828V21a1 1 0 0 0 1 1h3a1 1 0 0 0 1-1v-1a1 1 0 0 1 1-1h1a1 1 0 0 0 1-1v-1a1 1 0 0 1 1-1h.172a2 2 0 0 0 1.414-.586l.814-.814a6.5 6.5 0 1 0-4-4z"/><circle cx="16.5" cy="7.5" r=".5" fill="currentColor"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Credential access and abuse</h2>
      <p>Techniques used to obtain, abuse, or manipulate identity data, on premises and in the cloud identity provider.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/subject/">Subject</a> · <a href="/docs/scope/">Scope</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="account-enumeration" class="bad-term"><h3>Account Enumeration</h3><p>Discovering valid usernames, either externally by probing authentication responses (Reconnaissance, T1589) or internally by listing domain/cloud accounts after access (Discovery, T1087).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> External: many sign-in attempts returning 'user does not exist' (Entra error 50034, Windows 4625 with sub-status 0xC0000064) across many names. Internal: LDAP queries or net user /domain listings from a workstation.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1589/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1589</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1087/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1087</a></article>
    <article id="account-takeover" class="bad-term"><h3>Account Takeover</h3><p>Adversary gains unauthorized control of a user's account using stolen credentials or session tokens to impersonate the user.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A sign-in from a new device, location or ASN, then changes the real user would not make: a new MFA method, a new inbox rule, a changed recovery address, or edited payment details. The changes, not the sign-in, confirm it.</p></article>
    <article id="ad-cs-abuse" class="bad-term"><h3>AD CS Abuse</h3><p>Abusing misconfigured Active Directory Certificate Services templates or enrollment endpoints (the ESC1 to ESC8 family) to obtain a certificate that authenticates as another user, often a domain admin. Certificates outlive password resets, so revoke them during remediation.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Certificate requests (4886) and issuances (4887) where the requester and the subject differ, especially for privileged accounts, and certificate-based Kerberos logons (4768 with certificate fields) from unexpected hosts.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1649/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1649</a></article>
    <article id="aitm-phishing" class="bad-term"><h3>AiTM Phishing</h3><p>Adversary-in-the-middle phishing: a kit proxies the real login page, so the victim completes their own password and MFA prompts while the proxy captures the resulting session cookie.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An interactive, MFA-satisfied sign-in from a hosting-provider IP right after a link click, followed by the same session used from a new device.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1557/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1557</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1539</a></article>
    <article id="as-rep-roasting" class="bad-term"><h3>AS-REP Roasting</h3><p>Requesting Kerberos authentication data for accounts that have pre-authentication disabled, then cracking it offline to recover the password.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Event 4768 with pre-authentication type 0 for accounts that should not have it disabled.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1558/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1558.004</a></article>
    <article id="brute-force" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Brute Force</h3><p>Trying many passwords against one account until one works; defeated by lockout and rate limits.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many failures for one account from one or a few sources (4625, Entra error 50126), lockouts (4740), and any success that follows them. With no success and the account locked, it is a contained attempt.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1110/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1110.001</a></article>
    <article id="compromised-credentials" class="bad-term"><h3>Compromised Credentials</h3><p>Usernames and passwords exposed, stolen, or leaked, enabling attackers to gain unauthorized access to systems or services.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A credential that appears in infostealer logs, a breach corpus or a paste, or is used from infrastructure the user never used. A reset is not the end: revoke sessions and check what the credential already did.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1078/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1078</a></article>
    <article id="credential-dumping" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Credential Dumping</h3><p>Extracting credentials (hashes, tokens, tickets) from a compromised system's memory or files.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A non-system process opening lsass.exe with PROCESS_VM_READ (Sysmon Event 10); a comsvcs.dll MiniDump command line; ntds.dit copied via vssadmin or ntdsutil.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1003</a></article>
    <article id="credential-theft" class="bad-term"><h3>Credential Theft</h3><p>Stealing login information (passwords, hashes, tokens, session cookies) through phishing, infostealers, keyloggers, memory dumping, or system exploitation. An umbrella term: Credential Dumping, Token Theft and AiTM Phishing on this page describe the specific methods.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Look for the use that follows the theft: a new sign-in, device or token shortly after the phishing click, malware run or memory read. The theft itself is often visible only on the endpoint where it happened.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0006/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0006</a></article>
    <article id="dcsync" class="bad-term"><h3>DCSync</h3><p>Impersonating a domain controller and asking a real one to replicate password data, which returns the hash of any account, krbtgt included. Requires replication rights, so it follows a domain-admin-level compromise.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Directory replication requests (4662 with the replication GUIDs) from a host that is not a domain controller.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/006/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1003.006</a></article>
    <article id="device-code-phishing" class="bad-term"><h3>Device Code Phishing</h3><p>Tricking a user into entering an attacker-generated device code on the real Microsoft or other IdP sign-in page, which hands the attacker tokens for the user's account. No fake site and no password capture are involved. Used at scale by Storm-2372 from 2024. Blocking the device code flow where it is not needed is the main control.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Entra sign-in logs showing the device code authentication protocol for a user who does not use device-code apps, often right after a Teams or chat invite, then token use from a different IP.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1528</a></article>
    <article id="device-registration" class="bad-term"><h3>Device Registration</h3><p>Registering an attacker-controlled device to a compromised account so access survives a password change: as a new MFA method (an authenticator app or phone number), or as a registered or joined device in the identity provider or MDM, which can satisfy device-based Conditional Access and, in Microsoft Entra ID, obtain a Primary Refresh Token. Storm-2372 and Scattered Spider have both used it. An ATT&amp;CK Persistence technique.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Entra ID audit events such as "Register device", "Add registered owner to device" or "User registered security info" soon after a risky sign-in, a help-desk reset or a device code sign-in, from an unfamiliar location, or with a device name outside your naming scheme. A password reset does not remove it: list the account's devices and MFA methods and remove the ones nobody can account for. See <a href="/docs/reference/identity-sweep/">Identity-persistence sweep</a>.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1098/005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1098.005</a></article>
    <article id="diamond-and-sapphire-tickets" class="bad-term"><h3>Diamond and Sapphire Tickets</h3><p>Stealthier relatives of the golden ticket that start from a real TGT. A diamond ticket decrypts a legitimately issued TGT with the krbtgt key and edits its privilege data; a sapphire ticket goes further and embeds a real privileged user's privilege data obtained through Kerberos delegation (S4U2self). Because a genuine ticket request happened, the usual 'service ticket with no TGT' check misses them.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Harder to spot than a golden ticket because a matching 4768 exists. Look for privilege data that does not match the account (groups it does not hold), encryption types that do not fit the account, and how the attacker got the krbtgt key: any DCSync or NTDS.dit theft means krbtgt is compromised.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/next-gen-kerberos-attacks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Unit 42 (2022)</a></article>
    <article id="golden-saml" class="bad-term"><h3>Golden SAML</h3><p>Forging SAML tokens with a stolen federation token-signing key, such as an AD FS token-signing certificate. The attacker can then sign in to any service that trusts the key, as any user and with any claims, without a password or MFA, and resetting user passwords does not stop it. CyberArk named the technique in 2017; APT29 used it in the SolarWinds campaign.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Sign-ins at a federated service with no matching token issuance at the identity provider (for AD FS, no audit event 1200 for that user and time), an export of the token-signing certificate or a read of the AD FS key container in Active Directory, and federation-trust changes on a domain in the cloud audit log. Rotating the signing key ends it, once the attacker's access to the identity provider is removed. See <a href="/docs/reference/identity-sweep/">Identity-persistence sweep</a>.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1606/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1606.002</a></article>
    <article id="golden-ticket-attack" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Golden Ticket Attack</h3><p>Forging Kerberos TGTs with the krbtgt key to grant unrestricted domain access by impersonating any user.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Service-ticket requests (4769) with no matching TGT request (4768) for that account, or tickets with implausibly long lifetimes.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1558/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1558.001</a></article>
    <article id="illicit-consent-grant" class="bad-term"><h3>Illicit Consent Grant</h3><p>Phishing a user (or admin) into granting a malicious OAuth app permissions to their mailbox or files. The app's token keeps working after password resets and MFA changes, so revoking the grant is part of containment.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Entra audit events 'Consent to application' for an unfamiliar app requesting Mail.Read, Files.ReadWrite.All or offline_access, then Graph API activity from that app. Export the audit and sign-in records before the grant is revoked.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1528</a></article>
    <article id="kerberoasting" class="bad-term"><h3>Kerberoasting</h3><p>Requesting Kerberos service tickets for accounts with SPNs and cracking them offline to recover the service account's password, which then enables privilege escalation, persistence or lateral movement. Since Microsoft's CVE-2026-20833 hardening (enforced April 2026, rollback removed July 2026), domain controllers issue AES service tickets by default to accounts with no explicit msDS-SupportedEncryptionTypes, and AES tickets are far slower to crack. In event 4769, a Ticket Encryption Type of 0x17 (RC4-HMAC) is now a strong signal, and service accounts still explicitly configured for RC4 are the prime targets.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> One account requesting service tickets (4769) for many SPNs in a short burst, especially with Ticket Encryption Type 0x17 (RC4). A honey SPN account that no one should ever request makes the call near-certain.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1558/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1558.003</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://support.microsoft.com/en-us/topic/how-to-manage-kerberos-kdc-usage-of-rc4-for-service-account-ticket-issuance-changes-related-to-cve-2026-20833-1ebcda33-720a-4da8-93c1-b0496e1910dc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft KB (2026)</a></article>
    <article id="mfa-fatigue" class="bad-term"><h3>MFA Fatigue</h3><p>Flooding a user with push-notification approval requests, sometimes paired with a call pretending to be IT, until they accept one. Also called push bombing. Number matching and phishing-resistant MFA defeat it.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Several MFA push denials or timeouts for one user within minutes (Entra error 500121), then an approval, often with a sign-in from a new location. Ask the user whether they approved it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1621/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1621</a></article>
    <article id="ntds-dit-extraction" class="bad-term"><h3>NTDS.dit Extraction</h3><p>Copying the Active Directory database (ntds.dit) and the SYSTEM hive from a domain controller to extract every account's hash offline.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Ntdsutil "ifm" or vssadmin shadow-copy commands on a domain controller, or ntds.dit appearing outside its normal path.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1003.003</a></article>
    <article id="ntlm-relay" class="bad-term"><h3>NTLM Relay</h3><p>Forwarding a victim's NTLM handshake to another service (SMB, LDAP, MSSQL, or AD CS HTTP enrollment) to authenticate as the victim without knowing the password. Relaying to an SMB server is often called SMB relay. Usually paired with authentication coercion (PetitPotam, the printer bug) or LLMNR/NBT-NS poisoning. Blunted by SMB signing (required by default for inbound and outbound on Windows 11 24H2 Pro/Enterprise/Education, outbound only on Server 2025), LDAP signing and channel binding, Extended Protection for Authentication on AD CS, and Microsoft's deprecation of NTLM (announced 2024; NTLMv1 removed in Windows 11 24H2 / Server 2025).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> NTLM network logons (4624 type 3) for a user or machine account from an IP that is not their host, especially machine accounts authenticating to AD CS web enrollment or LDAP, and LLMNR or NBT-NS responses from an unexpected host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1557/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1557.001</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/storage/file-server/smb-signing" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft SMB signing</a><span id="smb-relay" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="kerberos-overpass-the-hash" class="bad-term"><h3>Overpass-the-Hash (Pass-the-Key)</h3><p>Using a stolen NT hash or Kerberos key to request a real Kerberos ticket, so the attacker continues with Kerberos instead of NTLM and blends in with normal domain authentication.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> TGT requests (4768) with RC4 encryption (0x17) for an account that normally gets AES, from a host the user is not logged on to.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1550/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1550</a></article>
    <article id="pass-the-hash" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Pass-the-Hash</h3><p>Using stolen password hashes to authenticate without needing to crack or know the actual password. Also called hash injection, because tools such as Mimikatz (sekurlsa::pth) inject the stolen NT hash into a new logon session.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> On the source host, 4624 logon type 9 with logon process seclogo; across the network, NTLM type 3 logons for an admin account fanning out from a workstation with no matching interactive logon by that user.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1550/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1550.002</a><span id="hash-injection" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="pass-the-ticket" class="bad-term"><h3>Pass-the-Ticket</h3><p>Stealing a Kerberos ticket (TGT or service ticket) from one session and injecting it into another to authenticate as that user without the password or hash. A stolen TGT can request tickets for any service the identity is entitled to; a stolen service ticket opens only the one service it was issued for.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A Kerberos ticket used from a host other than the one it was issued to (the client address in 4769 or 4624 does not match the 4768 that issued the TGT), and Rubeus or Mimikatz kerberos::ptt on the source host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1550/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1550.003</a></article>
    <article id="password-cracking" class="bad-term"><h3>Password Cracking</h3><p>Using brute force, dictionary attacks, or rainbow tables to recover plaintext passwords from hashes.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Offline, so invisible in your logs. You see the inputs (hash theft, Kerberoasting) and the result (a valid logon with a recovered password). Weak or old service-account passwords are the usual victims.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1110/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1110.002</a></article>
    <article id="password-spraying" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Password Spraying</h3><p>Trying a small number of common passwords against many accounts to avoid lockouts.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many accounts failing with the same error in the same window (Windows 4625, Entra error 50126), whether from one source or a pool of rotating residential proxies.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1110/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1110.003</a></article>
    <article id="prt-theft" class="bad-term"><h3>PRT Theft</h3><p>Stealing an Entra ID Primary Refresh Token, the device-bound token Windows uses for single sign-on to Microsoft cloud services. A stolen PRT gives the attacker the user's cloud sessions with MFA already satisfied. Token protection and hardware-backed keys make extraction harder.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Sign-ins with a PRT from a device ID or IP that does not match the registered device, browser-cookie or token tools on the endpoint, or a Defender alert for PRT access. Revoke sessions and review the device registration.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1528</a></article>
    <article id="rainbow-table" class="bad-term"><h3>Rainbow Table</h3><p>Precomputed table of hash values used to reverse cryptographic hashes, recovering plaintext passwords more efficiently. Salting defeats it: a unique random value added to each password before hashing makes precomputed tables useless, so rainbow tables matter mainly against unsalted hashes such as Windows LM and NTLM.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Offline, so there are no logs. Its triage relevance is exposure: treat any stolen unsalted hash (NTLM, legacy LM, old web-application MD5) as a recoverable password.</p></article>
    <article id="session-hijack" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Session Hijack</h3><p>Taking over an active session by stealing or guessing session tokens or cookies, bypassing authentication and MFA.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The same session token or cookie used from two IPs, devices or user agents at once, or activity with no interactive sign-in before it. Revoke the session, not just the password.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1539</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1550/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1550.004</a></article>
    <article id="shadow-credentials" class="bad-term"><h3>Shadow Credentials</h3><p>Writing an attacker-controlled key to a target account's msDS-KeyCredentialLink attribute, then using it to request Kerberos tickets as that account.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Unexpected changes to msDS-KeyCredentialLink (event 5136).</p></article>
    <article id="silver-ticket-attack" class="bad-term"><h3>Silver Ticket Attack</h3><p>Similar to Golden Ticket but forges a Kerberos service ticket (TGS) with the target service account's key, giving access to that one service. The forged ticket never touches a domain controller, which makes it quieter than a golden ticket.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Service access (4624 on the target) with no matching service-ticket request (4769) on any domain controller, because a forged service ticket never touches one; also RC4 tickets presented to services that only use AES.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1558/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1558.002</a></article>
    <article id="token-impersonation" class="bad-term"><h3>Token Impersonation</h3><p>Duplicating another process's existing Windows access token (for example with DuplicateTokenEx) and acting under it, the classic route from a service account holding SeImpersonatePrivilege to SYSTEM. Creating a fresh token from stolen credentials is the sibling technique T1134.003.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A service-account process (IIS, SQL Server) spawning a SYSTEM process, potato-family tools (JuicyPotato, PrintSpoofer, GodPotato) on disk or in command lines, or a SYSTEM shell under a web or database server parent.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1134/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1134.001</a></article>
    <article id="token-manipulation" class="bad-term"><h3>Token Manipulation</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK's umbrella technique (T1134) for abusing Windows access tokens: impersonating or stealing another process's token (T1134.001), creating a process with a stolen token (T1134.002), building a new token from stolen credentials (T1134.003), and spoofing a parent process (T1134.004). Token Impersonation and Parent PID Spoofing on this page are its common forms.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process running as a different user from its parent with no logon event to explain it, EDR alerts on DuplicateTokenEx or CreateProcessWithTokenW, and 4624 logon type 9 (new credentials) on the host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1134/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1134</a></article>
    <article id="token-theft" class="bad-term"><h3>Token Theft</h3><p>Stealing an already-issued bearer credential that has passed MFA (browser session cookie, OAuth access or refresh token, Entra Primary Refresh Token, cloud CLI token) from browsers, disk caches, memory or an AiTM proxy, then replaying it from attacker infrastructure. Theft: T1539 Steal Web Session Cookie, T1528 Steal Application Access Token. Replay: T1550.004, T1550.001. Duplicating a Windows access token on an endpoint is the separate T1134.001 (see Token Impersonation).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The same session or refresh token used from a new IP or ASN with no new interactive sign-in, especially right after an infostealer or AiTM event. Revoke the sessions and tokens; a password reset alone does not end it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1528</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1539</a></article>
  </div>

    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An attacker tries one password, Autumn2026!, against many accounts at once." class="bad-anim bad-anim--spray">

          <g transform="translate(265,60)"><circle r="14" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/><text y="3" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">👤</text></g>
          <g transform="translate(285,100)"><circle r="14" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/><text y="3" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">👤</text></g>
          <g transform="translate(265,140)"><circle r="14" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/><text y="3" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">👤</text></g>
          <g transform="translate(220,170)"><circle r="14" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/><text y="3" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">👤</text></g>
        </g>
        {/* single password attempting many users */}
        <g class="bad-anim__pw" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">
          <text x="100" y="50">Autumn2026!</text>

          <text x="100" y="150">Autumn2026!</text>

          <line x1="80" y1="100" x2="220" y2="30"/>
          <line x1="80" y1="100" x2="265" y2="60"/>
          <line x1="80" y1="100" x2="285" y2="100"/>
          <line x1="80" y1="100" x2="265" y2="140"/>

    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Password Spraying</h3>
      <p>One common password, many accounts. The inverse of brute force on a single account, designed to avoid lockouts and stay under detection thresholds. A seasonal candidate such as <code>Autumn2026!</code> tried once against every user in a directory will get a few hits and almost never trip a lockout. In identity-provider logs that looks like many accounts failing with the same error (bad password) in the same window, whether from one source or spread across many rotating IPs, often repeated at intervals over days.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Brute Force */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An attacker cycles common passwords such as 123456, qwerty and letmein against one account until one works." class="bad-anim bad-anim--brute">
        {/* attacker */}
        <rect x="20" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="50" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker</text>
        {/* password attempts cycling */}
        <g font-size="11" fill="#ef4444" class="bad-anim__pwlist">
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--1">password</text>
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--2">123456</text>
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--3">qwerty</text>
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--4">letmein</text>
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--5">admin</text>
          <text x="140" y="50" class="bad-anim__pw bad-anim__pw--6">Welcome1</text>
        </g>
        <text x="140" y="75" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">attempt #N</text>
        {/* lock target */}
        <g class="bad-anim__lockTarget">
          <rect x="240" y="70" width="60" height="60" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
          <path d="M255 80 V72 a15 15 0 0 1 30 0 V80" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="3" fill="none"/>
          <rect x="250" y="80" width="40" height="35" rx="3" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <circle cx="270" cy="98" r="3" fill="#ef4444"/>
        </g>
        {/* line */}
        <line x1="80" y1="100" x2="240" y2="100" stroke="#7f1d1d" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        <text x="160" y="160" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">cycle every common password</text>
        <text x="160" y="175" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">until one works</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Brute Force</h3>
      <p>The opposite of password spraying: one account, many passwords. The attacker cycles through common candidates (<code>password</code>, <code>123456</code>, <code>qwerty</code>, <code>letmein</code>) against a single account. Lockout policies and rate limits exist to defeat this; weak detection lets it grind silently for hours.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Pass-the-Hash */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 180" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An NTLM hash dumped from memory on host A authenticates the attacker to host B; no password is ever cracked." class="bad-anim bad-anim--pth">
        <rect x="20" y="60" width="80" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="60" y="82" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">host A</text>
        <text x="60" y="100" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">memory dump</text>
        {/* the hash flying across */}
        <g class="bad-anim__hashpkt">
          <rect x="0" y="-10" width="110" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1"/>
          <text x="55" y="5" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">NTLM hash 31d6...</text>
          <animateMotion dur="5s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M115 85 L240 85"/>
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="0;1;1;0" keyTimes="0;0.15;0.85;1" dur="5s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </g>
        <rect x="240" y="60" width="80" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="280" y="82" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">host B</text>
        <text x="280" y="100" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">auth as victim</text>
        <text x="170" y="160" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">no password ever cracked</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Pass-the-Hash</h3>
      <p>The attacker never needs the plaintext password. The NTLM hash from host A authenticates against host B because the protocol treats the hash as the credential. Silent, valid until the password changes, and the backbone of many lateral-movement campaigns. Triage signal: a 4624 logon with LogonType 9 and the seclogo logon process on the source host, or NTLM network logons for one account fanning out across many hosts.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Credential Dumping */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Credential hashes for several accounts stream out of lsass.exe memory to the attacker." class="bad-anim bad-anim--dump">
        {/* LSASS process */}
        <rect x="20" y="20" width="120" height="160" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        <text x="80" y="40" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">lsass.exe</text>
        <g class="bad-anim__creds" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="32" y="65" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--1">alice:31d6cf...</text>
          <text x="32" y="85" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--2">bob:a4f8c2...</text>
          <text x="32" y="105" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--3">svc-bot:e9b1...</text>
          <text x="32" y="125" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--4">admin:8c5fd...</text>
          <text x="32" y="145" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--5">dba:f1a3b9...</text>
          <text x="32" y="165" class="bad-anim__cred bad-anim__cred--6">root:c2d8e4...</text>
        </g>
        {/* attacker */}
        <g>
          <rect x="220" y="80" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
          <text x="260" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker</text>
        </g>
        <path d="M140 100 L220 100" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="4,3" fill="none"/>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Credential Dumping</h3>
      <p>LSASS memory holds reusable credential material (NT hashes, Kerberos tickets, sometimes plaintext) for interactive, RDP, RunAs, service and scheduled-task logons; plain network logons leave none. Tools like Mimikatz read it directly. EDR flags unusual processes opening lsass.exe for memory read (Sysmon Event ID 10). On current Windows 11 Enterprise, Credential Guard (default on eligible 22H2+ devices) and LSA protection/RunAsPPL (default on new enterprise-joined 22H2+ installs) block the classic dump.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Golden Ticket */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="The stolen krbtgt hash forges a TGT valid for 10 years, which lets the attacker impersonate anyone and own the domain." class="bad-anim bad-anim--gold">
        {/* krbtgt key */}
        <g class="bad-anim__krbtgt">
          <rect x="20" y="60" width="80" height="36" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#facc15" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="60" y="76" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#facc15">krbtgt hash</text>
          <text x="60" y="89" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">(stolen)</text>
        </g>
        {/* arrow forge */}
        <path d="M105 78 L130 78" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        {/* forged TGT */}
        <g class="bad-anim__tgt">
          <rect x="135" y="56" width="80" height="44" rx="6" fill="#facc15" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="2"/>
          <text x="175" y="74" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" font-weight="800" fill="#1a0606">TGT</text>
          <text x="175" y="90" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" font-weight="700" fill="#1a0606">forged · 10y</text>
        </g>
        <path d="M220 78 L245 78" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        {/* domain admin */}
        <g>
          <rect x="250" y="56" width="60" height="44" rx="6" fill="#ef4444" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="2"/>
          <text x="280" y="74" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" font-weight="700" fill="#fff">domain</text>
          <text x="280" y="88" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" font-weight="700" fill="#fff">owned</text>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="140" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">krbtgt → forge TGT → impersonate anyone</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Golden Ticket Attack</h3>
      <p>With the <code>krbtgt</code> account hash, an attacker forges a Kerberos Ticket Granting Ticket that authenticates as anyone, for as long as they want. The fix is rotating the krbtgt password twice, but only after the attacker's access is removed (otherwise they simply re-harvest the new key) and with at least the maximum ticket lifetime (10 hours by default) plus full replication between the two resets. Done properly the procedure is routine; that it is so rarely done is why Golden Tickets make such durable persistence.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Session Hijack */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A user's session cookie is stolen and replayed by an attacker, and the server welcomes the attacker as the user with no new sign-in." class="bad-anim bad-anim--hijack">
        {/* user */}
        <g>
          <circle cx="50" cy="80" r="18" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="50" y="83" text-anchor="middle" font-size="14">👤</text>
          <text x="50" y="115" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">user</text>
        </g>
        {/* session token */}
        <g class="bad-anim__token">
          <rect x="0" y="0" width="100" height="24" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#facc15" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="50" y="16" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#facc15">cookie: SID=...</text>
          <animateMotion dur="6s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M75 80 L200 80 L200 30"/>
        </g>
        {/* attacker */}
        <g>
          <rect x="170" y="20" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
          <text x="200" y="44" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker</text>
        </g>
        {/* server now thinks attacker is user */}
        <g>
          <rect x="240" y="100" width="65" height="50" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="272" y="120" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">server</text>
          <text x="272" y="138" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#86efac">welcome, user</text>
        </g>
        <path d="M200 60 L240 105" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        <text x="160" y="180" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">cookie replayed · no re-auth required</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Session Hijack</h3>
      <p>The session cookie is the credential after login. Steal it through an AiTM phishing proxy, an infostealer, XSS or a network MITM, then replay it elsewhere: unless the session is bound to the device, the server sees a valid, already-MFA'd session. No password prompt, no MFA. Same session ID, different person. (Detection has to come from context: a new IP, ASN or device fingerprint on a session that never re-authenticated.)</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     5. STEALTH AND DEFENSE IMPAIRMENT
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="evasion" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M10.733 5.076a10.744 10.744 0 0 1 11.205 6.575 1 1 0 0 1 0 .696 10.747 10.747 0 0 1-1.444 2.49"/><path d="M14.084 14.158a3 3 0 0 1-4.242-4.242"/><path d="M17.479 17.499a10.75 10.75 0 0 1-15.417-5.151 1 1 0 0 1 0-.696 10.75 10.75 0 0 1 4.446-5.143"/><path d="m2 2 20 20"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Stealth and defense impairment</h2>
      <p>ATT&amp;CK v19 (April 2026) splits the old Defense Evasion tactic in two. Stealth (TA0005) is hiding in plain sight; Defense Impairment (TA0112) is breaking the defenders' tools and telemetry. Each card below names the tactic it belongs to.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/alert/detection-mechanisms/">Alert</a> · <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="binary-obfuscation" class="bad-term"><h3>Binary Obfuscation</h3><p>Code is deliberately scrambled, encrypted, or disguised to make analysis harder and evade static analysis engines.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> File analysis showing high entropy, few readable strings and few imports, or a sample that only unpacks in memory. Obfuscation raises suspicion; behavior decides the verdict.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027</a></article>
    <article id="binary-padding" class="bad-term"><h3>Binary Padding</h3><p>Adds non-functional data or junk code to malware files to change their hash, inflate them past scanner size limits, and avoid signature-based detection.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Files far larger than their content needs, often over 100 MB, with long runs of null or junk bytes at the end. Many scanners skip large files, which is the point.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027.001</a></article>
    <article id="byovd" class="bad-term"><h3>Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver (BYOVD)</h3><p>Loading a legitimately signed but vulnerable kernel driver, then exploiting it to kill EDR processes or disable kernel protections from ring 0. The most common EDR-killer technique in 2025-2026 ransomware. Microsoft's vulnerable driver blocklist prevents many known drivers.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A new kernel driver service created (event 7045) or driver loaded (Sysmon event 6) shortly before security agents stop reporting.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1685/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1685</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1068/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1068</a></article>
    <article id="clear-windows-event-logs" class="bad-term"><h3>Clear Windows Event Logs</h3><p>Wiping Windows event logs (wevtutil cl, Clear-EventLog) to destroy evidence. T1685.005 in ATT&amp;CK v19; previously T1070.001. Event 1102 (Security log cleared) and 104 (System log cleared) are themselves high-signal alerts.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Security event 1102 or System event 104, wevtutil cl or Clear-EventLog in command lines, and gaps in forwarded logs. Clearing is rarely routine on a server, and your SIEM copy may still hold what was erased.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1685/005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1685.005</a></article>
    <article id="command-line-obfuscation" class="bad-term"><h3>Command-Line Obfuscation</h3><p>Malicious commands hidden using encoding (Base64), string concatenation, or misleading syntax to bypass command-line monitoring.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Command lines with -enc or -EncodedCommand, caret and escape characters, string concatenation or environment-variable substrings. Decode before judging: PowerShell script-block logging (4104) records the decoded script.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/010/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027.010</a></article>
    <article id="debugger-evasion" class="bad-term"><h3>Debugger Evasion</h3><p>Malware checks whether a debugger is attached (IsDebuggerPresent, timing checks) and changes behavior or exits so analysts cannot step through it.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Mostly a sandbox and reverse-engineering concern. A sample that behaves differently under analysis than in the field is evidence of evasion, not innocence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1622/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1622</a></article>
    <article id="delayed-execution" class="bad-term"><h3>Delayed Execution</h3><p>Malware deliberately waits (sleep calls, scheduled tasks, ping loops, API hammering) before acting, so automated sandboxes time out before they see malicious behaviour. ATT&amp;CK T1678 Delay Execution; also called time-based evasion. Waiting for user input such as mouse clicks or a document closing is a separate check, T1497.002 User Activity Based Checks.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Long sleeps, ping -n loops or timeout commands before the payload, and scheduled tasks set to run hours later. When a sandbox report is clean, check how long the sandbox ran.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1678/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1678</a><span id="time-based-evasion" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="disable-or-modify-tools" class="bad-term"><h3>Disable or Modify Tools</h3><p>Stopping, uninstalling, or crippling security tools: killing EDR processes (often with EDR killers such as AvNeutralizer or through BYOVD), tampering with AMSI (Antimalware Scan Interface) or ETW (Event Tracing for Windows), adding Defender exclusions, or stopping log forwarders. Replaced T1562 Impair Defenses in ATT&amp;CK v19. Pre-ransomware EDR killing is among the highest-signal events an analyst can catch; a host that suddenly stops reporting is a finding, not a gap.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Defender event 5007 (configuration change) adding exclusions, EDR or log-forwarder services stopped, AMSI or ETW patching flagged by EDR, and agents that go silent. Check the agent's health before trusting its silence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1685/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1685</a></article>
    <article id="email-hiding-rules" class="bad-term"><h3>Email Hiding Rules (Inbox Rules)</h3><p>Mailbox rules an attacker creates in a compromised account to move, mark as read or delete the messages the owner should not see: security alerts, replies from a payment contact, anything containing words such as "invoice", "wire" or "hacked". Common in business email compromise. A forwarding rule that copies mail to an outside address is the collection side of the same trick (T1114.003). Both live in the mailbox and survive a password reset.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> New-InboxRule, Set-InboxRule or UpdateInboxRules in the Microsoft 365 unified audit log, especially a rule that deletes mail or moves it to RSS Feeds, Archive or Conversation History, matches finance or security keywords, has a one-character name, or was created in the same session as a risky sign-in. Export the rules as evidence before they are removed; a password reset alone leaves them running.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1564/008/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1564.008</a></article>
    <article id="encoded-payloads" class="bad-term"><h3>Encoded Payloads</h3><p>Payloads encoded or encrypted (Base64, XOR) to hide true content until runtime, evading static detection.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Long Base64 or hex strings in scripts, command lines or registry values, and processes decoding them (certutil -decode, FromBase64String).</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/013/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027.013</a></article>
    <article id="environment-checks" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Environment Checks</h3><p>Malware inspects its environment for VM or sandbox artifacts before running (T1497), sometimes called virtual environment awareness: system checks such as disk size, drivers and MAC prefixes (T1497.001), user-activity checks such as mouse movement (T1497.002), and time-based checks such as uptime or sped-up sleeps (T1497.003). Checking for an attached debugger is the separate technique T1622 Debugger Evasion. A sample that is silent in the sandbox but active on the endpoint is evidence of evasion, not innocence.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Queries for VM artifacts (VirtualBox or VMware drivers and registry keys, virtual MAC prefixes), and uptime, screen-resolution or mouse-activity checks early in execution.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1497/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1497</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1622/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1622</a><span id="virtual-environment-awareness" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="exploitation-for-defense-impairment" class="bad-term"><h3>Exploitation for Defense Impairment</h3><p>Exploiting a vulnerability in security software or the operating system to switch off or bypass a defense, for example a flaw in an EDR agent or a kernel bug that lets the attacker unload protections. BYOVD is the most common form, using a vulnerable third-party driver.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A security product crashing or its service stopping just after an exploit-like event or a new driver load, or a known-vulnerable version of the security tool itself. Check the agent version against vendor advisories.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1687/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1687</a></article>
    <article id="file-extension-spoofing" class="bad-term"><h3>File Extension Spoofing</h3><p>Files disguised with misleading extensions (e.g., <code>invoice.pdf.exe</code>) to trick users and bypass basic file-type filters. It works because Windows File Explorer hides known file extensions by default. The Unicode right-to-left override character (U+202E, T1036.002) pulls off the same trick by visually reversing the end of the file name.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> File names with double extensions or the right-to-left override character (U+202E) in attachments or downloads, and an executable launched from a file whose name ends in a document type.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/007/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1036.007</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1036.002</a></article>
    <article id="html-smuggling" class="bad-term"><h3>HTML Smuggling</h3><p>Building a malicious file inside the victim's browser from JavaScript or encoded data in an HTML attachment or page, so email and web gateways only ever see HTML. LNK smuggling hides commands in shortcut files in a similar way (T1027.012).</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An HTML attachment or page that builds a download in the browser (a Blob or data URI), then a ZIP, ISO or script in Downloads with no matching web download in the proxy logs.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/006/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027.006</a></article>
    <article id="lolbins" class="bad-term"><h3>LOLBins</h3><p>Living Off the Land Binaries: trusted, signed system executables (certutil, rundll32, mshta) weaponized so the malicious part is the argument line, not the file on disk. The evasion face of the Living Off The Land technique covered under Execution.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Signed system binaries with network or script arguments: certutil -urlcache, mshta with a URL, regsvr32 /i: with a URL (Squiblydoo), rundll32 with a DLL from a user path. The parent and the arguments are the evidence.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://lolbas-project.github.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: LOLBAS project</a></article>
    <article id="low-and-slow" class="bad-term"><h3>Low-and-Slow</h3><p>Performing actions gradually or in minimal increments (scheduled transfers, small data chunks, sparse logins) to remain under detection thresholds. Detection needs long look-back windows rather than per-hour thresholds.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Exfiltration (TA0010)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Small, regular transfers or logins that never trip per-hour thresholds but add up over weeks. Use long look-back queries and per-entity totals.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1029/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1029</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1030/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1030</a></article>
    <article id="spoofed-macs" class="bad-term"><h3>MAC Address Spoofing</h3><p>Changing a network interface's hardware (MAC) address to impersonate an allowed device and slip past network access control (NAC) or MAC filtering. Note that phones and laptops now randomize Wi-Fi MACs by default, so a changing MAC is usually privacy behavior, not an attack.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005); no dedicated ATT&amp;CK technique</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> One MAC appearing on two switch ports, a known device's MAC on a new port or with a new OS fingerprint, or NAC logs showing an allowed MAC failing posture checks.</p></article>
    <article id="mark-of-the-web-bypass" class="bad-term"><h3>Mark-of-the-Web Bypass</h3><p>Delivering files in containers or formats that do not carry the Mark-of-the-Web zone tag (ISO, VHD, some archives) so SmartScreen and Office's macro block never apply. A file from the internet that lacks MOTW is worth asking about.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> ISO, VHD, IMG or archive files mounted or opened from email or downloads, and executables or LNK files run from the mounted drive with no Zone.Identifier stream.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1553/005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1553.005</a></article>
    <article id="masquerading" class="bad-term"><h3>Masquerading</h3><p>Disguising a malicious process, file or task as a trusted one (sometimes called process masquerading): svchost.exe running from C:\Users\Public, or a scheduled task named like a Windows one. Check the path, signer and parent, not the name. Matching a legitimate name or location (T1036.005) is the most common form.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A system binary name running from the wrong path (svchost.exe outside System32), a parent that does not fit (svchost not started by services.exe), or a signer that does not match the name.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1036</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/005/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1036.005</a><span id="process-masquerading" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="parent-pid-spoofing" class="bad-term"><h3>Parent PID Spoofing</h3><p>Spawns processes that appear to be launched by trusted executables (like <code>explorer.exe</code>), misleading analysts and EDR that rely on the process tree.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process whose reported parent could not have created it (explorer.exe as the parent of a SYSTEM process), or EDR showing a creator process that differs from the PPID. The ParentImage field alone can be fooled.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1134/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1134.004</a></article>
    <article id="polymorphic-malware" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Polymorphic Malware</h3><p>Malware that changes its bytes, and therefore its hash, with each copy (re-encryption, mutated decryptor stubs) while its behavior stays the same. It defeats hash IOCs, not behavioral detection.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many different hashes with the same behavior, detection names or C2. Pivot on behavior and infrastructure; hash IOCs will not match the next copy.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1027</a></article>
    <article id="right-to-left-override" class="bad-term"><h3>Right-to-Left Override (RLO)</h3><p>The Unicode control character U+202E, which displays the text after it in reverse. A file actually named <code>invoice</code>, then U+202E, then <code>fdp.exe</code> displays as "invoiceexe.pdf", so an executable passes for a PDF. A Masquerading technique; see also File Extension Spoofing.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Search file, attachment and process-creation logs for the U+202E character itself, not for the displayed name, and treat any file name that contains it as suspect. An executable whose displayed name ends in a document extension is the same tell. Some tools render the character as garbled bytes; see <a href="/docs/labs/">Telemetry labs</a>.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1036/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1036.002</a></article>
    <article id="safe-mode-boot" class="bad-term"><h3>Safe Mode Boot</h3><p>Rebooting a host into Safe Mode, where most security agents do not load, before running ransomware or other payloads (bcdedit /set safeboot).</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Defense Impairment (TA0112)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> bcdedit /set safeboot in a command line, a service added under the SafeBoot registry keys, and a reboot shortly after. Security agents go quiet during that reboot.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1688/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1688</a></article>
    <article id="subdomain-abuse" class="bad-term"><h3>Subdomain Abuse</h3><p>Using subdomains that genuinely belong to trusted services, so attacker traffic inherits the parent domain's reputation: an attacker's own app or bucket on a shared cloud platform (anyone can create an Azure web app, and its hostname sits under <code>azurewebsites.net</code>), or the takeover of one of your DNS records that still points at a deprovisioned cloud resource.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Resource Development (TA0042)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Links or connections to tenant-style subdomains on trusted platforms (azurewebsites.net, cloudfront.net, pages.dev) your business does not use, and your own DNS records pointing at deprovisioned cloud resources.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1584/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1584.001</a></article>
    <article id="signed-binary-proxy-execution" class="bad-term"><h3>System Binary Proxy Execution (formerly Signed Binary Proxy Execution)</h3><p>ATT&amp;CK's umbrella technique (T1218) for running malicious code through trusted system binaries such as rundll32, regsvr32, mshta and msiexec, so execution looks like normal OS activity. See LOLBins.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> rundll32, regsvr32, mshta or msiexec loading a script or DLL from a URL or user-writable path, especially with an Office app, browser or script host as the parent.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1218/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1218</a></article>
    <article id="timestomping" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Timestomping</h3><p>Alters file timestamps to make malicious activity appear older or less suspicious, usually by copying the times of a legitimate system file. On NTFS, common tools change only the standard-information timestamps, not those in the file-name attribute.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Stealth (TA0005)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> NTFS $STANDARD_INFORMATION times earlier than the $FILE_NAME times, timestamps with zeroed sub-second values, or a file older than the folder it sits in.
