Sysmon event 2 records file creation-time changes.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1070/006/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1070.006</a></article>
    <article id="user-agent-spoofing" class="bad-term"><h3>User-Agent Spoofing</h3><p>Alters HTTP headers to mimic trusted applications or browsers, blending into normal web traffic. A browser user agent coming from a process that is not a browser is the tell.</p><p class="bad-status">Tactic: Command and Control (TA0011)</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Proxy logs where the user-agent string does not match the process making the request (from EDR network events), outdated or rare browser versions, or one host presenting many agents in a short window.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071.001</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Polymorphic / shape-shift animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 180" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="One malware sample keeps changing its hash while its behavior stays the same." class="bad-anim bad-anim--poly">
        <text x="40" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#fca5a5" class="bad-anim__poly1">
          0x4a3b9e2c
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="1;0;0;1;1" keyTimes="0;0.25;0.5;0.75;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </text>
        <text x="40" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#fca5a5" class="bad-anim__poly2">
          0xdeadbeef
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="0;1;0;0;0" keyTimes="0;0.25;0.5;0.75;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </text>
        <text x="40" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#fca5a5" class="bad-anim__poly3">
          0x9f1c5d22
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="0;0;1;0;0" keyTimes="0;0.25;0.5;0.75;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </text>
        <text x="40" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#fca5a5" class="bad-anim__poly4">
          0xc0ffee01
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="0;0;0;1;0" keyTimes="0;0.25;0.5;0.75;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </text>
        <text x="40" y="115" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">↑ signature changes; behavior stays the same</text>
        {/* behavior box */}
        <rect x="200" y="50" width="100" height="80" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="250" y="80" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">behavior:</text>
        <text x="250" y="98" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">read keys</text>
        <text x="250" y="113" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">exfil HTTPS</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Polymorphic Malware</h3>
      <p>Each copy re-encrypts or mutates itself: different hashes, different bytes, identical behavior. Signature-based detection sees four "different" samples; behavior-based detection sees one campaign. The hash on the left cycles while the behavior box on the right stays static. This is why TTP-based hunting outperforms IOC matching, and why even commodity malware now defeats hash blocklists.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Sandbox / VM Detection */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Malware checks disk size, uptime and mouse movement, finds VMware Tools, decides it is in a sandbox and exits clean." class="bad-anim bad-anim--sandbox">
        <rect x="20" y="20" width="280" height="160" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="160" y="40" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">malware boots, runs environment checks</text>
        <g font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="40" y="65" class="bad-sb-check bad-sb-check--1">✓ disk size  &gt; 60 GB?</text>
          <text x="40" y="85" class="bad-sb-check bad-sb-check--2">✓ uptime    &gt; 20 min?</text>
          <text x="40" y="105" class="bad-sb-check bad-sb-check--3">✓ user moved mouse?</text>
          <text x="40" y="125" class="bad-sb-check bad-sb-check--4">✗ VMware Tools found</text>
          <text x="40" y="145" class="bad-sb-check bad-sb-check--5">→ sandbox detected · exit clean</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Sandbox / Environment Detection</h3>
      <p>Most sandboxes are not very lived-in. They have small disks, brief uptime, no mouse activity, suspicious driver names, and known artifacts. Malware that runs checks like these (disk size, uptime, mouse movement, VM drivers) will sit silent in analysis, then unfurl on a real user's host. A sample that is silent in the sandbox but active on the endpoint is evidence of evasion, not innocence.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Timestomping */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An implant's November 2024 created and modified timestamps are rewritten to July 2009 to blend in with system files." class="bad-anim bad-anim--stomp">
        <rect x="40" y="30" width="240" height="140" rx="8" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="160" y="50" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">implant.dll metadata</text>
        <g font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="60" y="80">created:   <tspan class="bad-stomp-real">2024-11-04 09:13</tspan></text>
          <text x="60" y="80">created:   <tspan class="bad-stomp-fake">2009-07-13 17:14</tspan></text>
          <text x="60" y="105">modified:  <tspan class="bad-stomp-real">2024-11-04 09:14</tspan></text>
          <text x="60" y="105">modified:  <tspan class="bad-stomp-fake">2009-07-13 17:14</tspan></text>
          <text x="60" y="130">accessed:  <tspan class="bad-stomp-real">2024-11-04 09:14</tspan></text>
          <text x="60" y="130">accessed:  <tspan class="bad-stomp-fake">2009-07-13 17:14</tspan></text>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="160" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">timestamps overwritten · file looks ancient</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Timestomping</h3>
      <p>Attackers rewrite the MACB timestamps (modified, accessed, entry changed, born/created) on dropped files to a date that matches everything around them. A binary that "shipped with Windows" in 2009 will not catch the eye of an analyst scanning recent-changes lists. Same bytes, different forensic story. The tell: most tools rewrite only the $STANDARD_INFORMATION timestamps, so a $FILE_NAME creation time later than $SI, sub-second fields of exactly zero, or a USN (Update Sequence Number) journal entry dated this week all expose the lie.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     6. C2 INFRASTRUCTURE
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="c2" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M4.9 16.1C1 12.2 1 5.8 4.9 1.9"/><path d="M7.8 4.7a6.14 6.14 0 0 0-.8 7.5"/><circle cx="12" cy="9" r="2"/><path d="M16.2 4.8c2 2 2.26 5.11.8 7.47"/><path d="M19.1 1.9a9.96 9.96 0 0 1 0 14.1"/><path d="M9.5 18h5"/><path d="m8 22 4-11 4 11"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Command and control infrastructure</h2>
      <p>Mechanisms adversaries use to communicate with and control compromised systems, and the infrastructure tricks that keep those channels alive.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="beaconing" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Beaconing</h3><p>Periodic network communication from an infected host to a C2 server. Legitimate software beacons too (updaters, telemetry and EDR agents), so baseline known-good cadences before you pivot.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Connections from one host to one destination at a steady interval (with or without jitter) over hours, small consistent request sizes, a destination rare in your environment, and a process that is not a known updater.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071</a></article>
    <article id="bulletproof-hosting" class="bad-term"><h3>Bulletproof Hosting (BPH)</h3><p>Hosting services known for ignoring abuse complaints and allowing malicious activities to operate with minimal risk of takedown. Traffic to a known BPH network is a strong risk multiplier during triage. CISA and partners published dedicated mitigation guidance in November 2025.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Connections to ASNs or IP ranges flagged as bulletproof hosting in threat intelligence, especially from servers. Weigh the process: a browser visiting is different from a service beaconing.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/resources-tools/resources/bulletproof-defense-mitigating-risks-bulletproof-hosting-providers" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="c2-framework" class="bad-term"><h3>C2 Framework</h3><p>A commercial or open-source toolkit that provides implants, listeners and operator consoles for hands-on-keyboard intrusions: Cobalt Strike, Sliver, Havoc, Mythic, Brute Ratel C4. Malleable profiles let operators reshape traffic to imitate legitimate services, so detection keys on implant behavior and beacon shape rather than fixed signatures.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Implant behaviors rather than names: default or odd named pipes, sacrificial processes (rundll32 or dllhost with no arguments), in-memory loaders, and regular beacons. One confirmed running implant means an operator whose access no control has contained: the escalation tripwire fires, and the case escalates as hands-on-keyboard activity.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071</a></article>
    <article id="command-and-control-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Command and Control (C2)</h3><p>The channel an attacker uses to send commands to compromised systems and receive results or stolen data (ATT&amp;CK tactic TA0011). It usually hides in common protocols or trusted cloud services, so rhythm and destination give it away, not the port.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A host repeatedly talking to a destination it has no business with, on a rhythm. Confirmed C2 that no control has blocked means an attacker can act on that host now: the escalation tripwire fires.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0011</a></article>
    <article id="custom-encryption-protocols" class="bad-term"><h3>Custom Encryption Protocols</h3><p>Non-standard encryption schemes that wrap malicious traffic to avoid detection by DPI or SSL/TLS inspection.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> High-entropy payloads on ports or protocols that should be plain text or standard TLS, with no recognizable TLS handshake. JA4 or JA3 fingerprints that match no known client help.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1573/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1573</a></article>
    <article id="custom-protocols-over-non-standard-ports" class="bad-term"><h3>Custom Protocols over Non-Standard Ports</h3><p>Known protocols on ports they are not normally paired with (HTTPS on 8088, SSH on 443), or fully custom protocols (a home-grown binary C2 protocol over raw TCP), chosen to slip past detection that expects known service behavior.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A protocol and port mismatch in network metadata (TLS on 8088, SSH on 443, an unknown binary protocol on 80), especially from servers. Zeek or firewall application identification usually flags it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1571/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1571</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1095/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1095</a></article>
    <article id="dead-drop-resolver-ddr" class="bad-term"><h3>Dead Drop Resolver (DDR)</h3><p>Malware fetches a pointer, usually an encoded domain or IP, from content posted on a legitimate service (Pastebin, GitHub, a social-media profile) and then connects to the real C2 server it points to. The legitimate site hides the C2 address from static analysis and lets the operator rotate infrastructure. Pivot on whatever the host connects to next. If the commands themselves are read from the web service, that is T1102.002 (bidirectional) or T1102.003 (one-way).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A non-browser process fetching a specific Pastebin, GitHub or social-media page, then connecting to a new IP or domain within seconds.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1102/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1102.001</a></article>
    <article id="dns-tunneling" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>DNS Tunneling</h3><p>Abusing DNS to encapsulate and exfiltrate data or create covert C2, bypassing traditional network controls.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Long, high-entropy subdomain labels under one parent domain, high query volume to it, TXT or NULL record types, and one host generating most of that traffic.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071.004</a></article>
    <article id="domain-fronting" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Domain Fronting</h3><p>Disguises the true destination of HTTPS traffic by routing through a legitimate front-facing domain: the DNS lookup and TLS SNI name an allowed domain on a CDN, while the HTTP Host header inside the encryption names the attacker's domain on the same CDN. Many major CDNs now reject the mismatch, so it is less common than it was.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> With TLS inspection, an SNI and Host header that do not match. Without it, a non-browser process sending steady traffic to a CDN-hosted name the business does not use.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1090.004</a></article>
    <article id="domain-generation-algorithm-dga" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Domain Generation Algorithm (DGA)</h3><p>Algorithm generating many domain names on the fly, making C2 connections hard to block via static domain blocklists.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Bursts of NXDOMAIN responses for machine-generated names from one host, followed by a successful lookup and connection to one of them.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1568/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1568.002</a></article>
    <article id="dynamic-resolution-fallback-channels" class="bad-term"><h3>Dynamic Resolution / Fallback Channels</h3><p>Ways an implant finds its C2 when the primary address is blocked: computed domains (DGA), rotating DNS answers (fast flux), or a built-in list of backup domains and protocols it falls back to (sometimes called callback domain rotation). Blocklisting one address rarely ends the session; hunt for the next destination the host tries.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> After a block, the same host trying new domains or IPs within minutes on the same cadence and from the same process. Pivot on the process and the timing, not the indicator.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1568/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1568</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1008/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1008</a><span id="callback-domain-rotation" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="email-based-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Email-based C2</h3><p>Using SMTP/IMAP or a cloud mail API to send or receive commands and data, hidden in attachments or content that blends with business traffic.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A non-mail process connecting to SMTP, IMAP or a mail API, or a mailbox whose drafts or folders change on a schedule with no user activity. Mailbox audit and Graph API logs carry it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071.003</a></article>
    <article id="fast-flux-dns" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Fast Flux DNS</h3><p>Rapidly rotating DNS records that cycle a malicious domain through many IP addresses, hiding C2 servers behind shifting infrastructure and defeating IP-based blocking and takedowns. The domain itself remains blockable through DNS filtering and sinkholing.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Domains with very short TTLs and many A records from scattered residential ASNs that change between lookups. Block and sinkhole the domain, not the IPs.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1568/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1568.001</a></article>
    <article id="https-c2" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>HTTPS C2</h3><p>Standard HTTP/HTTPS traffic carrying C2, blending with normal web activity.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Steady HTTPS sessions from a non-browser process to a young domain or rare certificate, with uniform sizes. JA4 fingerprints of known frameworks help when you have them.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1071/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1071.001</a></article>
    <article id="icmp-tunneling" class="bad-term"><h3>ICMP Tunneling</h3><p>Embedding commands or data in ICMP echo packets (ping) to establish covert C2 communication that traditional firewalls often miss.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> ICMP echo packets with large or varying payloads, steady pings to one external host, or ICMP volume far above baseline from one machine.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1095/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1095</a></article>
    <article id="orb-network" class="bad-term"><h3>ORB Network</h3><p>Operational relay box network: compromised or leased routers, IoT devices and VPS nodes chained into a proxy mesh that nation-state actors route through (Volt Typhoon's KV botnet is one example). Traffic arrives from residential or small-business IPs near the victim, which defeats IP reputation and geolocation checks.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Activity from residential or small-business IPs near you that change often, against VPN or edge devices. IP reputation will look clean, so pivot on the account, the device and the behavior.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1090</a></article>
    <article id="peer-to-peer-p2p-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Peer-to-Peer (P2P) C2</h3><p>Mesh-like networks where infected systems communicate with each other to share commands and updates without a centralized server, so taking down one node does not cut the botnet off.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Internal hosts talking to each other on unusual high ports, or many internal hosts reaching many external consumer IPs with no central destination.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1090</a></article>
    <article id="protocol-tunneling" class="bad-term"><h3>Protocol Tunneling</h3><p>Encapsulates malicious traffic within legitimate protocols (HTTPS, DNS, ICMP) to bypass network detection.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> One protocol carrying traffic it should not: SSH inside HTTPS, RDP over an SSH tunnel (plink, chisel), or a tunneling tool binary on a server. Find the tool, then the session.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1572/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1572</a></article>
    <article id="residential-proxies" class="bad-term"><h3>Residential Proxies</h3><p>Commercial or criminal proxy services that route attacker traffic through home internet connections, so sign-ins and sprays appear to come from ordinary consumer ISPs. A reason "one source" detections fail and per-IP rate limits barely slow attackers.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Sign-ins from consumer ISPs in unexpected regions that change between attempts while the user agent or device fingerprint stays the same. Detect on the account and the fingerprint, not the IP.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1090.002</a></article>
    <article id="slack-based-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Slack-based C2</h3><p>Abuse of Slack's APIs, webhooks, or tokens to create covert C2 by embedding commands or exfiltrated data within messages or bots. The same pattern runs over Discord, Microsoft Graph/OneDrive, Google Sheets/Drive and Dropbox; pivot on which process is talking to the API, not on the destination.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process other than the Slack client calling Slack API or webhook endpoints, or a bot token used from an unexpected host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1102/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1102.002</a></article>
    <article id="social-media-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Social Media C2</h3><p>Using X (formerly Twitter), Facebook or Instagram posts, images or profiles on trusted domains to carry encoded commands or retrieve data.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A non-browser process fetching social-media profile or post URLs on a schedule, followed by new outbound connections or commands.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1102/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1102</a></article>
    <article id="telegram-bots" class="bad-term"><h3>Telegram Bots</h3><p>Leveraging Telegram's bot API to issue commands or receive data, creating C2 over an encrypted, trusted messaging platform.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Connections to api.telegram.org from processes that are not the Telegram client, especially from servers or scripts. Infostealers and RATs use it to send stolen data.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1102/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1102.002</a></article>
    <article id="tor-based-c2" class="bad-term"><h3>Tor-based C2</h3><p>Leveraging Tor to anonymize attacker and malware communication, making attribution and traffic analysis significantly harder.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Connections to known Tor relays or Tor ports (9001, 9030, or 9050 locally), tor.exe or a renamed copy on a host. Tor from a server is suspicious in most organizations.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1090/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1090.003</a></article>
    <article id="tunneling-services" class="bad-term"><h3>Tunneling Services</h3><p>Legitimate reverse-tunnel services (ngrok, Cloudflare Tunnel, Microsoft dev tunnels) that expose an internal host or relay C2 through a trusted vendor domain. The domain is reputable, so pivot on which process opened the tunnel and whether the organization uses the service.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> ngrok, cloudflared or devtunnel binaries on hosts that do not use them, DNS lookups for those services' domains, and a long-lived outbound session from a server. Ask who started the tunnel.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1572/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1572</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Beaconing animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="An infected host checks in with its C2 server every 60 seconds." class="bad-anim bad-anim--beacon">
        {/* infected host */}
        <rect x="30" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="60" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">host</text>
        {/* C2 server */}
        <rect x="230" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="260" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">C2</text>
        {/* pulse rings */}
        <g class="bad-anim__rings" fill="none" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5">
          <circle cx="60" cy="100" r="20" class="bad-anim__ring bad-anim__ring--1"/>
          <circle cx="60" cy="100" r="20" class="bad-anim__ring bad-anim__ring--2"/>
          <circle cx="60" cy="100" r="20" class="bad-anim__ring bad-anim__ring--3"/>
        </g>
        {/* dotted line to C2 */}
        <line x1="92" y1="100" x2="228" y2="100" stroke="#7f1d1d" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        {/* packet */}
        <circle r="5" fill="#ef4444">
          <animate attributeName="cx" values="92;228;228" keyTimes="0;0.7;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
          <animate attributeName="cy" values="100;100;100" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="1;1;0" keyTimes="0;0.7;1" dur="4s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </circle>
        <text x="160" y="135" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">every 60s</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Beaconing</h3>
      <p>Periodic check-in from an infected host to its C2 server. The cadence is the signature: regular intervals (60s, 5m, 1h) with consistent packet sizes betray a beacon even when the destination and protocol look legitimate. Modern implants add jitter (randomizing the sleep around a base interval), so hunters look for tight statistical distributions rather than exact periodicity. Legitimate software beacons too (updaters, telemetry and EDR agents), so baseline before you escalate.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: DNS Tunneling */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Data encoded in subdomain labels such as aGVsbG8.dGhpcw.attacker.example passes through DNS to the attacker's name server, looking normal to the firewall." class="bad-anim bad-anim--dns">
        {/* host */}
        <rect x="20" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="50" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">host</text>
        {/* DNS server (looks legit) */}
        <rect x="140" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="170" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">DNS</text>
        {/* attacker NS */}
        <rect x="260" y="80" width="60" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        <text x="290" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker NS</text>
        {/* DNS query packet */}
        <g class="bad-anim__pkt">
          <rect x="85" y="50" width="180" height="20" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1"/>
          <text x="175" y="64" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">aGVsbG8.dGhpcw.attacker.example</text>
        </g>
        <text x="175" y="40" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">↑ data encoded in subdomain</text>
        {/* arrows */}
        <g stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="3,3">
          <path d="M80 100 L140 100" fill="none"/>
          <path d="M200 100 L260 100" fill="none"/>
        </g>
        <text x="170" y="155" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">DNS looks normal to the firewall</text>
        <text x="170" y="170" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">but carries C2 and exfil data</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>DNS Tunneling</h3>
      <p>The data is in the subdomain. <code>aGVsbG8.dGhpcw.attacker.example</code> looks like a DNS query to your firewall, but the labels are base64-encoded chunks of command-and-control traffic. Because DNS is rarely blocked or deep-inspected, the channel hides in plain sight. Long, high-entropy subdomains to the same parent zone are the canonical detection signal. Encrypted DNS (DNS over HTTPS) that bypasses the corporate resolver removes that visibility, so unsanctioned DoH from an endpoint is itself worth a look.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Fast Flux DNS */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 280 220" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="One domain, bad.example, resolves to a different IP address every 60 seconds." class="bad-anim bad-anim--flux">
        {/* domain in center */}
        <circle cx="140" cy="110" r="36" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        <text x="140" y="108" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">bad.example</text>
        <text x="140" y="122" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">TTL: 60s</text>
        {/* rotating IPs */}
        <g class="bad-anim__flux-orbit">
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--1">
            <rect x="200" y="30" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="235" y="46" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">203.0.113.7</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--2">
            <rect x="200" y="100" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="235" y="116" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">198.51.100.42</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--3">
            <rect x="200" y="170" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="235" y="186" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">192.0.2.99</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--4">
            <rect x="10" y="30" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="45" y="46" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">203.0.113.88</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--5">
            <rect x="10" y="100" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="45" y="116" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">198.51.100.5</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__ip bad-anim__ip--6">
            <rect x="10" y="170" width="70" height="22" rx="4" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1"/>
            <text x="45" y="186" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">192.0.2.31</text>
          </g>
        </g>
        {/* connecting lines */}
        <g stroke="#7f1d1d" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="2,2">
          <line x1="170" y1="110" x2="200" y2="40"/>
          <line x1="172" y1="110" x2="200" y2="110"/>
          <line x1="170" y1="110" x2="200" y2="180"/>
          <line x1="110" y1="110" x2="80"  y2="40"/>
          <line x1="108" y1="110" x2="80"  y2="110"/>
          <line x1="110" y1="110" x2="80"  y2="180"/>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Fast Flux DNS</h3>
      <p>One domain. Six (or sixty, or six hundred) IPs rotating beneath it on short TTLs, usually compromised machines acting as proxies. Block one IP and the next resolution returns another. Defenders trying to take down the infrastructure cannot pin it long enough for a block to stick. The stable pivot is the domain itself (and its name servers, when they rotate too in double flux): block or sinkhole the domain, and treat the IPs as disposable, possibly victim-owned, hosts.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: DGA */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Malware generates a list of random domain names each day and tries them all; only the one the attacker registered resolves to the live C2." class="bad-anim bad-anim--dga">
        <text x="170" y="28" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">generated daily, only one is live</text>
        <g font-size="12" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--1">xj4kq9z2.test</text>
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--2">m7bf3vl8.example</text>
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--3">c2pq8whe.invalid</text>
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--4">tx9k4rmn.test</text>
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--5">f3hp7vlq.example</text>
          <text x="170" y="65" text-anchor="middle" class="bad-dga bad-dga--6">a1c8bd0r.invalid</text>
        </g>
        <g font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">
          <text x="170" y="95" text-anchor="middle">attacker registers 1 of N each day</text>
          <text x="170" y="110" text-anchor="middle">malware tries all; only 1 resolves</text>
        </g>
        <rect x="100" y="130" width="140" height="34" rx="6" fill="#ef4444" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="170" y="148" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" font-weight="700" fill="#fff">today's live C2:</text>
        <text x="170" y="162" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fff" class="bad-anim__dga-live">c2pq8whe.invalid</text>
        <text x="170" y="185" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">static block lists can't keep up</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Domain Generation Algorithm</h3>
      <p>Instead of a hardcoded C2 domain, the malware computes a fresh list each day (or hour) from a seed. The attacker registers just one of the generated names. The malware tries them all; only the registered one resolves. Static blocklists lag behind because the candidate names change continuously. Researchers who reverse-engineer a DGA can predict and pre-block future names (registries locked every Conficker A domain this way in 2009), but per-victim seeds and thousands of candidates a day make that hard to repeat. The more reliable signal is the search itself: bursts of NXDOMAIN (no such domain) responses for random-looking names. Wordlist DGAs that string real words together are harder to spot by entropy alone.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: HTTPS C2 hiding in plain sight */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A TLS-inspected proxy log in which one beacon hides among ordinary HTTPS requests and stands out only by its regular rhythm." class="bad-anim bad-anim--httpsc2">
        <rect x="20" y="30" width="300" height="140" rx="8" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="170" y="50" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">enterprise proxy log (TLS-inspected)</text>
        <g font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">
          <text x="32" y="75">GET https://cdn.example.com/assets/main.js</text>
          <text x="32" y="92">GET https://api.weather.example/forecast</text>
          <text x="32" y="109" class="bad-anim__hidden-c2">GET https://updates-cdn.example/heartbeat</text>
          <text x="32" y="126">GET https://fonts.cdn.example/css2</text>
          <text x="32" y="143">GET https://api.git.example/repos/acme/app</text>
          <text x="32" y="160">GET https://chat.example/api/conversations.list</text>
        </g>
        <g font-size="9" fill="#fecaca" text-anchor="end">
          <text x="312" y="75">200</text>
          <text x="312" y="92">200</text>
          <text x="312" y="109" class="bad-anim__hidden-c2">200</text>
          <text x="312" y="126">200</text>
          <text x="312" y="143">200</text>
          <text x="312" y="160">200</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>HTTPS C2</h3>
      <p>The beacon hides in normal-looking HTTPS traffic: <code>200 OK</code>, encrypted body, a plausible hostname. At a glance it blends in with its neighbors. What gives it away is shape, not content: regular timing, near-constant byte counts, a young or rarely visited domain, a user agent that does not match the host's browser. TLS inspection and JA4 fingerprinting add more signal (JA3 is degraded against modern browsers).</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Domain Fronting */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="The firewall sees an allowed CDN name in the SNI and DNS lookup, while the Host header inside the encrypted request routes to the attacker's C2." class="bad-anim bad-anim--front">
        {/* outer SNI */}
        <rect x="20" y="30" width="300" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="170" y="48" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">SNI / DNS: cdn.bigcloud.example (looks legit)</text>
        <text x="170" y="62" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#86efac">↑ what the firewall sees</text>
        <path d="M170 75 L170 95" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        {/* inner Host header */}
        <rect x="20" y="100" width="300" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="170" y="118" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#ef4444">Host: attacker-c2.evil.example</text>
        <text x="170" y="132" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">↑ where traffic actually goes</text>
        <text x="170" y="175" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">outer name allowed · inner Host routes anywhere</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Domain Fronting</h3>
      <p>The SNI (and DNS lookup) point at a trusted content delivery network (CDN), so the firewall sees only the allowed front. Inside the encrypted request, the <code>Host:</code> header redirects to attacker infrastructure on the same CDN. That mismatch between outer and inner names is where the technique lives, and why most major CDNs eventually shut the door on it. Encrypted Client Hello (ECH) now hides the inner name legitimately, so the outer-versus-inner comparison only works where TLS is inspected.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     7. DATA THEFT AND EXFILTRATION
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="exfil" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M21 15v4a2 2 0 0 1-2 2H5a2 2 0 0 1-2-2v-4"/><path d="m17 8-5-5-5 5"/><path d="M12 3v12"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Data theft and exfiltration</h2>
      <p>How data leaves: attacker exfiltration techniques, plus the accidental exposures that trigger the same regulatory clocks. "Breach" is a legal word: whether an event is a reportable breach is decided by privacy or legal counsel, not the analyst, so describe what happened and escalate (see <a href="/docs/scope/regulatory/">Scope &gt; Regulatory</a>).</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/scope/">Scope</a> · <a href="/docs/risk/impact-likelihood/">Risk</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <h3 class="bad-subhead">Attacker techniques</h3>
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="archive-collected-data" class="bad-term"><h3>Archive Collected Data</h3><p>Compressing and often password-protecting collected files (7-Zip, WinRAR, tar) before exfiltration, which shrinks transfers and hides content from inspection.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> 7z.exe, rar.exe or tar run with a password switch (-p, -hp) against shares or user folders, producing large archives in Temp, ProgramData or a share root.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1560/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1560</a></article>
    <article id="data-exfiltration" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Data Exfiltration</h3><p>Deliberate and unauthorized transfer of data from a system or network to an external location (ATT&amp;CK tactic TA0010). The covert routes, sometimes called data leakage channels, include the C2 channel itself, cloud storage, DNS tunneling and other protocol tunnels; each has its own entry on this page.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Outbound volume that breaks the host's or user's baseline, to a first-seen destination, from an unusual process, often after archive creation. Record what left, how much and when: confirmed exfiltration is an escalation criterion and may start regulatory clocks.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0010/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK TA0010</a><span id="data-leakage-channels" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="data-harvesting" class="bad-term"><h3>Data Harvesting</h3><p>Automated or bulk collection of data via scrapers, malware, or scripts, often used for profiling, fraud, or resale.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> High request rates from one client or rotating proxies walking through IDs or pages in order, unusual API pagination, and accounts that read far more records than they work on.</p></article>
    <article id="data-staging" class="bad-term"><h3>Data Staging</h3><p>Collected data consolidated in one place before exfiltration (a local directory, a file share, or a cloud instance), often compressed and encrypted into archives (T1560). Staging reduces the number of outbound connections. A multi-gigabyte .7z or .rar appearing in ProgramData, %TEMP% or a share root is one of the best pre-exfiltration detection opportunities. Splitting transfers to stay under thresholds is a separate technique (T1030).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Large archives or copied file sets appearing in Temp, ProgramData, the recycle bin or a share root, created by a server process or remote session shortly before outbound transfers.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1074/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1074</a></article>
    <article id="data-theft-extortion" class="bad-term"><h3>Data-Theft Extortion</h3><p>Stealing data and demanding payment to keep it off a leak site, with no encryption at all (Cl0p's file-transfer campaigns are the defining example). There may be no ransomware alert, so the exfiltration itself is the incident.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Exploitation of an internet-facing file-transfer or data platform, then bulk downloads, then an extortion email to executives. The platform's own logs show which files left.</p></article>
    <article id="encrypted-web-uploads" class="bad-term"><h3>Encrypted Web Uploads</h3><p>Data uploaded over HTTPS to cloud storage or attacker-controlled servers, so content inspection sees only encrypted traffic. Volume, destination age and the uploading process are the signals.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Bytes out far larger than bytes in to a single new destination, from a process that does not normally upload, often outside working hours.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1048/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1048.002</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1567/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1567</a></article>
    <article id="exfiltration-over-c2-channel" class="bad-term"><h3>Exfiltration Over C2 Channel</h3><p>Sending stolen data out over the same channel the implant already uses for commands, so there is no new destination to spot; the signal is a change in upload volume on an existing beacon.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An existing beacon's upload size jumps, or its interval changes during a data copy. Compare bytes out before and after the staging activity.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1041/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1041</a></article>
    <article id="exfiltration-to-cloud-storage" class="bad-term"><h3>Exfiltration to Cloud Storage</h3><p>Bulk upload or sync of stolen data to a cloud storage service (MEGA, Dropbox, a cloud bucket) with tools such as rclone.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Rclone.exe or a renamed copy on a server, its config file, or sustained outbound volume to a storage provider the host never used before.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1567/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1567.002</a></article>
    <article id="insider-data-theft" class="bad-term"><h3>Insider Data Theft</h3><p>Unauthorized removal of sensitive data by someone with legitimate access: an employee, contractor, or a fraudulent remote hire such as a DPRK IT worker. Signals are behavioral (unusual volume, new destinations, off-hours access), not malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Observable technical activity only: bulk downloads or repository clones, uploads to personal storage, USB writes (event 6416 plus DLP), or printing far outside the person's baseline. HR-held context reaches the case only through the insider-risk route.</p></article>
    <article id="sensitive-data-in-transit" class="bad-term"><h3>Interception in Transit (Adversary-in-the-Middle)</h3><p>Capturing or altering data as it crosses a network, by sniffing unencrypted protocols, ARP or LLMNR poisoning, or proxying a TLS session (as AiTM phishing kits do to steal session cookies).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> ARP, LLMNR or NBT-NS responses from an unexpected host, clients presented with untrusted certificates, and sign-ins from a phishing proxy's IP right after the user's own.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1557/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1557</a></article>
    <article id="transfer-data-to-cloud-account" class="bad-term"><h3>Transfer Data to Cloud Account</h3><p>Moving data to another cloud account the attacker controls inside the same provider (sharing a snapshot or bucket cross-account), so no data crosses the network perimeter. Only cloud audit logs show it.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Cloud audit events that share a snapshot, image, disk or bucket with an external account ID, such as ModifySnapshotAttribute or a bucket policy granting another account in AWS CloudTrail.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1537/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1537</a></article>
  </div>

  <h3 class="bad-subhead">Exposure conditions</h3>
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="data-breach" class="bad-term"><h3>Data Breach</h3><p>A security incident that results in unauthorized access to, or accidental or unlawful loss, alteration, destruction or disclosure of, protected data. Whether an event is legally a breach (and starts a notification clock) is for privacy or legal counsel to decide, not the analyst. Scale is irrelevant, and accidental disclosures can qualify. See <a href="/docs/scope/regulatory/">Scope &gt; Regulatory</a>.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Not an analyst call. Your job is the facts: what data, how many records, whose, whether it was accessed or taken, and when you became aware of it. Record the time of awareness, because legal clocks may run from it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://gdpr-info.eu/art-4-gdpr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: GDPR Art. 4(12)</a></article>
    <article id="data-exposure" class="bad-term"><h3>Data Exposure</h3><p>Data left accessible without proper controls, often due to misconfigurations like open cloud storage, unsecured APIs, or an application revealing internal details or user data. Information disclosure is the matching vulnerability class (CWE-200): a system reveals internal details, metadata or user data to someone not entitled to it. Exposure is a condition, not proof of access: check the access logs before you call it theft.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A public bucket, open share, unauthenticated API or verbose error found by a scan or report. Pull the access logs for the whole exposure window before deciding whether anyone took anything.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/200.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CWE-200</a><span id="information-disclosure" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="data-leak" class="bad-term"><h3>Data Leak</h3><p>Informal term for data exposed outside its intended boundary, often accidentally (misdirected email, public bucket). An accidental leak of personal data or protected health information (PHI) can still be a reportable breach, so escalate it the same way. Not the same as extortion leak sites, where stolen data is published deliberately. See <a href="/docs/scope/regulatory/">Scope &gt; Regulatory</a>.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A misdirected email, public link or open bucket reported by a user or scanner. Capture who could reach the data, for how long, and whether access logs show anyone did.</p></article>
    <article id="shadow-data" class="bad-term"><h3>Shadow Data</h3><p>Sensitive or regulated data stored in unmanaged, forgotten, or unsanctioned locations: abandoned cloud buckets, rogue spreadsheets, old backups, and prompts or files pasted into unsanctioned AI tools.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Data discovery or DSPM tools finding regulated data in unmanaged locations, and uploads of sensitive files to unsanctioned SaaS or AI tools in CASB or proxy logs.</p></article>
    <article id="third-party-data-exposure" class="bad-term"><h3>Third-Party Data Exposure</h3><p>Compromise or exposure of sensitive data caused by a breach, misconfiguration, or negligence on the part of an external vendor or partner. Your own logs may show nothing, yet the data is still yours, and so is the notification decision.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Vendor breach notices, threat-intelligence reports or leak-site listings naming a supplier, and unusual activity by that vendor's integrations or accounts in your tenant (OAuth apps, API keys, support access).</p></article>
    <article id="unstructured-data-exposure" class="bad-term"><h3>Unstructured Data Exposure</h3><p>Leakage of documents, logs, screenshots, or audio: content that is harder to classify or monitor with traditional DLP tools.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> DLP alerts on documents, screenshots or recordings leaving through chat, email or upload, and bulk access to file shares or meeting recordings by one account.</p></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Data Exfiltration */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 340 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Files such as PII.csv, prod.db and keys.json leak across the corporate perimeter to an attacker's cloud drop." class="bad-anim bad-anim--exfil">
        {/* corporate perimeter */}
        <rect x="20" y="30" width="180" height="150" rx="10" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="6,4"/>
        <text x="110" y="22" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fca5a5">corporate perimeter</text>
        {/* files inside */}
        <g font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--a">
            <rect x="40" y="50" width="60" height="22" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d"/>
            <text x="50" y="65">📄 PII.csv</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--b">
            <rect x="40" y="80" width="60" height="22" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d"/>
            <text x="50" y="95">📄 prod.db</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--c">
            <rect x="40" y="110" width="60" height="22" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d"/>
            <text x="50" y="125">📄 keys.json</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--d">
            <rect x="40" y="140" width="60" height="22" rx="3" fill="#7f1d1d"/>
            <text x="50" y="155">📄 source.zip</text>
          </g>
        </g>
        {/* leak hole */}
        <circle cx="200" cy="105" r="9" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="2"/>
        {/* attacker drop */}
        <g>
          <rect x="240" y="80" width="80" height="50" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
          <text x="280" y="100" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker</text>
          <text x="280" y="115" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">cloud drop</text>
        </g>
        {/* moving file copy */}
        <g class="bad-anim__moving">
          <rect x="0" y="0" width="60" height="22" rx="3" fill="#ef4444"/>
          <text x="10" y="15" font-size="9" fill="#fff">leaking…</text>
          <animateMotion dur="5s" repeatCount="indefinite" path="M105 95 Q160 95 200 95 L260 95"/>
          <animate attributeName="opacity" values="0;1;1;0" keyTimes="0;0.15;0.85;1" dur="5s" repeatCount="indefinite"/>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Data Exfiltration</h3>
      <p>The deliberate, unauthorized transfer of data out of the network. Files leave through whatever channel the attacker can use: bulk sync to cloud storage with tools such as rclone or MEGA, uploads over the existing C2 channel, a DNS tunnel, or a slow drip below the DLP threshold. Watch for both shapes: a large, sustained outbound volume (often bandwidth-throttled) to a storage provider the host never talks to, and small repeating transfers in a direction the network was not expecting.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     8. EXPLOITED VULNERABILITIES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="vulns" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><rect x="3" y="11" width="18" height="11" rx="2"/><path d="M7 11V7a5 5 0 0 1 9.9-1"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Exploited vulnerabilities</h2>
      <p>Real-world CVEs and architectural flaws that adversaries used at scale, or that reshaped defense. For what is being exploited right now, check CISA's <a href="https://www.cisa.gov/known-exploited-vulnerabilities-catalog" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog</a>.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a> · <a href="/docs/risk/impact-likelihood/">Risk</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="bluekeep-cve-2019-0708" class="bad-term"><h3>BlueKeep (CVE-2019-0708)</h3><p>Critical RDP vulnerability allowing remote code execution without authentication on older Windows versions. "Wormable": it could in principle self-propagate like WannaCry, though no worm ever appeared (in-the-wild use from late 2019 was human-operated coin mining). Microsoft fixed it in the May 2019 Patch Tuesday and took the rare step of also patching out-of-support XP and Server 2003. Highlighted the danger of exposing RDP directly to the internet.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Inbound RDP (3389) from the internet to unpatched Windows 7, Server 2008 or older hosts, termdd.sys crashes, and IDS hits for the MS_T120 channel request. Start with exposure: is any unpatched RDP reachable?</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2019-0708" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2019-0708</a></article>
    <article id="citrix-bleed-cve-2023-4966" class="bad-term"><h3>Citrix Bleed (CVE-2023-4966)</h3><p>Memory over-read in Citrix NetScaler ADC and Gateway that leaks valid session tokens, letting attackers hijack already-authenticated sessions and skip passwords and MFA. Exploited as a zero-day from August 2023 and then by LockBit affiliates. Patching did not end the exposure: stolen sessions stayed valid until every session was killed.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Oversized requests to the appliance's OpenID configuration endpoint, then one session ID used from several IPs. Patching is not enough: every active session has to be killed, as the advisory required.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-325a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-325A</a></article>
    <article id="dirty-cow-cve-2016-5195" class="bad-term"><h3>Dirty COW (CVE-2016-5195)</h3><p>Linux kernel privilege escalation via a race condition in the copy-on-write handling of memory mappings. An unprivileged process with read access to a file can race the COW path to write the underlying page, modifying files it should only be able to read, including system binaries owned by root. Discovered after an in-the-wild exploit was captured against a Linux server; later weaponized by Android malware (ZNIU) to root devices. Impacted a wide range of distributions and Android.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An unprivileged local process gaining root on an old Linux kernel, often with modified setuid binaries or /etc/passwd. Mostly relevant now on embedded and legacy Linux, so check kernel versions.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2016-5195" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2016-5195</a></article>
    <article id="eternalblue" class="bad-term"><h3>EternalBlue</h3><p>NSA SMBv1 exploit leaked by Shadow Brokers in 2017. Buffer overflow allows unauthenticated remote code execution. It was instrumental in WannaCry and NotPetya, and spurred deprecation of SMBv1 and stronger patching practices.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> SMBv1 traffic, especially one host reaching many on port 445, IDS signatures for the exploit, and SYSTEM-level processes appearing on the target with no logon. SMBv1 still enabled anywhere is a Scope finding.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2017-0144" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2017-0144</a></article>
    <article id="follina-cve-2022-30190" class="bad-term"><h3>Follina (CVE-2022-30190)</h3><p>Vulnerability in the Microsoft Support Diagnostic Tool (MSDT) triggered via crafted Word documents. Executes code through the ms-msdt protocol handler without macros when the document is opened; the RTF variant fires from the Explorer preview pane with no click at all. Stealthier than VBA-based attacks, it was used in phishing campaigns before it was fully addressed; MSDT itself is now deprecated.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> msdt.exe launched by an Office process with ms-msdt or PCWDiagnostic on the command line, followed by sdiagnhost.exe spawning a shell.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2022-30190" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2022-30190</a></article>
    <article id="heartbleed-cve-2014-0160" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Heartbleed (CVE-2014-0160)</h3><p>OpenSSL flaw allowing attackers to read up to 64KB of server memory per request (private keys, passwords, session tokens). About 17% of TLS-enabled web servers were vulnerable at disclosure.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> TLS heartbeat requests whose declared length is far larger than their payload, against old OpenSSL. Treat keys and sessions on an exposed server as compromised: rotate certificates and revoke sessions.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2014-0160" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2014-0160</a></article>
    <article id="ivanti-connect-secure-cve-2023-46805" class="bad-term"><h3>Ivanti Connect Secure (CVE-2023-46805 / CVE-2024-21887)</h3><p>An authentication bypass chained with a command injection in Ivanti's VPN appliance, exploited as zero-days by a China-nexus actor from December 2023 and then at scale in January 2024, prompting a CISA emergency directive. A defining case of edge-device exploitation that leaves little or no endpoint telemetry, and of attackers persisting through appliance upgrades.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Appliance logs and the vendor's integrity checker, not endpoint telemetry: unexpected files on the appliance, web shell paths, and internal connections from the appliance that it would not normally make. Reset credentials that passed through it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/directives/ed-24-01-mitigate-ivanti-connect-secure-and-ivanti-policy-secure-vulnerabilities" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA ED 24-01</a></article>
    <article id="log4shell-cve-2021-44228" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228)</h3><p>Critical RCE in Apache Log4j. A logged string containing <code>&#36;&#123;jndi:ldap://…&#125;</code> triggers a JNDI lookup; the attacker hosts a class on the referenced server that the JVM then fetches and runs.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Strings such as jndi:ldap, jndi:rmi or jndi:dns (often obfuscated) in any logged field, followed by an outbound LDAP or RMI connection from the Java server. The outbound connection, not the string, shows the attempt worked.</p><p>Campaign history: <a href="#log4shell">Log4Shell (2021)</a>.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2021-44228" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2021-44228</a></article>
    <article id="pan-os-globalprotect-cve-2024-3400" class="bad-term"><h3>PAN-OS GlobalProtect (CVE-2024-3400)</h3><p>Unauthenticated command injection (CVSS 10.0) in the GlobalProtect feature of Palo Alto Networks firewalls, exploited as a zero-day in April 2024 to run code as root on the firewall itself. Another reminder that the security appliance at the edge is itself attack surface.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Unusual entries in the firewall's GlobalProtect and telemetry logs, unexpected files or cron jobs on the device, and outbound connections from the firewall itself. Run the vendor's checks: endpoint tools cannot see inside the appliance.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2024-3400" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2024-3400</a></article>
    <article id="proxylogon" class="bad-term"><h3>ProxyLogon</h3><p>The March 2021 Exchange Server zero-day chain (CVE-2021-26855 server-side request forgery, chained with CVE-2021-26857, -26858 and -27065) first used by HAFNIUM and then mass-exploited by many groups to plant web shells. ProxyShell, a separate chain, followed in August 2021.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Exchange application pools (w3wp.exe) spawning cmd or powershell, new .aspx files in Exchange web directories, and unusual requests to /ecp/ or /owa/auth/. Web shells from 2021 may still sit on servers that were patched without being checked.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2021-26855" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2021-26855</a></article>
    <article id="proxyshell" class="bad-term"><h3>ProxyShell</h3><p>Three chained Exchange vulnerabilities (CVE-2021-34473, -34523, -31207) allowing unauthenticated SYSTEM-level RCE through AutoDiscover and PowerShell endpoint abuse. Mass exploitation followed the August 2021 Black Hat talk, with attackers dropping web shells and staging ransomware. Not the same as ProxyLogon, the March 2021 zero-day chain.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Requests to /autodiscover/autodiscover.json carrying /mapi/nspi or /powershell, New-MailboxExportRequest writing .aspx files, and new web shells in Exchange directories.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2021-34473" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2021-34473</a></article>
    <article id="shellshock-cve-2014-6271" class="bad-term"><h3>Shellshock (CVE-2014-6271)</h3><p>GNU Bash flaw allowing arbitrary command execution via environment variables. Especially dangerous through CGI scripts. It affected web servers, routers and IoT devices, exploits appeared within hours of disclosure, and it drove a broad review of UNIX-based software.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Request headers (User-Agent, Referer, Cookie) carrying a Bash function definition, the pattern that starts with '() ', aimed at CGI scripts or appliances, then the web server spawning bash, curl or wget.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2014-6271" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2014-6271</a></article>
    <article id="spectre-meltdown" class="bad-term"><h3>Spectre / Meltdown</h3><p>Hardware vulnerabilities in modern processors. Spectre (CVE-2017-5753, CVE-2017-5715) affects virtually all modern speculative CPUs (Intel, AMD, Arm); Meltdown (CVE-2017-5754) chiefly Intel, plus some Arm and IBM POWER cores (not AMD). Both exploit speculative execution to leak memory across trust boundaries. No confirmed in-the-wild exploitation, but the firmware, OS and cloud-hypervisor mitigations reshaped how hardware trust boundaries are viewed.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> No practical triage signal: exploitation would happen in memory and leave little trace. The work is exposure management: microcode, OS and hypervisor patches on shared hosts.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://meltdownattack.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: meltdownattack.com</a></article>
    <article id="zerologon-cve-2020-1472" class="bad-term"><h3>Zerologon (CVE-2020-1472)</h3><p>Netlogon protocol flaw. Netlogon used AES-CFB8 with a fixed all-zero IV, so about one in 256 all-zero authentication attempts succeeds. An unauthenticated attacker on the network can impersonate a domain controller, reset its machine-account password to empty, and then dump domain hashes (DCSync). Actively exploited soon after disclosure.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Netlogon events 5827 to 5829 (vulnerable Netlogon connections), a domain controller's machine-account password changed by ANONYMOUS LOGON (4742), then replication requests from a host that is not a domain controller.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2020-1472" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NVD CVE-2020-1472</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Heartbleed memory leak animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 180" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A heartbeat request makes OpenSSL return server memory to the attacker, leaking a session cookie, a private key and a user name." class="bad-anim bad-anim--bleed">
        {/* server */}
        <rect x="30" y="40" width="120" height="100" rx="8" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        <text x="90" y="95" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">OpenSSL</text>
        <text x="90" y="110" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">heartbeat</text>
        {/* attacker */}
        <rect x="200" y="55" width="100" height="70" rx="8" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
        <text x="250" y="95" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="#fecaca">attacker</text>
        {/* pipe with leaked bytes */}
        <g class="bad-anim__leak">
          <text x="160" y="80" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">cookie:Bz9q...</text>
          <text x="160" y="95" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">key:----BEG..</text>
          <text x="160" y="110" font-size="9" fill="#ef4444">user:admin</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Heartbleed (CVE-2014-0160)</h3>
      <p>OpenSSL heartbeat extension flaw that lets a remote attacker read up to 64KB of memory per request (private keys, passwords, session tokens, anything in the server's heap). The attack leaves no obvious log traces, which is why the post-disclosure cleanup required mass key rotation across the internet.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Log4Shell JNDI chain */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="User input containing a JNDI lookup reaches Log4j, which contacts the attacker's LDAP server, fetches a payload and runs it: four steps from a log line to code execution." class="bad-anim bad-anim--log4">
        {/* Step 1: malicious input */}
        <g class="bad-anim__step bad-anim__step--1">
          <rect x="20" y="30" width="120" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="80" y="48" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fecaca">user input</text>
          <text x="80" y="62" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#ef4444">${"${jndi:ldap://...}"}</text>
        </g>
        {/* Step 2: Log4j */}
        <g class="bad-anim__step bad-anim__step--2">
          <rect x="180" y="30" width="120" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="240" y="48" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">Log4j</text>
          <text x="240" y="62" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">resolves JNDI</text>
        </g>
        {/* Step 3: attacker LDAP */}
        <g class="bad-anim__step bad-anim__step--3">
          <rect x="180" y="100" width="120" height="40" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" stroke-dasharray="3,3"/>
          <text x="240" y="118" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">attacker LDAP</text>
          <text x="240" y="132" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fca5a5">serves payload</text>
        </g>
        {/* Step 4: RCE */}
        <g class="bad-anim__step bad-anim__step--4">
          <rect x="20" y="100" width="120" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ef4444" stroke="#fff" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="80" y="118" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fff" font-weight="700">RCE</text>
          <text x="80" y="132" text-anchor="middle" font-size="8" fill="#fff">code executes</text>
        </g>
        {/* arrows */}
        <g stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5" fill="none">
          <path d="M140 50 L180 50"/>
          <path d="M240 70 L240 100"/>
          <path d="M180 120 L140 120"/>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="170" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">step 1 → step 2 → step 3 → step 4</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228)</h3>
      <p>One line of user input becomes remote code execution. The JNDI lookup inside <code>{"${jndi:ldap://...}"}</code> tricks Log4j into contacting attacker-controlled infrastructure, which returns a payload that the JVM then executes. Four steps in sequence: an attacker submits a string, Log4j resolves it, LDAP serves a malicious class, the JVM runs it. (On patched JVMs, exploitation used deserialization gadget chains instead, and even failed attempts leaked secrets through <code>{"${env:...}"}</code> DNS lookups.)</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     9. MALWARE TYPES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="mtypes" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="m10 16 1.5 1.5"/><path d="m14 8-1.5-1.5"/><path d="M15 2c-1.798 1.998-2.518 3.995-2.807 5.993"/><path d="m16.5 10.5 1 1"/><path d="m17 6-2.891-2.891"/><path d="M2 15c6.667-6 13.333 0 20-6"/><path d="m20 9 .891.891"/><path d="M3.109 14.109 4 15"/><path d="m6.5 12.5 1 1"/><path d="m7 18 2.891 2.891"/><path d="M9 22c1.798-1.998 2.518-3.995 2.807-5.993"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Malware types</h2>
      <p>Categories of malware defined by technique, payload style, and evasion characteristics.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/alert/detection-mechanisms/">Alert</a> · <a href="/docs/uncover/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Term grid: before the featured animations, so a lookup lands on the list */}
  <div class="bad-terms">
    <article id="adware" class="bad-term"><h3>Adware</h3><p>Malware that displays unwanted advertisements, often bundled with legitimate software. Advanced adware can harvest data or serve as a delivery mechanism for more serious malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Browser extensions or bundled installers the user did not knowingly choose, scheduled tasks that relaunch them, and ad or redirect traffic. On its own it usually scores low impact; re-score if it downloads further payloads or harvests data.</p></article>
    <article id="backdoors" class="bad-term"><h3>Backdoors</h3><p>Malicious code that provides unauthorized access to a compromised system, bypassing normal authentication. Often installed post-exploitation to maintain persistent remote control.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A listening port or outbound connection from a process with no business reason, a new service or account with no change record, or a modified authentication module (sshd, PAM, an IIS module). A confirmed backdoor means the host is compromised.</p></article>
    <article id="banker-trojans" class="bad-term"><h3>Banker Trojans</h3><p>Malware designed to steal banking credentials and financial information by intercepting login sessions, injecting fake forms, or logging keystrokes. Often targets online banking and payment systems.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Browser processes with injected code, certificates or proxy settings added to the browser, and bank fraud alerts that line up with the host's sign-in times.</p></article>
    <article id="bootkits" class="bad-term"><h3>Bootkits</h3><p>Malware that tampers with the boot chain so it runs before the OS loads. On legacy BIOS systems that means the MBR/VBR; on UEFI systems it means boot files in the EFI System Partition (ATT&amp;CK T1542.003). BlackLotus (2023) was the first publicly known UEFI bootkit to bypass Secure Boot on fully updated Windows 11. Firmware implants (e.g., MoonBounce, CosmicStrand) go further and live in SPI flash, where they survive disk wipes and replacement, so remediation may require reflashing firmware, not just reimaging.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Changes in the EFI System Partition or boot configuration, Secure Boot reported as off, or a measured-boot or boot-integrity alert. Reimaging the OS volume alone may not remove it; the boot partition and firmware need checking too.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1542/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1542.003</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2023/03/01/blacklotus-uefi-bootkit-myth-confirmed/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ESET (2023)</a></article>
    <article id="botnet" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Botnet</h3><p>A network of infected machines (bots) controlled by an attacker through a central server or peer-to-peer relays, used for DDoS, spam, credential stuffing, proxying, and more.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many hosts making the same periodic connections to the same rare destination, or one host suddenly sending spam, scans or login attempts outbound.
