Treat each member as a compromised host.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/botnet" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="click-fraud-malware" class="bad-term"><h3>Click Fraud Malware</h3><p>Malware that simulates user clicks on advertisements to generate fraudulent revenue. Often run on compromised systems in large volumes to exploit pay-per-click ad platforms.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Hidden or headless browser instances generating ad traffic, and high volumes of requests to ad networks from servers or idle hosts.</p></article>
    <article id="crypters-packers" class="bad-term"><h3>Crypters / Packers</h3><p>Tooling that uses encryption or obfuscation to hide malicious code and evade antivirus. Not malicious on their own but commonly paired with trojans, RATs, or ransomware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> High entropy, unusual or missing section names (UPX0), and a tiny import table in a file analysis report. Legitimate software is packed too, so packing raises suspicion but does not settle the verdict.</p></article>
    <article id="cryptojacker" class="bad-term"><h3>Cryptojacker</h3><p>Malware that mines cryptocurrency on someone else's compute: hijacked servers, cloud instances created with stolen keys, or containers. The signal is sustained high CPU or GPU, connections to mining pools, and unexpected cloud spend.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> CPU or GPU pinned near 100 percent on a server or container, connections to mining-pool ports (3333, 4444, 5555) or stratum URLs, xmrig or a renamed copy, and new compute instances in regions the organization does not use.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1496/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1496.001</a></article>
    <article id="double-extortion" class="bad-term"><h3>Double Extortion</h3><p>Encrypting a victim's systems and stealing data, then threatening to publish it on a leak site if the ransom is not paid. Popularized by Maze in late 2019 and now standard; some crews skip encryption entirely (data-theft-only extortion). Restoring from backup does not end the incident, because the stolen data is still out there.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Large outbound transfers or rclone-style sync in the days before encryption, and archives staged on servers. When encryption hits, assume theft too until the exfiltration evidence says otherwise.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1486/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1486</a></article>
    <article id="banking-droppers-loaders" class="bad-term"><h3>Droppers and Loaders</h3><p>A dropper carries its payload inside itself; a downloader/loader fetches it from the network. Both exist to get a follow-on payload (stealer, C2 implant, ransomware) running, and in practice the terms are often used interchangeably. Notable families: Emotet, ZLoader, SmokeLoader, IcedID, Bumblebee, Latrodectus, HijackLoader, GootLoader, SocGholish. Europol's Operation Endgame (May 2024 onward, with further phases through 2026) has repeatedly disrupted this ecosystem, but operators tend to rebuild or move to new families.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A user-run file (script, MSI, LNK, ISO contents) followed within seconds by a new process from AppData or Temp, a scheduled task, and periodic outbound connections. Loader alerts come early: catch one and you head off what it was sent to fetch.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.operation-endgame.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Operation Endgame</a></article>
    <article id="fileless-malware" class="bad-term"><h3>Fileless Malware</h3><p>Malware that runs from memory, keeping little or nothing on disk (payloads often hide in the registry or WMI), which makes it harder for file-scanning AV to detect. Often leverages legitimate system tools like PowerShell or WMI for execution.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Encoded PowerShell command lines, scripts stored in registry values, WMI subscriptions, and script-block logs (PowerShell event 4104) showing decoded content. The evidence lives in memory, so preserve it before any reboot.</p></article>
    <article id="infostealers" class="bad-term"><h3>Infostealers</h3><p>Software that harvests sensitive data from victims: passwords, session cookies, browser history, autofill, crypto wallets. The resulting logs are sold in bulk and are a leading source of the valid credentials and session tokens used for initial access.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A cracked installer or fake update run by the user, then bulk reads of browser credential and cookie stores, then an upload to one new destination. Every credential and session on that device is exposed: passwords reset, sessions revoked, MFA methods reviewed.</p></article>
    <article id="iot-malware" class="bad-term"><h3>IoT Malware</h3><p>Designed to infect Internet of Things devices like routers, cameras, smart appliances. Often used in botnets (e.g., Mirai) due to weak authentication and outdated firmware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> IoT devices making outbound connections to rare destinations, scanning telnet or SSH internally, or sending sudden traffic bursts. These devices seldom keep logs; network flow data is the evidence.</p></article>
    <article id="keylogger" class="bad-term"><h3>Keylogger</h3><p>Malware or a malware module that records keystrokes, usually to capture passwords and messages. Often bundled into RATs, infostealers and banker trojans.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A process installing a low-level keyboard hook (SetWindowsHookEx with WH_KEYBOARD_LL) or polling GetAsyncKeyState, and small periodic uploads or log files appearing in user folders.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1056/001/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1056.001</a></article>
    <article id="logic-bombs" class="bad-term"><h3>Logic Bombs</h3><p>Malicious code triggered by specific conditions, such as a date or user action. Can remain dormant until activated, then execute destructive or stealthy functions.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Scheduled tasks, cron jobs or code paths keyed to a date or to an account being disabled, usually planted by someone with administrative rights. Review recent privileged changes to scripts and jobs when an administrator's access is removed.</p></article>
    <article id="mobile-malware" class="bad-term"><h3>Mobile Malware</h3><p>Malware targeting mobile devices, often distributed via malicious apps or phishing links. Includes SMS stealers, fake banking apps, mobile RATs.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> MDM or mobile threat defense alerts for sideloaded apps, apps requesting accessibility or SMS permissions, or a configuration profile installed outside MDM. Corporate sessions from that device are suspect until it is cleaned.</p></article>
    <article id="ransomware" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Ransomware</h3><p>Malware that encrypts a victim's files and demands payment for the key. Modern ransomware often adds data theft and extortion, and increasingly encrypts virtual machines from the hypervisor (ESXi) where endpoint agents cannot see it. Some groups skip encryption and extort on stolen data alone, so the absence of encrypted files does not rule out a ransomware-class incident.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Mass file renames with a new extension, ransom notes appearing across shares, shadow-copy deletion, and ESXi hosts with VMs suddenly powered off. Encryption comes late: look back for the access, staging and exfiltration that came first.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/stopransomware" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA StopRansomware</a></article>
    <article id="ransomware-as-a-service-raas" class="bad-term"><h3>Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS)</h3><p>A business model in which developers rent their ransomware, leak site and negotiation services to affiliates who carry out the intrusions, splitting each ransom. Explains why the same ransomware brand shows very different tradecraft from one victim to the next: the affiliate, not the brand, did the intrusion.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Do not scope from the brand's profile alone. The affiliate's own tradecraft (entry vector, tools, exfiltration method) is what you will find on your hosts, so let the evidence drive scope.</p></article>
    <article id="remote-access-trojan-rat" class="bad-term"><h3>Remote Access Trojan (RAT)</h3><p>Malware that gives an operator interactive remote control of a host (keylogging, screen capture, file transfer, webcam). It is often delivered as a trojan the user runs, but loaders, exploits and hands-on-keyboard operators install RATs too. Attackers also commonly skip custom RATs and abuse legitimate remote-access/RMM tools (AnyDesk, ScreenConnect, Quick Assist, NetSupport), which are signed and often allowlisted: ATT&amp;CK T1219 (Remote Access Tools), sub-technique T1219.002. See RMM Tool Abuse.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A persistent process holding a long-lived outbound connection, screen or webcam capture in use, and an operator issuing commands at human timing. Check for legitimate RMM tools installed alongside it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1219/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1219</a></article>
    <article id="rmm-tool-abuse" class="bad-term"><h3>RMM Tool Abuse</h3><p>Using legitimate remote monitoring and management or remote-access software (AnyDesk, ScreenConnect, Quick Assist, TeamViewer, NetSupport) as the attacker's remote-control channel. The binaries are signed and often allowlisted, so the signal is context: an RMM tool the organization does not use, installed by a user after a help-desk call or a phishing lure, or run as a portable executable from a user folder.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An RMM installer arriving after a phone call or help-desk contact, a remote session the IT team cannot account for, or an agent enrolled to an account or tenant that is not the organization's.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1219/002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1219.002</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-025a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-025A</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2024/05/15/threat-actors-misusing-quick-assist-in-social-engineering-attacks-leading-to-ransomware/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft (2024)</a></article>
    <article id="rootkits" class="bad-term"><h3>Rootkits</h3><p>Malware that hides its presence by subverting the OS or using kernel-level access. Often combined with other malware to maintain stealth and persistence.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Disagreement between views: a process, file, driver or connection visible in a memory image or offline scan but not from the live OS. The live host is not a reliable witness, so memory and offline analysis decide it.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/rootkit" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="scareware" class="bad-term"><h3>Scareware</h3><p>Software that tricks users into believing their device is infected, pressuring them to buy fake antivirus tools. Often a social-engineering precursor to real malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Browser pop-ups or notifications claiming infection, with a phone number or download link. Check whether the user called the number or installed anything, which turns it into a remote-access case.</p></article>
    <article id="spyware" class="bad-term"><h3>Spyware</h3><p>Malware that secretly monitors user activity: keystrokes, screen captures, camera or microphone access. Used for surveillance, espionage, or credential theft.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Unexpected processes using the camera, microphone or screen-capture APIs, and small regular uploads. On phones, MDM or mobile threat defense alerts on profiles or exploits.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/spyware" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="virus" class="bad-term"><h3>Virus</h3><p>Malware that infects other files or programs and spreads when they are run. Rare in modern intrusions compared with worms and trojans, but the word is still used loosely for any malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Many executables on a host or share changing at once with the same appended code, so their hashes change together. Rare today; most 'virus' alerts are other malware types, so read the detection details.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/virus" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
    <article id="web-shell" class="bad-term"><h3>Web Shell</h3><p>A script planted on a web server that gives the attacker remote command execution through ordinary HTTP requests; the standard foothold after edge-device and file-transfer exploitation (HAFNIUM, ProxyShell, MOVEit, SharePoint ToolShell).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The web server process (w3wp.exe, httpd, java) spawning a shell such as cmd.exe, powershell or /bin/sh.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1505/003/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1505.003</a></article>
    <article id="wiper-malware" class="bad-term bad-term--featured"><h3>Wiper Malware</h3><p>Malware designed to irreversibly delete data and destroy system functionality. Examples: NotPetya, Shamoon, WhisperGate, HermeticWiper.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Raw disk or MBR writes, mass file deletion or overwriting, and services stopped across many hosts at once, sometimes behind a fake ransom note. There is no key to buy and recovery depends on backups. Confirmed wiper activity fires the escalation tripwire: escalate at once.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1485/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK T1485</a></article>
    <article id="worm" class="bad-term"><h3>Worm</h3><p>Self-propagating malware that spreads host to host without user action, usually through a network-reachable vulnerability ('wormable' describes such a flaw, e.g. EternalBlue, BlueKeep).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> One host scanning many others on the same port (SMB 445, RDP 3389), then the same malware appearing on them. Spread is measured in minutes, so escalate immediately and let IR choose the containment.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/worm" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NIST glossary</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Ransomware lock animation */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 220 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A padlock closes over files whose contents scramble into encrypted bytes." class="bad-anim bad-anim--lock">
        {/* shackle */}
        <path d="M75 80 V60 a35 35 0 0 1 70 0 V80" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="8" fill="none" stroke-linecap="round" class="bad-anim__shackle"/>
        {/* body */}
        <rect x="55" y="80" width="110" height="90" rx="14" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        {/* keyhole */}
        <g fill="#0a0303">
          <circle cx="110" cy="115" r="9"/>
          <rect x="106" y="118" width="8" height="22" rx="2"/>
        </g>
        {/* encryption noise */}
        <g class="bad-anim__bits" fill="#fca5a5" font-size="7" opacity="0.8">
          <text x="14" y="40">0x4a3b</text>
          <text x="170" y="44">0xdead</text>
          <text x="18" y="190">0xbeef</text>
          <text x="160" y="194">0x9f1c</text>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Ransomware</h3>
      <p>Malware that encrypts a victim's files or systems and demands payment (usually in cryptocurrency) for the decryption key. Modern ransomware operators add data theft and public-leak extortion. In telemetry, the encryption sweep shows as rapid sequential file writes across the filesystem. Earlier tells include shadow-copy deletion (vssadmin, wmic, bcdedit) and backup-service kills. Watch for modern variants that encrypt VMs from the ESXi host, where endpoint agents see nothing, or encrypt only parts of each file to evade write-rate detection.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Botnet */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 280 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Many infected bots check in with a central C2 server on a regular rhythm." class="bad-anim bad-anim--botnet">
        {/* central C2 */}
        <g>
          <rect x="115" y="80" width="50" height="40" rx="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
          <text x="140" y="105" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">C2</text>
        </g>
        {/* infected bot ring */}
        <g class="bad-anim__bots">
          <circle cx="40"  cy="40"  r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--1"/>
          <circle cx="140" cy="20"  r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--2"/>
          <circle cx="240" cy="40"  r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--3"/>
          <circle cx="260" cy="100" r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--4"/>
          <circle cx="240" cy="160" r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--5"/>
          <circle cx="140" cy="180" r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--6"/>
          <circle cx="40"  cy="160" r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--7"/>
          <circle cx="20"  cy="100" r="6" class="bad-bot bad-bot--8"/>
        </g>
        {/* lines from C2 to bots */}
        <g stroke="#7f1d1d" stroke-width="1" stroke-dasharray="2,2" fill="none">
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="40"  y2="40"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="140" y2="20"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="240" y2="40"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="260" y2="100"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="240" y2="160"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="140" y2="180"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="40"  y2="160"/>
          <line x1="140" y1="100" x2="20"  y2="100"/>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Botnet</h3>
      <p>Many infected machines (bots) taking orders from a controller, either a central C2 server or peer-to-peer relays between bots. The rhythm of regular check-ins is what defenders look for. DDoS, credential stuffing, spam, and proxy abuse all ride on this primitive. Modern botnets span PCs, servers, routers, and IoT.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: Wiper */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Documents, contracts, source code, a customer database and backups are irreversibly overwritten." class="bad-anim bad-anim--wiper">
        {/* file rows */}
        <g font-size="10" fill="#fecaca">
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--1">
            <rect x="40" y="30" width="240" height="22" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d" opacity="0.7"/>
            <text x="50" y="46">📄 documents.xlsx</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--2">
            <rect x="40" y="58" width="240" height="22" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d" opacity="0.7"/>
            <text x="50" y="74">📄 contracts.docx</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--3">
            <rect x="40" y="86" width="240" height="22" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d" opacity="0.7"/>
            <text x="50" y="102">📄 source.zip</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--4">
            <rect x="40" y="114" width="240" height="22" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d" opacity="0.7"/>
            <text x="50" y="130">📄 customers.db</text>
          </g>
          <g class="bad-anim__file bad-anim__file--5">
            <rect x="40" y="142" width="240" height="22" rx="4" fill="#7f1d1d" opacity="0.7"/>
            <text x="50" y="158">📄 backups.tar</text>
          </g>
        </g>
        <text x="160" y="185" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#ef4444">↑ irreversibly overwritten</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Wiper Malware</h3>
      <p>The destructive cousin of ransomware. There is no decryption key, no negotiation, no way to buy the data back: the wiper overwrites disk structures so the drive cannot be restored, and only offline backups help. NotPetya and WhisperGate wore the ransom-note costume while destroying the underlying data. Shamoon did not bother pretending: early variants overwrote files and disk structures with an image, and later ones used random data.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     10. MALWARE FAMILIES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="families" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="m8 2 1.88 1.88"/><path d="M14.12 3.88 16 2"/><path d="M9 7.13v-1a3.003 3.003 0 1 1 6 0v1"/><path d="M12 20c-3.3 0-6-2.7-6-6v-3a4 4 0 0 1 4-4h4a4 4 0 0 1 4 4v3c0 3.3-2.7 6-6 6"/><path d="M12 20v-9"/><path d="M6.53 9C4.6 8.8 3 7.1 3 5"/><path d="M6 13H2"/><path d="M3 21c0-2.1 1.7-3.9 3.8-4"/><path d="M20.97 5c0 2.1-1.6 3.8-3.5 4"/><path d="M22 13h-4"/><path d="M17.2 17c2.1.1 3.8 1.9 3.8 4"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Malware families</h2>
      <p>Named tools and campaign malware tied to specific actors or operations. Status lines were last reviewed in October 2026; takedowns degrade these operations rather than end them, so check current reporting before you rely on one.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/uncover/threat-intelligence/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  <div class="bad-families">
    <article id="blackcat-alphv" class="bad-family"><h3>BlackCat / ALPHV</h3><p>Sophisticated ransomware-as-a-service operation, one of the first to use Rust, making detection and analysis harder. Highly customizable Windows / Linux payloads, double extortion with leak-site shaming. Successor to BlackMatter in the DarkSide lineage; ties to REvil are affiliate overlap, not direct succession. Defunct: the FBI disrupted ALPHV in December 2023, and in March 2024 the operators exit-scammed their affiliates after Change Healthcare's reported $22M ransom payment, posting a fake law-enforcement seizure notice. Its affiliates moved to other RaaS programs.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Defunct since March 2024 (exit scam).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical brand; its affiliates moved on. A BlackCat-branded note today more likely means a copycat or a reused builder, so scope by the tradecraft on your hosts.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-353a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-353A</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://krebsonsecurity.com/2024/03/blackcat-ransomware-group-implodes-after-apparent-22m-ransom-payment-by-change-healthcare/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: KrebsOnSecurity (2024)</a></article>
    <article id="cobalt-strike" class="bad-family"><h3>Cobalt Strike</h3><p>Commercial adversary-simulation framework (sold by Fortra) whose cracked copies were heavily abused by APTs, ransomware gangs and commodity malware: Beacon C2, fileless injection, lateral movement, credential harvesting. Historically delivered by loaders such as TrickBot and BazarLoader (both retired around 2022). Fortra reports an 80% drop in unauthorized copies after a 2023 court order won with Microsoft and Health-ISAC and the June 2024 Operation Morpheus takedown of 593 servers, and actors increasingly use alternatives such as Sliver. Beacon's sleep/jitter check-in pattern is still the reference beaconing signature.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Still seen, but cracked-copy abuse sharply reduced since 2024.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Default named pipes (MSSE-number-server, postex_), rundll32 or dllhost running with no command line yet making network connections, and a beacon cadence with jitter. A Beacon detection means hands-on-keyboard: treat it as an active intruder.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0154/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0154</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://therecord.media/malicious-cobalt-strike-use-down" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: The Record (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="conti" class="bad-family"><h3>Conti</h3><p>One of the most prolific ransomware groups before disbanding in 2022. It ran a centralized structure, a professional affiliate model, and human-operated intrusions, using Cobalt Strike and TrickBot for movement. Internal chat-log leaks in 2022 exposed its structure, operations, and finances. Many former members moved to BlackCat or Royal; Royal later rebranded as BlackSuit, whose infrastructure law enforcement seized in July 2025.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Defunct since 2022; successor brands also disrupted.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical. Its playbook (Cobalt Strike, AdFind, rclone, vssadmin) lives on in successor crews, so the tradecraft still matters even when the name does not.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0575/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0575</a></article>
    <article id="dridex" class="bad-family"><h3>Dridex</h3><p>Banking Trojan evolved into a modular platform for credential theft, lateral movement, and ransomware delivery. Historically spread via Office macro attachments, a vector Microsoft sharply reduced by blocking internet-sourced macros by default from 2022. Injects into browsers to harvest banking credentials. Used to deploy Locky and BitPaymer. Associated with Evil Corp, which has relied on it less since about 2020 as it moved to its own ransomware and then LockBit; treat a new Dridex sighting as notable rather than routine.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Unclear; activity low since 2023, no public takedown.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An Office or script host starting a DLL through rundll32 or regsvr32 from a user folder, then injected explorer.exe making HTTPS connections. Rare now; read a Dridex detection as a loader alert that may precede ransomware.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0384/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0384</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/unc2165-shifts-to-evade-sanctions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Mandiant (2022)</a></article>
    <article id="emotet" class="bad-family"><h3>Emotet</h3><p>Banking trojan turned modular loader botnet; for years the dominant initial-access delivery vehicle, dropping TrickBot, QakBot, and ultimately ransomware. Spread via thread-hijacked phishing with macro documents. Taken down by law enforcement in January 2021, resurfaced later that year, and was targeted again by Operation Endgame in May 2024. Its playbook still defines the loader-botnet ecosystem.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Disrupted (2021, again May 2024); sporadic since.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> An email that replies in a real past thread with a new attachment or link, then a DLL launched by rundll32 or regsvr32 from a random-named folder in AppData. Infected hosts send mail outbound, so check SMTP egress too.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0367/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0367</a></article>
    <article id="empyre-empire" class="bad-family"><h3>EmPyre / Empire</h3><p>Open-source post-exploitation framework with a Python server: PowerShell agents for Windows and Python agents for Linux/macOS (EmPyre, the former Python project, was merged into Empire 2.0). The original project was discontinued and its repository archived in January 2020; BC Security maintains the active fork, which adds C#, IronPython and Go agents. Originally a red-team tool, adopted by various actors. Encrypted C2, evasion techniques, in-memory execution. Less common in the wild than Cobalt Strike, but its presence in a compromise should be treated as a high-risk indicator.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Maintained by BC Security; original project archived 2020.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> PowerShell with a long Base64 launcher, a stager calling out on a fixed cadence, and the default Empire HTTP paths (/admin/get.php, /news.php, /login/process.php) in untuned deployments.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0363/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0363</a></article>
    <article id="icedid-bokbot" class="bad-family"><h3>IcedID (BokBot)</h3><p>Banking Trojan turned loader that dropped Cobalt Strike and ransomware, spread through malicious documents and phishing. Modular: credential theft, web injection, lateral movement, with persistence via scheduled tasks and registry keys. Was a key initial-access loader for ransomware crews. Latrodectus, likely written by the same developers, took over much of its role from late 2023, and Operation Endgame (May 2024) targeted IcedID's infrastructure.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Disrupted May 2024 (Operation Endgame); largely replaced by Latrodectus.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A script- or ISO-delivered DLL run by rundll32, a scheduled task for persistence, and the main module downloaded as a fake GZIP file. Mostly historical; Latrodectus fills the same role.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0483/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0483</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.europol.europa.eu/media-press/newsroom/news/largest-ever-operation-against-botnets-hits-dropper-malware-ecosystem" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Europol (2024)</a></article>
    <article id="lumma-stealer" class="bad-family"><h3>Lumma Stealer</h3><p>The dominant malware-as-a-service infostealer of the mid-2020s, inheriting RedLine's and Raccoon's market. Harvests browser credentials, cookies, session tokens, and crypto wallets; the resulting logs are sold in bulk and seed downstream intrusions, including the 2024 Snowflake customer breaches. Distributed via malvertising, cracked software, and fake-CAPTCHA ClickFix lures. A May 2025 action led by Microsoft and the U.S. DOJ took down or blocked about 2,300 Lumma domains, though operators attempted to rebuild.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Disrupted May 2025; operators have attempted to rebuild.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A user who pasted a ClickFix command or ran a cracked installer, then browser credential and cookie stores read by a non-browser process, then an upload. Treat every credential and session on that device as exposed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa25-141b" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA25-141B</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/05/21/lumma-stealer-breaking-down-the-delivery-techniques-and-capabilities-of-a-prolific-infostealer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft (2025)</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/justice-department-seizes-domains-behind-major-information-stealing-malware-operation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: U.S. DOJ (2025)</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/unc5537-snowflake-data-theft-extortion" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Mandiant UNC5537</a></article>
    <article id="maze" class="bad-family"><h3>Maze</h3><p>Pioneered double extortion (encrypting systems and stealing data, then threatening to leak it) in late 2019, running a RaaS model with a public leak blog. It exploited RDP, phishing, and known vulnerabilities, and used Cobalt Strike and Mimikatz internally. Announced retirement in late 2020, but affiliates continued under the Egregor banner (the code-sharing Sekhmet family had operated in parallel).</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Retired late 2020.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical; its double-extortion model is now standard. Encryption plus a leak-site threat means scoping the exfiltration from the first hour.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0449/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0449</a></article>
    <article id="mimikatz" class="bad-family"><h3>Mimikatz</h3><p>The canonical credential-theft tool: reads LSASS (Local Security Authority Subsystem Service) memory to extract plaintext passwords, NTLM hashes, and Kerberos tickets, and implements pass-the-hash, pass-the-ticket, golden and silver tickets. Written by Benjamin Delpy as a proof of concept; now embedded in nearly every intrusion toolchain and re-implemented inside countless loaders and C2 frameworks. EDR watches lsass access patterns specifically because of it, and Credential Guard and LSA protection, now default-on for many Windows 11 configurations, block its classic LSASS read.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Active open-source tool, still maintained and widely reimplemented.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> lsass.exe opened by a non-system process (Sysmon event 10), command lines with sekurlsa::, lsadump:: or kerberos::, and Defender or AMSI hits on renamed builds. A Mimikatz alert means the credentials cached on that host are exposed: scope which accounts had logged on there.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0002/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0002</a></article>
    <article id="qakbot-qbot" class="bad-family"><h3>Qakbot (Qbot)</h3><p>Multifunctional banking Trojan and malware dropper that evolved into a full-featured loader, spread through email-thread hijacking, with persistence, network discovery, credential harvesting, and encrypted C2. A cornerstone of many enterprise compromises. A U.S.-led multinational operation in August 2023 redirected the botnet to FBI-controlled servers and pushed an uninstaller to more than 700,000 infected computers. Its operators kept working (DOJ says they were still deploying ransomware in January 2025), and in May 2025 the U.S. indicted the alleged leader, Rustam Gallyamov, as part of Operation Endgame.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Disrupted August 2023; leader indicted May 2025.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Thread-hijacked email, a script or an ISO, OneNote or HTML attachment, a DLL run by rundll32 or regsvr32, then injected processes running network discovery. Mostly historical since the 2023 takedown; similar chains now deliver other loaders.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0650/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0650</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/leader-qakbot-malware-conspiracy-indicted-involvement-global-ransomware-scheme" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: U.S. DOJ (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="redline" class="bad-family"><h3>RedLine</h3><p>Infostealer sold on underground forums. Harvests credentials, browser data, cryptocurrency wallets, and system information. Distributed via cracked software, malvertising, phishing, and fake installers. SOAP-based C2 (a .NET WCF service) over HTTP/S. Customizable and affordable; popular among low-skill actors and initial-access brokers. Infrastructure seized in the international Operation Magnus takedown (October 2024).</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Disrupted October 2024 (Operation Magnus).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A cracked-software or fake-installer download, a .NET process reading browser stores, and outbound traffic to a SOAP endpoint on a non-standard port. Treat every credential and session on that device as exposed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.redline_stealer" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Malpedia</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S1240/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S1240</a></article>
    <article id="ryuk" class="bad-family"><h3>Ryuk</h3><p>High-impact ransomware operated by Wizard Spider, typically delivered via TrickBot or BazarLoader. It used AES/RSA file encryption against hospitals, municipalities and critical infrastructure, encrypted fast, and killed backup and recovery processes. Its operators are believed to overlap heavily with the subsequent Conti operation, generally treated as the successor brand.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Superseded by Conti (2021); inactive.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical. The Ryuk-era chain (TrickBot or BazarLoader, Cobalt Strike, then mass deployment through PsExec or Group Policy) is still the template for human-operated ransomware: the encryption comes last.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0446/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0446</a></article>
    <article id="shamoon" class="bad-family"><h3>Shamoon</h3><p>Destructive wiper associated with Iranian state-sponsored actors. Infamous for the 2012 attack on Saudi Aramco that wiped roughly 30,000 machines; returned in 2016-2017 ("Shamoon 2") against other Saudi organizations, including the General Authority of Civil Aviation. It overwrites the MBR with provocative imagery and renders devices inoperable, spreading via stolen credentials and admin shares. Its goal is destruction, not financial gain.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Historical (waves in 2012, 2016-2017 and 2018).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical, but the pattern recurs: reused credentials reaching many hosts over admin shares, then a wipe scheduled for one moment, often a weekend or holiday. Raw-disk driver loads and MBR writes are the last warning.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0140/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0140</a></article>
    <article id="trickbot" class="bad-family"><h3>TrickBot</h3><p>Began as a banking Trojan and evolved into a modular enterprise-scale framework for credential theft, recon, and ransomware delivery. It often arrived via Emotet and then provided the foothold for Ryuk or Conti, with decentralized infrastructure, encrypted comms, and plugins for SMB spread and AD enumeration. A coordinated takedown in October 2020 only dented it; the operators wound the botnet down by 2022 as work shifted into Conti.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Inactive since 2022.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical. Its successors still follow its chain: loader, Active Directory enumeration, then Cobalt Strike and ransomware within days.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0266/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0266</a></article>
    <article id="vidar" class="bad-family"><h3>Vidar</h3><p>Widely distributed infostealer harvesting credentials, browser data, cookies, cryptocurrency wallets, and more. Delivered via phishing, malvertising and fake installers, with HTTP POST C2 and encrypted exfiltration. Customizable and available as malware-as-a-service; often bundled with or mistaken for RedLine / Raccoon. Vidar 2.0 (October 2025) was a ground-up rewrite.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Active (Vidar 2.0, October 2025).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A fake installer or malvertised download, a non-browser process reading browser and wallet stores, and C2 addresses fetched from a social-media or gaming profile (a dead drop resolver). Treat every credential and session on that device as exposed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.vidar" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Malpedia</a></article>
    <article id="whispergate" class="bad-family"><h3>WhisperGate</h3><p>Destructive malware deployed against Ukraine in January 2022. It masquerades as ransomware but irreversibly corrupts the MBR and deletes system files in two stages: first it corrupts the bootloader, then it drops a wiper. There is no recovery mechanism: the goal was disruption, not profit. Attributed by Microsoft to Cadet Blizzard; in 2024 the U.S. indicted officers of GRU Unit 29155 for the operation (see CISA AA24-249A). Echoed NotPetya tactics.</p><p class="bad-status">Status (Oct 2026): Historical (2022).</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical. The lesson stands: a ransom note with no working payment path and an overwritten MBR is a wiper. Escalate it as destructive activity, not extortion.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0689/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S0689</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa24-249a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA24-249A</a></article>
  </div>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     11. THREAT ACTOR PROFILES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="actors" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M16 21v-2a4 4 0 0 0-4-4H6a4 4 0 0 0-4 4v2"/><circle cx="9" cy="7" r="4"/><path d="M22 21v-2a4 4 0 0 0-3-3.87"/><path d="M16 3.13a4 4 0 0 1 0 7.75"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Threat actor profiles</h2>
      <p>Named groups and individuals known for malicious cyber activity. Their tradecraft, motivations, and historical campaigns shape modern triage.</p>
      <p>One group, many names: every vendor names actors from its own telemetry, so the same cluster carries a Mandiant APT number (APT29), a CrowdStrike animal (Cozy Bear), a Microsoft weather family (Midnight Blizzard), and a Proofpoint TA number. Under each card, a chip gives the actor's motivation (nation-state or financially motivated) and attributed country, followed by the main vendor names, including Google Threat Intelligence's 2026 cryptonyms where they exist. Microsoft publishes its full mapping on its <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/defender-xdr/microsoft-threat-actor-naming" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">threat actor naming page</a> and as a <a href="https://github.com/microsoft/mstic/blob/master/PublicFeeds/ThreatActorNaming/MicrosoftMapping.json" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">JSON feed</a>. Treat each name as one vendor's view of overlapping activity, not a precise identity. Actor status changes fast; these cards were last reviewed in October 2026.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/uncover/threat-intelligence/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  <div class="bad-actors">
    <article id="akira" class="bad-actor"><h3>Akira <span>VPNs without MFA</span></h3><p>Ransomware operation active since March 2023 that hits small and mid-sized organizations across most sectors. CISA's advisory, updated in November 2025, lists its usual ways in: VPNs without MFA, and exploitation of Cisco ASA and FTD (CVE-2020-3259, CVE-2023-20269), SonicWall (CVE-2024-40766) and Veeam backup servers (CVE-2023-27532, CVE-2024-40711). It encrypts Windows, Linux and VMware ESXi hosts, added Nutanix AHV in 2025, and practices double extortion.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A VPN sign-in without MFA from an unfamiliar address, often a hosting provider, may be the only early warning. Remote-access tools, credential theft from Veeam backup servers and access to ESXi hosts follow before encryption.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · RaaS</span> ATT&amp;CK: G1024 · CrowdStrike: Punk Spider · Microsoft: no actor name for the brand (affiliates tracked separately)</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa24-109a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA24-109A</a></article>
    <article id="apt10" class="bad-actor"><h3>APT10 <span>Cloud Hopper</span></h3><p>Chinese state-sponsored group associated with global IP theft. Its Cloud Hopper campaign targeted managed service providers (MSPs) to compromise downstream customers. It has focused on healthcare, defense, aerospace and technology, using RedLeaves, custom loaders, spear-phishing, credential theft, and cloud-service abuse aligned with China's economic and military goals.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical as a name; the pattern it set is current. Activity that arrives through a managed service provider's accounts or remote tools is not trusted activity: check whether the provider expected that session.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · China</span> Microsoft: Purple Typhoon · CrowdStrike: Stone Panda</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0045/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0045</a></article>
    <article id="apt28" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="Fancy Bear; Forest Blizzard"><h3>APT28 <span>Fancy Bear / Forest Blizzard</span></h3><p>Linked to Russia's GRU and known for aggressive operations: credential harvesting, malware deployment, and destructive attacks against political and military targets. Tied to the 2016 DNC breach and many NATO-related campaigns. It uses zero-days and weaponized Office documents, and is noisier and more direct than APT29; a primary actor in Russian hybrid warfare.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Password spraying and credential phishing against webmail and VPN, exploitation of edge devices and of Outlook (CVE-2023-23397 leaked NTLM hashes through a calendar reminder), and outbound NTLM authentication to external hosts. Sign-ins relayed through compromised small-office routers are common.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Russia (GRU)</span> Microsoft: Forest Blizzard · GTIG: LAKE RELIC · CrowdStrike: Fancy Bear</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0007/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0007</a></article>
    <article id="apt29" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="Cozy Bear; Midnight Blizzard; UNC2452; NOBELIUM"><h3>APT29 <span>Cozy Bear / Midnight Blizzard / UNC2452 / NOBELIUM</span></h3><p>Sophisticated espionage group associated with Russia's SVR, built for stealth and long-term access. It targets governments, think tanks, healthcare and vaccine researchers. It is the group behind the SolarWinds SUNBURST compromise (tracked by Mandiant as UNC2452 until it was merged into APT29 in April 2022). Custom malware, living-off-the-land and well-crafted spear-phishing let its operations go undetected for months. As Midnight Blizzard (Microsoft's name), it breached Microsoft's own corporate email in 2023-2024 by password-spraying a legacy test tenant.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Identity-plane tradecraft: password spraying from residential proxies against legacy or test tenants, OAuth applications granted broad mail access, device code and other token phishing, and malicious RDP files sent by email (October 2024). Look in identity and application audit logs, not only on endpoints.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Russia (SVR)</span> Microsoft: Midnight Blizzard (formerly NOBELIUM) · GTIG: ICE RELIC · CrowdStrike: Cozy Bear</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0016/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0016</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/unc2452-merged-into-apt29" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Mandiant (2022)</a><span id="unc2452" class="bad-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span></article>
    <article id="apt33" class="bad-actor"><h3>APT33 <span>Iran / aviation + energy</span></h3><p>Iranian government-linked group targeting aerospace, energy, and critical infrastructure. It mixes espionage with destructive tooling: ATT&amp;CK links it to the StoneDrill wiper and, tentatively, to the 2019 DEADWOOD wiper (since adopted by Agrius), and industry reporting has suspected ties to later Shamoon waves. Its phishing has used fake aviation job postings. As Peach Sandstorm (Microsoft), it ran password-spray campaigns against thousands of organizations from 2023 and deployed the custom Tickler backdoor in 2024.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> High-volume password spraying across many tenants. One successful sign-in among thousands of failures is the event that matters, especially if new Azure subscriptions or virtual machines appear in the tenant afterward.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Iran</span> Microsoft: Peach Sandstorm · GTIG: BLEAK ION · CrowdStrike: Refined Kitten</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0064/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0064</a></article>
    <article id="charming-kitten" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="APT35; Mint Sandstorm; Magic Hound"><h3>Charming Kitten <span>APT35 / Mint Sandstorm / Magic Hound</span></h3><p>Iranian group conducting espionage against academics, human rights activists, and government entities through phishing, fake social personas and credential harvesting. It is known for impersonating journalists and think tanks, has supported Iran's surveillance and foreign intelligence collection for over a decade, and is still active.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Long, friendly email or messaging conversations, often from someone posing as a journalist, researcher or event organizer, that end with a credential-harvesting link or a malicious file. The early messages carry no payload, so user reports matter.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Iran</span> Microsoft: Mint Sandstorm (formerly Phosphorus) · GTIG: RICH ION · ATT&amp;CK: Magic Hound</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0059/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0059</a></article>
    <article id="cl0p" class="bad-actor"><h3>Cl0p <span>MOVEit / mass exploitation</span></h3><p>Russian-speaking extortion group (TA505/FIN11-adjacent) that industrialized mass exploitation of file-transfer software: Accellion FTA (2020-21), GoAnywhere MFT (2023), MOVEit Transfer (2023), which alone touched 2,700+ organizations, and Cleo Harmony/VLTrader/LexiCom (2024). In late 2025 an extortion wave under the Cl0p brand followed zero-day exploitation of Oracle E-Business Suite (CVE-2025-61882), though Google/Mandiant had not formally attributed the intrusions. Cl0p began as ransomware in 2019, but these campaigns skip encryption in favor of pure data-theft extortion at scale: one zero-day monetized across thousands of victims at once.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Exploitation of an internet-facing file-transfer server: web shells or new admin accounts on MOVEit, GoAnywhere or Cleo hosts, and large downloads from them. The first sign many victims saw was the extortion email, so check these servers the day an advisory lands.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Russian-speaking</span> Microsoft: Lace Tempest (per Microsoft's MOVEit reporting) · Mandiant: FIN11 overlap</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-158a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-158A</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/oracle-ebusiness-suite-zero-day-exploitation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: GTIG (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="dragonforce" class="bad-actor"><h3>DragonForce <span>ransomware cartel</span></h3><p>Ransomware operation that appeared in late 2023 with encryptors built from leaked LockBit and Conti code. In March 2025 it rebranded as a "cartel" that lets affiliates run attacks under their own brands on its infrastructure. Its encryptor was used in the April 2025 Marks &amp; Spencer attack, where Scattered Spider carried out the intrusion, and it claimed the Co-op attack the same month.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The brand on the ransom note may say little about who was inside. Work the intrusion evidence (help-desk resets, remote-access tools, ESXi access), not the note.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · RaaS</span> Microsoft and ATT&amp;CK: no dedicated name</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/deep-dive-into-dragonforce-ransomware-and-its-scattered-spider-connection/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: BleepingComputer (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="evil-corp" class="bad-actor"><h3>Evil Corp <span>Dridex / WastedLocker</span></h3><p>Russian cybercrime group behind the Dridex banking trojan and BitPaymer / WastedLocker ransomware, which stole hundreds of millions from financial institutions worldwide. U.S. Treasury sanctioned the group and its alleged leader Maksim Yakubets in 2019, and it has since rotated malware names to evade sanctions and tracking. In October 2024, coordinated UK/US/Australian sanctions detailed the group's ties to Russian intelligence (FSB) and identified senior member Aleksandr Ryzhenkov as a LockBit affiliate.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Fake browser updates (SocGholish) leading to Cobalt Strike, then ransomware under a rotating name. Before any ransom payment, legal must check the strain and the actor against sanctions lists: a payment to a sanctioned group may itself violate sanctions.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Russia</span> Microsoft: Manatee Tempest · CrowdStrike: Indrik Spider</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0119/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0119</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/jy2623" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: U.S. Treasury (2024)</a></article>
    <article id="fin7" class="bad-actor"><h3>FIN7 <span>Carbanak / Bateleur</span></h3><p>Well-organized cybercrime group conducting large-scale intrusions against banks, POS systems, and hospitality: financially motivated but operationally on par with nation-state actors. Used Carbanak and Bateleur malware (ATT&amp;CK tracks the Carbanak group separately), phishing, backdoors and supply-chain compromise. Recruited unwitting IT staff through fake security firms (Combi Security before the 2018 arrests, Bastion Secure afterward), was later linked to REvil, DarkSide and other ransomware operations, and sold the AvNeutralizer EDR-killer to other ransomware crews.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Malicious ads and look-alike download sites delivering fake installers for popular software (including MSIX packages), PowerShell loaders, and EDR-killer tools before ransomware. A signed installer from an unexpected publisher followed by PowerShell is worth a look.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Eastern Europe-based</span> Microsoft: Sangria Tempest (per Microsoft reporting) · GTIG: WILD COMET · CrowdStrike: Carbon Spider</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0046/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0046</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.fbi.gov/contact-us/field-offices/seattle/news/stories/how-cyber-crime-group-fin7-attacked-and-stole-data-from-hundreds-of-us-companies" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: FBI (2018)</a></article>
    <article id="hafnium" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="Silk Typhoon"><h3>HAFNIUM <span>Silk Typhoon / Exchange zero-days</span></h3><p>Suspected Chinese state-sponsored group, widely known for exploiting the ProxyLogon Microsoft Exchange zero-days in early 2021, followed by web-shell deployment and data theft from education, law firms, NGOs, and defense contractors. Microsoft now tracks it as Silk Typhoon; in 2025 it pivoted to abusing IT providers' remote-management and cloud tooling. An alleged member was arrested in Italy in July 2025 and extradited to the U.S. in 2026.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Web shells and new child processes under internet-facing servers (Exchange, SharePoint, edge devices), and, in its current form, stolen API keys and remote-management access at IT providers used to reach their customers' cloud tenants.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · China</span> Microsoft: Silk Typhoon (formerly HAFNIUM)</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0125/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0125</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/03/05/silk-typhoon-targeting-it-supply-chain/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="lapsus" class="bad-actor"><h3>LAPSUS$ <span>Okta, Microsoft, Nvidia</span></h3><p>Loosely organized group known for extortion, data leaks, and high-profile breaches. It got in through SIM swapping, credential theft and insider recruitment rather than malware, and courted media attention with public leaks. Members were believed to be primarily teenagers, and UK arrests and convictions followed. It favored disruption and publicity over stealth, and the name resurfaced in 2025 alongside Scattered Spider and ShinyHunters.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> SIM swaps and help-desk calls that reset MFA, MFA-fatigue prompts, and bought or insider-supplied credentials, followed by access to source code, ticketing and chat systems. A help-desk MFA reset followed by a sign-in from a new device deserves a look.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · UK/Brazil-based</span> Microsoft: Strawberry Tempest · CrowdStrike: Slippy Spider</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G1004/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G1004</a></article>
    <article id="lazarus-group" class="bad-actor"><h3>Lazarus Group <span>North Korea / RGB</span></h3><p>Umbrella term for several North Korean operations under the Reconnaissance General Bureau, spanning espionage, sabotage, and financially motivated attacks: Sony Pictures, WannaCry, bank and crypto-exchange theft. It blends APT tradecraft with cybercrime to fund the regime. Its TraderTraitor cluster (Microsoft: Jade Sleet) stole about $1.5B from the Bybit exchange in February 2025, and a sister cluster (Jasper Sleet) places fake remote IT workers inside Western companies, now a routine hiring and insider-threat triage scenario.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Fake job offers and coding tests that deliver malware to developers and cryptocurrency staff, trojanized npm or PyPI packages, and, for the IT-worker scheme, a new hire whose remote access comes from a laptop farm or a remote-desktop tool. See DPRK Remote IT Workers.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · North Korea</span> Microsoft: Diamond Sleet; related: Jade Sleet, Sapphire Sleet, Jasper Sleet · CrowdStrike: Labyrinth Chollima</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0032/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0032</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.ic3.gov/PSA/2025/PSA250226" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: FBI PSA (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="lockbit" class="bad-actor"><h3>LockBit <span>RaaS at scale</span></h3><p>The most deployed ransomware-as-a-service operation of 2022-2023, responsible for thousands of victims across every sector. Ran a professionalized affiliate program with a slick leak site, bug bounties, and payment discipline. Operation Cronos seized its infrastructure in February 2024, and in May 2024 the same taskforce named and sanctioned its leader, Dmitry Khoroshev. The brand nonetheless returned as LockBit 5.0 in September 2025, with Windows, Linux and ESXi builds: takedowns degrade RaaS programs rather than end them.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Affiliates vary, so the way in does too: exposed RDP or VPN, edge-device exploits, stolen credentials. A late but clear warning is a new or edited Group Policy object that disables Defender or creates scheduled tasks across the domain to push the encryptor.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · RaaS</span> CrowdStrike: Bitwise Spider · Status (Oct 2026): active again as LockBit 5.0</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-165a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-165A</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://blog.checkpoint.com/research/lockbit-returns-and-it-already-has-victims/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Check Point (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="play" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="Playcrypt"><h3>Play <span>Playcrypt / closed group</span></h3><p>Ransomware group active since June 2022 that CISA and its partners describe as presumed to be a closed group rather than an open RaaS program; by May 2025 about 900 organizations had been affected. Ways in have included FortiOS flaws (CVE-2018-13379, CVE-2020-12812), Exchange ProxyNotShell, exposed RDP and VPN, and in 2025 SimpleHelp remote-support software (CVE-2024-57727). It uses intermittent encryption (encrypting only parts of each file, for speed) and has an ESXi variant.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> FortiOS or remote-support exploits and exposed RDP for entry, AdFind and the Grixba scanner for discovery, WinRAR for staging and WinSCP for exfiltration, then intermittent encryption. Grixba or AdFind on a server with no admin reason is a clear warning.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Closed group</span> ATT&amp;CK: G1040 · Microsoft: no actor name for the brand (affiliates tracked separately)</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-352a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-352A</a></article>
    <article id="qilin" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="Agenda"><h3>Qilin <span>Agenda / healthcare disruption</span></h3><p>Ransomware-as-a-service operation, also called Agenda, active since at least 2022, with Go and later Rust encryptors for Windows, Linux and VMware ESXi. Its June 2024 attack on Synnovis, a pathology provider to London NHS hospitals, halted blood testing and forced postponed operations and appointments. Several trackers ranked it the most active ransomware brand of 2025, helped by affiliates arriving from disrupted operations.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Stolen VPN or remote-access credentials, often without MFA, then Group Policy or ESXi access to deploy the encryptor. One 2024 intrusion used a Group Policy logon script to harvest passwords saved in Chrome across the domain. Hypervisor sign-ins outside change windows are the tell.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · RaaS</span> ATT&amp;CK: S1242 (software) · Microsoft: no actor name for the brand (affiliates tracked separately)</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S1242/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK S1242</a></article>
    <article id="salt-typhoon" class="bad-actor"><h3>Salt Typhoon <span>telecom espionage</span></h3><p>Chinese state-sponsored group behind the 2024 intrusions into major U.S. telecommunications providers, where it reached lawful-intercept systems and the call records of political figures. It gets in through edge routers and other network devices and alters router configurations to stay. The August 2025 joint advisory AA25-239A, co-signed by agencies from over a dozen countries, describes the same activity against telecom, government, transportation, lodging and military networks worldwide.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> The evidence lives in network-device configurations and logs, not on endpoints: unexpected configuration changes, new local accounts, GRE tunnels or packet-capture settings on routers, and SSH or management access from unfamiliar addresses.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · China</span> Microsoft: Salt Typhoon · CrowdStrike: Operator Panda · also tracked as GhostEmperor and RedMike</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa25-239a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA25-239A</a></article>
    <article id="sandworm" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="APT44"><h3>Sandworm <span>APT44 / GRU / destructive ops</span></h3><p>Destructive GRU unit behind the 2015 / 2016 Ukraine power grid attacks (BlackEnergy, Industroyer) and the global NotPetya wiper. Its tools include VPNFilter, KillDisk and Cyclops Blink. It specializes in industrial control system and operational technology (ICS/OT) disruption, hybrid warfare, and psychological operations aligned with Russian military objectives.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Destructive activity launched through the victim's own management tools: Group Policy or scheduled tasks pushing a wiper, and changes to OT or SCADA systems. Confirmed wiper activity fires the escalation tripwire.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Russia (GRU)</span> Microsoft: Seashell Blizzard · GTIG: SANDWORM RELIC (formerly APT44) · CrowdStrike: Voodoo Bear</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0034/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0034</a></article>
    <article id="scattered-spider" class="bad-actor" data-aliases="UNC3944; Octo Tempest"><h3>Scattered Spider <span>UNC3944 / Octo Tempest</span></h3><p>Native-English-speaking group behind the 2023 MGM and Caesars intrusions and the defining identity-attack playbook: help-desk social engineering, SIM swapping, MFA fatigue, AiTM phishing kits, then abuse of the victim's own identity provider (IdP) and cloud tooling. It overlaps with the loose "The Com" ecosystem and later partnered with RaaS operations for encryption. In April 2025 it hit UK retail (M&amp;S, Co-op, Harrods); at M&amp;S, DragonForce's encryptor was deployed on VMware ESXi hosts. UK arrests followed in July 2025. U.S. prosecutors charged Thalha Jubair over at least 120 intrusions and $115M in ransoms, and in July 2026 Jubair and Owen Flowers were sentenced in the UK for the 2024 Transport for London hack. The NCA says the action effectively halted the group, but its playbook (help-desk resets, MFA re-registration, hypervisor encryption) is widely copied. The actor profile most relevant to single sign-on (SSO) era triage.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A help-desk call that resets a password or MFA method, followed within minutes by a sign-in from a new device, a new MFA registration, remote-access tools, and access to the identity provider, vCenter or ESXi. Verify the caller out of band before the reset; after it, check the account's new devices and methods.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · UK/US-based</span> Microsoft: Octo Tempest · Mandiant: UNC3944 · CrowdStrike: Scattered Spider</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G1015/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G1015</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.nationalcrimeagency.gov.uk/news/two-sentenced-for-hacking-transport-for-london-in-uk-s-biggest-ever-cyber-crime-case" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: NCA (2026)</a></article>
    <article id="shinyhunters" class="bad-actor"><h3>ShinyHunters <span>data-theft extortion</span></h3><p>Financially motivated crew that has stolen and sold or leaked large databases since 2020. Public reporting links it to the 2024 Snowflake customer-account thefts and, in 2025, to voice-phishing calls that talked staff into authorizing a malicious connected app in Salesforce, followed by extortion. In August 2025 it surfaced with Scattered Spider and LAPSUS$ remnants under the "Scattered LAPSUS$ Hunters" banner. There is no encryption: the stolen data is the leverage, so the case is a breach investigation from the first hour.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A voice-phishing call followed by a new connected app or OAuth authorization in Salesforce or another SaaS platform, then bulk API exports. SaaS audit logs (connected-app installs, API query volume) carry it; endpoints see nothing.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · data-theft extortion</span> GTIG: UNC6040 (voice phishing), UNC6240 (extortion)</p></article>
    <article id="silverterrier" class="bad-actor"><h3>SilverTerrier <span>Nigerian BEC</span></h3><p>Collective term for Nigerian actors conducting Business Email Compromise fraud. Less technically advanced than APTs but highly profitable, they use phishing, credential theft and social engineering to divert funds from corporate communications. Many operate openly on social media, and hundreds of arrests have not stopped the activity.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> A mailbox compromise followed by inbox rules that hide replies, a look-alike domain in a payment thread, and a request to change bank details. Verify any payment change by calling a number you already hold.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Nigeria</span> ATT&amp;CK: G0083</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0083/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0083</a></article>
    <article id="storm-2372" class="bad-actor"><h3>Storm-2372 <span>device code phishing</span></h3><p>Cluster Microsoft has tracked since August 2024 running device code phishing against government, NGO, IT, defense, telecom, health, higher-education and energy organizations. Operators build rapport over WhatsApp, Signal or Microsoft Teams, then send a fake Teams meeting invite that leads the target to enter an attacker-generated device code, handing over tokens. From February 2025 it used the Microsoft Authentication Broker client ID to register devices and obtain Primary Refresh Tokens. Microsoft assesses with moderate confidence that it aligns with Russian state interests, and a later update to its report places it as an initial-access sub-cluster of Midnight Blizzard.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Device code sign-ins (authentication protocol "deviceCode" in Entra ID sign-in logs) by users who never use device-code apps, often right after a meeting invite from a new contact, then device registration through the Authentication Broker client.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Russia-aligned</span> Microsoft: Storm-2372</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/02/13/storm-2372-conducts-device-code-phishing-campaign/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="ta505" class="bad-actor"><h3>TA505 <span>large-scale malware ops</span></h3><p>Prolific cybercriminal group distributing banking trojans, ransomware, and RATs globally through massive phishing waves delivering Dridex, FlawedAmmyy and Locky. It often acts as an initial-access broker for other groups and frequently shifts tactics, payloads, and infrastructure: an example of the malware-as-a-service model.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Mostly historical. Mass phishing with malicious attachments that load a downloader, then remote-access tools. Its later file-transfer extortion campaigns are covered under Cl0p.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Russian-speaking</span> Microsoft: Spandex Tempest · CrowdStrike: Graceful Spider</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0092/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0092</a></article>
    <article id="turla" class="bad-actor"><h3>Turla <span>FSB / long-running espionage</span></h3><p>Long-running Russian cyber-espionage group linked to the FSB that targets governments, embassies and militaries. Custom implants include Snake (whose global network the FBI disabled in Operation MEDUSA, May 2023), Carbon, and Kazuar. It hijacks other actors' infrastructure to obscure attribution: Microsoft and Lumen reported in December 2024 that it had been running operations from a Pakistani group's (Storm-0156) hijacked C2 servers since late 2022. Watering holes, poisoned updates and compromised email systems round out tradecraft built for longevity and covertness.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Rarely seen directly. The signs are long-lived, low-volume implants on servers, C2 through compromised websites or another actor's infrastructure, and mail-server backdoors that take commands by email. Record the behavior and leave attribution to threat intelligence.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · Russia (FSB)</span> Microsoft: Secret Blizzard · GTIG: TURLA RELIC · CrowdStrike: Venomous Bear</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0010/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0010</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-129a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-129A</a></article>
    <article id="volt-typhoon" class="bad-actor"><h3>Volt Typhoon <span>critical-infrastructure prepositioning</span></h3><p>Chinese state-sponsored group that pre-positions inside U.S. critical infrastructure (energy, water, communications, transportation) for potential disruptive action rather than day-to-day espionage. Its defining tradecraft is living off the land: built-in tools, valid accounts, and hands-on-keyboard operation with almost no malware, which defeats IOC-driven detection. It relayed traffic through compromised SOHO routers (the KV botnet, disrupted by the FBI in January 2024). Disclosed May 2023 by Microsoft and CISA; the campaign itself is covered under notable campaigns below.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Built-in tools used with valid accounts: ntdsutil or vssadmin copying ntds.dit, netsh port proxies, WMI and PowerShell discovery, and sign-ins relayed through small-office routers. There is no malware to catch, so baseline administrative activity.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Nation-state · China</span> Microsoft: Volt Typhoon · CrowdStrike: Vanguard Panda</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G1017/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G1017</a></article>
    <article id="wizard-spider" class="bad-actor"><h3>Wizard Spider <span>Ryuk / Conti / TrickBot</span></h3><p>Russia-based cybercriminal group that ran Ryuk, Conti, and TrickBot and extorted hundreds of millions from hospitals, municipalities and corporations. It operated within a larger ecosystem of initial access brokers and malware developers, with fast lateral movement, data theft and double extortion, usually after TrickBot or BazarLoader infections. That era ended in 2022, when Conti shut down and TrickBot and BazarLoader went quiet; members dispersed into successor ransomware brands.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage signal:</strong> Historical as a brand; the sequence still recurs. A loader infection (TrickBot, BazarLoader), Cobalt Strike within hours, domain discovery with AdFind, then encryption across the domain. The loader on the first host is the earliest and cheapest point to stop it.</p><p class="bad-actor__meta"><span class="bad-chip">Financially motivated · Russia</span> Microsoft: Periwinkle Tempest · CrowdStrike: Wizard Spider · Status (Oct 2026): defunct as a brand since 2022</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0102/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: ATT&CK G0102</a></article>
  </div>
</section>

{/* ============================================================
     12. NOTABLE CAMPAIGNS AND BREACHES
     ============================================================ */}
<section id="campaigns" class="bad-section">
  <header class="bad-section__head">
    <span class="bad-section__ico" aria-hidden="true"><svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="1.75" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round"><path d="M15 12h-5"/><path d="M15 8h-5"/><path d="M19 17V5a2 2 0 0 0-2-2H4"/><path d="M8 21h12a2 2 0 0 0 2-2v-1a1 1 0 0 0-1-1H11a1 1 0 0 0-1 1v1a2 2 0 1 1-4 0V5a2 2 0 1 0-4 0v2a1 1 0 0 0 1 1h3"/></svg></span>
    <div>
      <h2>Notable campaigns and breaches</h2>
      <p>High-impact, widely analyzed intrusions and operations that shaped modern security thinking. Campaign cards were last reviewed in October 2026.</p>
      <p class="bad-section__phases">Vocabulary for: <a href="/docs/uncover/threat-intelligence/">Uncover</a></p>
    </div>
  </header>

  {/* Campaign grid */}
  <div class="bad-campaigns">
