    <article id="cambridge-analytica" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2018"><h3>Cambridge Analytica</h3><p>Political consulting firm that improperly harvested data from 87 million Facebook users via a quiz app; the scandal broke in 2018. Psychological profiling and micro-targeted ads aimed at influencing votes, most prominently the 2016 U.S. election (the UK ICO's investigation later concluded its role in Brexit had been overstated). Not a traditional cyberattack, but it exposed serious flaws in data privacy and third-party app oversight and triggered global debate about surveillance capitalism and election interference. Direct consequences included a record $5 billion FTC penalty against Facebook (July 2019) and the UK ICO's maximum £500,000 fine under pre-GDPR law. GDPR itself was adopted in 2016 and became applicable on May 25, 2018, about ten weeks after the story broke.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> No intrusion at all: access the platform granted was used far beyond its purpose. Third-party apps and API consumers holding your users' data deserve the scrutiny you give attackers.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2019/07/ftc-imposes-5-billion-penalty-sweeping-new-privacy-restrictions-facebook" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: US FTC (2019)</a></article>
    <article id="change-healthcare" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2024"><h3>Change Healthcare</h3><p>February 2024 ransomware attack on the largest U.S. health-claims clearinghouse by an ALPHV/BlackCat affiliate. Entry came through a Citrix remote-access portal protected by a stolen password and no MFA. Claims processing and pharmacy payments stalled nationwide for weeks; the company paid a reported $22M ransom, and the data of roughly 190 million people was exposed, the largest healthcare breach on record. ALPHV then exit-scammed the affiliate.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> One remote-access portal without MFA was enough. A password-only sign-in to an internet-facing portal is worth an alert, and an outage at a single critical vendor is your incident too.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.hhs.gov/hipaa/for-professionals/special-topics/change-healthcare-cybersecurity-incident-frequently-asked-questions/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: HHS OCR</a></article>
    <article id="cloud-hopper" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2017"><h3>Cloud Hopper</h3><p>Widespread espionage campaign by China's APT10 targeting MSPs globally. Compromising MSPs gave attackers stealthy access to customer networks across aerospace, finance, manufacturing, and healthcare. Credential theft, custom malware, and RATs. Victims often unaware due to the trusted MSP position. A textbook example of supply-chain compromise for espionage. The actor is profiled above under APT10.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Activity from a trusted provider's accounts is not trusted activity. Know which provider accounts and tools touch your environment, and when they should.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2017/04/27/intrusions-affecting-multiple-victims-across-multiple-sectors" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA TA17-117A</a></article>
    <article id="colonial-pipeline" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2021"><h3>Colonial Pipeline</h3><p>May 2021 ransomware incident attributed to DarkSide that hit the IT systems of one of the largest fuel pipeline operators in the U.S. The company preemptively shut down operations, causing East Coast shortages. Initial access came through a compromised VPN password. A $4.4M ransom was paid and partly recovered by the U.S. government. Spurred new cybersecurity mandates for pipeline operators.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A legacy VPN account with no MFA, no longer in use, was the way in. A dormant account that still authenticates deserves an alert, and IT-side ransomware can stop operations even when OT is untouched.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa21-131a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA21-131A</a></article>
    <article id="equifax-breach" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2017"><h3>Equifax breach</h3><p>2017 breach exposing PII (SSNs, birth dates, addresses) for 147 million Americans. Caused by failure to patch Apache Struts (CVE-2017-5638). Attackers operated undetected for roughly 76 days. Handling and disclosure drew widespread criticism. Resulted in a historic $700M settlement and regulatory reforms focused on data protection.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A known, patchable flaw in an internet-facing application, and an expired certificate on the inspection device that left the traffic unmonitored for months. A monitoring gap is a finding, not background noise.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.ftc.gov/enforcement/refunds/equifax-data-breach-settlement" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: US FTC</a></article>
    <article id="hafnium-exchange" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2021"><h3>HAFNIUM Exchange</h3><p>Early 2021 ProxyLogon chain of zero-day exploits in Microsoft Exchange Server (CVE-2021-26855, -26857, -26858, -27065), first used by HAFNIUM (now Silk Typhoon) and then by many other groups. Tens of thousands of organizations were affected worldwide across government, academia and business. Web shells were deployed for data theft, persistence, and lateral movement, and Microsoft issued out-of-band patches. Not to be confused with the later ProxyShell wave (August 2021). Exchange Server 2016 and 2019 reached end of support in October 2025, so surviving on-premises servers carry this risk unpatched.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> After a mass-exploited server flaw, patching is not enough: hunt for web shells and new accounts planted before the patch.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa21-062a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA21-062A</a></article>
    <article id="log4shell" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2021"><h3>Log4Shell</h3><p>CVE-2021-44228, a critical remote code execution (RCE) flaw in Apache Log4j. Attacker-controlled input is interpolated into a log message that triggers a JNDI lookup to an attacker's server. On older JVMs that server hands back a class to run; on patched JVMs exploitation used gadget chains, and even failed attempts leaked environment secrets through DNS lookups (a key triage clue). Disclosed December 2021 and exploited globally within hours, it affected vast swaths of Java applications, renewed calls for software bills of materials and open-source security funding, and reset assumptions about transitive-dependency risk.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Exploit strings arrived in any logged field (user agents, form fields, chat names). Outbound LDAP or DNS lookups from servers that never make them were the clearest early signal; knowing where a vulnerable library runs decides how fast you can scope the next one.</p><p>Technique detail: <a href="#log4shell-cve-2021-44228">Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228)</a>.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa21-356a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA21-356A</a></article>
    <article id="moveit-transfer-mass-exploitation" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2023"><h3>MOVEit Transfer mass exploitation</h3><p>May–June 2023 zero-day SQL-injection in Progress Software's MOVEit Transfer (CVE-2023-34362), exploited at scale by the Cl0p ransomware group. Thousands of organizations were affected through the vendor's customer base, including U.S. federal agencies, state DMVs and Fortune 500 firms. A defining case for third-party-software risk and for ransomware groups shifting from encryption to pure data extortion. The actor is profiled above under Cl0p.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The first evidence many victims saw was the extortion email. Know which internet-facing file-transfer servers you run, and hunt them the day an advisory lands.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa23-158a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA23-158A</a></article>
    <article id="notpetya" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2017"><h3>NotPetya</h3><p>Destructive cyberattack masquerading as ransomware, attributed to Russia's GRU Sandworm. Spread via a compromised update to Ukrainian accounting software (MeDoc), then jumped globally. It irreversibly wiped disks despite the ransom note. Maersk, Merck and FedEx were among the victims, damages ran into the billions, and it remains a defining case study in supply-chain abuse.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A ransom note does not make an event ransomware: check whether recovery is even possible. A trusted vendor's software update can be the infection path.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2017/07/01/petya-ransomware" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2017)</a></article>
    <article id="okta-support-system-breach" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2023"><h3>Okta support-system breach</h3><p>October 2023 intrusion of Okta's customer support case-management system. Attackers used a service-account credential that an employee had saved to their personal Google account, which was signed in to Chrome on the employee's Okta-managed laptop. They used it to access HAR files that customers had uploaded for troubleshooting. Some of those files contained valid session tokens. Okta later disclosed that the attacker also downloaded a report containing the names and email addresses of all its customer-support-system users. Drove home that "support" surfaces handle production credentials and need the same identity-provider rigor as the products themselves.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Files customers upload to support (HAR files, logs) can hold live session tokens. Sanitize them before upload, and treat a support-system breach as possible session theft for every customer whose files it held.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://sec.okta.com/articles/2023/11/unauthorized-access-oktas-support-case-management-system-root-cause" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Okta RCA (2023)</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://sec.okta.com/articles/harfiles/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Okta Security</a></article>
    <article id="operation-aurora" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2010"><h3>Operation Aurora</h3><p>2009–2010 China-attributed intrusions targeting Google, Adobe, and dozens of others. Zero-day in Internet Explorer used to deploy backdoors and steal source code and IP. Google's public disclosure was a turning point in public discussion of state-sponsored industrial espionage and APTs.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Historical. It made targeted, state-sponsored intrusion part of corporate threat models: a browser zero-day against chosen employees, aimed at source code.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://googleblog.blogspot.com/2010/01/new-approach-to-china.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Google (2010)</a></article>
    <article id="operation-shadowhammer" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2019"><h3>Operation ShadowHammer</h3><p>2019 supply-chain compromise of the ASUS software update utility. Compromised updates were signed and shipped from ASUS's own servers, reaching hundreds of thousands. The payload activated only on specific targets (matched by MAC address), suggesting selective espionage. It combined stolen code-signing certificates with supply-chain trust and has been linked to nation-state-capable APT activity.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A vendor-signed update from the vendor's own server can still be malicious. The signature and the source tell you who shipped it, not whether it is safe.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://securelist.com/operation-shadowhammer/89992/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Kaspersky Securelist</a></article>
    <article id="opm-breach" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2015"><h3>OPM breach</h3><p>2015 breaches exposing 21.5 million SF-86 background-investigation records, including 5.6 million fingerprint sets, plus 4.2 million personnel files. Attributed to Chinese state-sponsored actors, the stolen files gave a foreign intelligence service a map of who holds U.S. security clearances. Major reforms in U.S. government cybersecurity practices and incident response readiness followed.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Some stolen data can never be reset. Fingerprints and background-investigation files stay sensitive for life, which raises the impact of any access to them.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.gao.gov/products/gao-17-614" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: US GAO</a></article>
    <article id="salesloft-drift-oauth-theft" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2025"><h3>Salesloft Drift OAuth-token theft</h3><p>August 2025. An actor tracked by Google as UNC6395 used OAuth tokens stolen from the Salesloft Drift chat integration to export data from hundreds of customers' Salesforce instances, then searched the data for secrets such as cloud access keys and passwords. No victim password or MFA was involved: the trusted SaaS-to-SaaS integration was the way in.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Third-party OAuth grants are credentials. Bulk API exports by an integration's identity, outside its normal pattern, are the signal, and revoking and rotating the grants is part of containment.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: GTIG (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="shai-hulud-npm-worm" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2025"><h3>Shai-Hulud npm worm</h3><p>September 2025. A self-propagating worm in the npm ecosystem stole developer and continuous integration (CI) tokens from infected machines, then used stolen npm credentials to publish trojanized versions of the victims' own packages, compromising more than 500 packages.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> In a package ecosystem, a developer workstation is an initial-access vector for every downstream consumer. Install scripts that read tokens or publish packages from a workstation or CI runner are the tell; npm, GitHub and cloud tokens on an affected host must be rotated.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/09/23/widespread-supply-chain-compromise-impacting-npm-ecosystem" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="sharepoint-toolshell" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2025"><h3>SharePoint ToolShell</h3><p>July 2025 mass exploitation of on-premises Microsoft SharePoint (CVE-2025-53770, a variant of the CVE-2025-49704/-49706 chain). Attackers planted web shells and stole the servers' ASP.NET machine keys, which let them keep forging valid requests even after patching. Microsoft attributed exploitation to several China-based actors, one of which deployed ransomware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> For edge and server zero-days, patching is not remediation. Rotate the stolen keys (here the ASP.NET machine keys) and hunt for web shells, such as spinstall0.aspx, planted before the patch.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/07/20/update-microsoft-releases-guidance-exploitation-sharepoint-vulnerability-cve-2025-53770" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="snowflake-customer-account-theft" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2024"><h3>Snowflake customer-account theft</h3><p>April–June 2024. A threat group later tracked as UNC5537 used credentials harvested from infostealer logs to access Snowflake customer environments that had no MFA configured. Victims included Ticketmaster, Santander, AT&amp;T and LendingTree. This was not a Snowflake CVE: it was a customer-side identity-hygiene failure at scale. Snowflake responded by making MFA the default and phasing out password-only sign-ins.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Infostealer logs turned old passwords into working sign-ins wherever MFA was missing. Password-only sign-ins to a SaaS data platform from an unfamiliar client deserve an alert.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/unc5537-snowflake-data-theft-extortion" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Mandiant / Google</a></article>
    <article id="solarwinds-sunburst" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2020"><h3>SolarWinds SUNBURST</h3><p>Late-2020 supply-chain compromise of SolarWinds Orion. A trojanized update gave APT29 (then tracked as UNC2452) backdoor access to thousands of customers including U.S. government agencies and Fortune 500 firms. Against a chosen few it then ran months of stealthy command-and-control, lateral movement with forged SAML tokens, and data exfiltration. It reshaped trust assumptions around software vendors.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The intrusion arrived in a signed update, then moved to the cloud with forged SAML tokens (Golden SAML). Sign-ins with no matching identity-provider event, and unusual DNS from a management server, were the signals.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa20-352a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA20-352A</a></article>
    <article id="sony-pictures-hack" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2014"><h3>Sony Pictures hack</h3><p>2014 destructive attack attributed to North Korea's Lazarus Group, reportedly in retaliation for The Interview. Wiper malware destroyed systems while the attackers leaked unreleased films and executive communications, blending political retaliation, information warfare, and corporate sabotage. It was the first high-profile nation-state attack on a private company over content.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Destruction and data theft arrived together. A wiper case can also be a breach case, so scope what left as well as what was destroyed.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/update-on-sony-investigation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: FBI (2014)</a></article>
    <article id="storm-0558-microsoft-cloud-email" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2023"><h3>Storm-0558 / Antique Typhoon (Microsoft cloud email)</h3><p>July 2023 intrusion in which a China-aligned actor (now tracked by Microsoft as Antique Typhoon) forged Azure AD (now Microsoft Entra ID) access tokens using a stolen Microsoft consumer-MSA signing key, reading emails of ~25 organizations including U.S. State Department and Commerce officials via Outlook Web Access. The key should not have signed enterprise tokens; a validation gap let it. The U.S. Cyber Safety Review Board's April 2024 review detailed the Microsoft operational and strategic decisions that led to the intrusion. It reshaped how the industry talks about identity-provider key custody, token binding, and the blast radius of a single signing key.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A customer found it first: a U.S. State Department team spotted unusual mailbox access in audit logs (MailItemsAccessed) it had paid to enable. Logging you have not enabled is evidence you will not have.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2023/07/14/analysis-of-storm-0558-techniques-for-unauthorized-email-access/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: Microsoft (2023)</a></article>
    <article id="stuxnet" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2010"><h3>Stuxnet</h3><p>Landmark cyberweapon widely attributed to a joint U.S.-Israeli operation (never officially acknowledged) to disrupt Iran's nuclear enrichment. Multiple zero-days and PLC-specific payloads targeted Siemens controllers at Natanz. It altered centrifuge speeds while reporting normal readings: the first malware known to cause real-world industrial damage, and the template for later ICS-targeting malware.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The monitoring screens were part of the attack. When the impact claim rests on what a system reports about itself, look for an independent view of the same process.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/ics-advisories/icsa-10-272-01" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA ICSA-10-272-01</a></article>
    <article id="target-breach" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2013"><h3>Target breach</h3><p>2013 breach exposing payment card data for about 40 million accounts and, as a separate data set, names, mailing addresses, phone numbers or email addresses for up to 70 million people. Initial access came through a compromised HVAC contractor's credentials; the attackers moved laterally to POS systems, where malware on the registers captured card data. A landmark for third-party risk management and network segmentation, it cost hundreds of millions and led to executive resignations.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The malware alerts fired, and the response did not follow. A contractor's credentials opened the network; the triage failure was alerts left unworked.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.commerce.senate.gov/services/files/24d3c229-4f2f-405d-b8db-a3a67f183883" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: U.S. Senate Commerce Committee (2014)</a></article>
    <article id="ukraine-power-grid" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2015"><h3>Ukraine power grid</h3><p>2015 and 2016 attacks marking the first known successful cyberattacks to cause power outages. Attributed to Russia's Sandworm. In 2015, spear-phishing planted BlackEnergy 3 in the business networks. Stolen credentials and VPN access then let the attackers reach the ICS networks and open breakers remotely, hands-on-keyboard, through the utilities' own remote-administration tools and HMI software. KillDisk wipes, firmware-bricked serial-to-Ethernet devices, and a telephone denial-of-service against a call center slowed the response. The utilities restored service by switching to manual operations. About 225,000 customers lost power. In 2016, Industroyer / CrashOverride automated the attack against grid-specific protocols. Defining examples of cyber warfare against civilian infrastructure.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The attackers used the operators' own remote-administration tools. In OT, a legitimate tool used at the wrong time or by the wrong account is the signal, and recovery may mean manual operation.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/ics-alerts/ir-alert-h-16-056-01" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA IR-ALERT-H-16-056-01</a></article>
    <article id="volt-typhoon-salt-typhoon" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2023-25"><h3>Volt Typhoon / Salt Typhoon</h3><p>Two long-running China-linked campaigns against critical infrastructure. <strong>Volt Typhoon:</strong> disclosed May 2023 by Microsoft and CISA, it pre-positioned in U.S. energy, water, communications, and transportation networks using living-off-the-land techniques for stealthy persistence. <strong>Salt Typhoon:</strong> disclosed in late 2024, it compromised major U.S. telecom carriers' lawful-intercept systems with potential access to call metadata and the federal wiretap apparatus. An August 2025 advisory co-sealed by agencies from 13 countries tied overlapping activity to compromises of backbone, provider-edge and customer-edge routers across telecommunications, government, transportation, lodging and military networks worldwide, mostly through publicly known CVEs rather than zero-days. Together they reframed the boundary between espionage and prepositioning for disruptive action.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> Pre-positioning looks like administration. Valid accounts, built-in tools and network-device access with no visible data theft do not make the impact low: the purpose may be disruption later.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa24-038a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA24-038A (2024)</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa25-239a" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA AA25-239A (2025)</a></article>
    <article id="wannacry" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2017"><h3>WannaCry</h3><p>May 2017 ransomware outbreak using EternalBlue (NSA SMB exploit leaked by Shadow Brokers). It wormed across networks, encrypting files in 150+ countries, and hit the UK NHS hard. Microsoft had released the patch two months earlier, but many systems were unpatched. Attributed to North Korea's Lazarus Group, it was a wake-up call on patch hygiene.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> A worm leaves no time for queue order. Confirmed spread from host to host meets the lateral-movement criterion, so the case escalates at once, before the ransom notes multiply.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2017/05/12/indicators-associated-wannacry-ransomware" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2017)</a></article>
    <article id="xz-utils-backdoor" class="bad-campaign" data-year="2024"><h3>XZ Utils backdoor</h3><p>March 2024. A multi-year social-engineering campaign by a fictitious maintainer ("Jia Tan") inserted a stealthy backdoor (CVE-2024-3094) into xz/liblzma 5.6.0-5.6.1. It reached rolling and pre-release distributions (Debian testing/unstable, Fedora Rawhide, Kali, openSUSE Tumbleweed) but no major stable release. On systems where a distro-patched sshd loads liblzma through libsystemd, it hooked SSH RSA key verification to give whoever held the attacker's private key pre-authentication remote code execution. Caught by chance by a Microsoft engineer noticing a 500ms slowdown. A defining open-source supply-chain case and the catalyst for renewed maintainer-trust scrutiny.</p><p class="bad-signal"><strong>Triage lesson:</strong> The tell was performance: a half-second SSH slowdown. A small, unexplained change in a trusted component deserves a question, and a trusted maintainer is not a guarantee.</p><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2024/03/29/reported-supply-chain-compromise-affecting-xz-utils-data-compression-library-cve-2024-3094" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: CISA (2024)</a><a class="g-cite" href="https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2024/03/29/4" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Source: oss-security (2024)</a></article>
  </div>

  {/* Featured animations */}
  {/* Featured: Stuxnet centrifuge */}
  <article class="bad-feature">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 320 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="A centrifuge swings between 120 and 84,600 RPM while the operators' monitor shows a NORMAL 63,800 RPM." class="bad-anim bad-anim--stux">
        {/* centrifuge tube */}
        <rect x="100" y="30" width="60" height="130" rx="30" fill="none" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="2"/>
        {/* spinning rotor */}
        <g class="bad-anim__rotor" transform-origin="130 95">
          <line x1="130" y1="60" x2="130" y2="130" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="3" stroke-linecap="round"/>
          <line x1="105" y1="95" x2="155" y2="95" stroke="#ef4444" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round"/>
          <circle cx="130" cy="95" r="6" fill="#7f1d1d" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
        </g>
        <text x="130" y="180" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">centrifuge</text>
        {/* monitor showing fake "normal" reading */}
        <g>
          <rect x="200" y="50" width="100" height="60" rx="6" fill="#0a0303" stroke="#fca5a5" stroke-width="1.5"/>
          <text x="250" y="68" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#22c55e">monitor</text>
          <text x="250" y="85" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12" fill="#22c55e">NORMAL</text>
          <text x="250" y="100" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#86efac">63,800 RPM</text>
        </g>
        <text x="250" y="135" text-anchor="middle" font-size="9" fill="#fca5a5">↑ what operators saw</text>
        <text x="250" y="165" text-anchor="middle" font-size="10" fill="#ef4444" class="bad-anim__truth">actual: 120 / 84,600 RPM</text>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>Stuxnet</h3>
      <p>The watershed cyber-physical weapon. Stuxnet altered centrifuge speeds at Iran's Natanz facility while feeding operators normal readings on the SCADA (supervisory control and data acquisition) human-machine interfaces (HMIs). The gap between what the equipment was doing and what the monitors reported is the breakthrough that turned malware into a tool of kinetic effect.</p>
    </div>
  </article>

  {/* Featured: WannaCry worm spread */}
  <article class="bad-feature bad-feature--reverse">
    <div class="bad-feature__art">
      <svg viewBox="0 0 240 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="WannaCry spreads on its own from patient zero to every unpatched host it can reach over SMB." class="bad-anim bad-anim--worm">
        {/* 6x5 grid of host dots */}
        <g class="bad-anim__hosts">
          {/* Row 1 */}
          <circle cx="40"  cy="40" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--14"/>
          <circle cx="80"  cy="40" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
          <circle cx="120" cy="40" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--10"/>
          <circle cx="160" cy="40" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
          <circle cx="200" cy="40" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--14"/>
          {/* Row 2 */}
          <circle cx="40"  cy="80" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
          <circle cx="80"  cy="80" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--6"/>
          <circle cx="120" cy="80" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--4"/>
          <circle cx="160" cy="80" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--6"/>
          <circle cx="200" cy="80" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
          {/* Row 3 - patient zero */}
          <circle cx="40"  cy="120" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--10"/>
          <circle cx="80"  cy="120" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--4"/>
          <circle cx="120" cy="120" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--0"/>
          <circle cx="160" cy="120" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--4"/>
          <circle cx="200" cy="120" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--10"/>
          {/* Row 4 */}
          <circle cx="40"  cy="160" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
          <circle cx="80"  cy="160" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--6"/>
          <circle cx="120" cy="160" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--4"/>
          <circle cx="160" cy="160" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--6"/>
          <circle cx="200" cy="160" r="10" class="bad-worm-host bad-worm-host--12"/>
        </g>
      </svg>
    </div>
    <div class="bad-feature__text">
      <span class="bad-feature__tag">FEATURED</span>
      <h3>WannaCry: worm spread</h3>
      <p>WannaCry used the EternalBlue exploit (MS17-010) to propagate through SMB to any unpatched host it could reach. From patient zero, it spread autonomously by scanning local subnets and random IPs, infecting roughly 200,000 systems across 150 countries within 24 hours of the May 12, 2017 outbreak. By the time most SOCs had triaged the first alert, the second wave of hosts was already encrypting. That speed is what "wormable" means in practice: the malware does its own lateral movement; defenders do not get the usual reaction window.</p>
    </div>
  </article>
</section>

{/* Self-check */}
<section id="selfcheck" class="bad-section">

<Quiz title="B.A.D. self-check" subtitle="Ten questions across initial access, credentials, command and control, exfiltration, evasion and data exposure. No grades; the point is retention.">

  <QuizItem
    prompt="Why does Pass-the-Hash work without the attacker ever cracking a password?"
    options={[
      { value: "protocol", label: "The protocol accepts the hash itself as the credential, so a hash stolen on one host works on another." },
      { value: "weak",     label: "NTLM hashes are weak enough that any laptop can reverse them into the plaintext password within seconds." },
      { value: "mfa",      label: "Password checks are skipped entirely on any account that does not have MFA enforced for sign-in." },
      { value: "keylog",   label: "It is really a keylogging technique that captures the password as the user types it in." },
    ]}
    answer="protocol"
    explanation="The NTLM hash is the credential. An attacker who reads a hash from one host's memory can authenticate to other hosts with it directly; the plaintext never enters the picture, so password strength is irrelevant. That is what makes the technique silent and the backbone of many lateral-movement campaigns: the stolen hash keeps working on every host where that account is valid until the password (and with it the hash) is changed. Unique local-admin passwords (LAPS), Credential Guard, and Microsoft's ongoing NTLM deprecation all shrink the blast radius."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="An attacker obtains the krbtgt account hash in an Active Directory domain. What can they do, and what is the real fix?"
    options={[
      { value: "one",   label: "Impersonate that one service account until its tickets expire; the fix is disabling the account outright." },
      { value: "crack", label: "Crack it offline into the domain-admin password; the fix is a longer password policy for all admins." },
      { value: "forge", label: "Forge tickets as any user; the fix is evicting them, then resetting krbtgt twice, spaced apart." },
      { value: "read",  label: "Passively decrypt all Kerberos traffic; the fix is enforcing AES encryption on every domain controller." },
    ]}
    answer="forge"
    explanation="The krbtgt hash signs every Ticket Granting Ticket in the domain, so holding it lets the attacker mint tickets as any user, with any lifetime: the Golden Ticket. Forged tickets are self-contained, so changing user passwords does nothing. The fix is rotating the krbtgt password twice, but only after the attacker's access is removed (otherwise they simply re-harvest the new key) and with at least the maximum ticket lifetime (10 hours by default) plus full replication between the two resets. One reset is not enough because krbtgt's password history keeps the two most recent keys. Two resets in quick succession break every legitimate ticket in the domain at once. Done properly the procedure is routine; that it is so rarely done is why Golden Tickets make such durable persistence."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="Malware using a Domain Generation Algorithm (DGA) defeats static domain blocklists. How?"
    options={[
      { value: "fast",     label: "It rotates through thousands of pre-registered domains faster than any blocklist can update." },
      { value: "tor",      label: "It routes all of its C2 over Tor, so there is never a domain name to block in the first place." },
      { value: "ip",       label: "It connects by raw IP address only, and DNS blocklists can only ever match domain names." },
      { value: "computes", label: "It computes a fresh list of names each day; the attacker registers only one of them." },
    ]}
    answer="computes"
    explanation="The malware and the attacker share the algorithm and the seed. Each day or hour the malware computes the same candidate list the attacker does, tries them all, and reaches whichever single name the attacker actually registered. Blocking yesterday's domains accomplishes little because tomorrow's list is already different, unless someone reverse-engineers the algorithm and pre-blocks future names. Detection shifts to the search itself: bursts of failed (NXDOMAIN) lookups for machine-generated names, which usually look random, though some DGAs stitch together dictionary words to defeat entropy checks."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="What distinguishes password spraying from a classic brute-force attack?"
    options={[
      { value: "speed",   label: "Speed: spraying uses GPU cracking rigs, while classic brute force runs from one single machine." },
      { value: "inverse", label: "A few common passwords are tried across many accounts, staying under each lockout threshold." },
      { value: "offline", label: "Spraying runs offline against a stolen hash database, while brute force is always live." },
      { value: "svc",     label: "Spraying targets service accounts only, because those accounts can never be locked out." },
    ]}
    answer="inverse"
    explanation="Classic brute force hammers one account with many passwords and trips the lockout in minutes. Spraying inverts the shape: one or two plausible passwords against hundreds of accounts, then a pause, then the next password. Each account sees so few failures that lockouts never fire, which is why detection depends on looking across accounts rather than at any single one: many usernames failing in the same window with the same error (bad password), whether from one source or spread across many rotating IPs (residential proxies). Aggregate by tenant, failure code, user agent and timing, not just by source IP."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="A user typed their password and approved the MFA prompt on what turned out to be an AiTM phishing page. Is the account safe because MFA was satisfied?"
    options={[
      { value: "safe",    label: "Yes: the user completed MFA themselves, so the attacker cannot sign in without that second factor." },
      { value: "reset",   label: "Partly: a password reset on its own ends the attacker's access, because the session depends on it." },
      { value: "exposed", label: "No: the proxy captured the session cookie, so the account stays exposed until sessions are revoked." },
      { value: "device",  label: "No, but only on that device: a stolen session can never be replayed from another machine or network." },
    ]}
    answer="exposed"
    explanation="An adversary-in-the-middle kit relays the real sign-in page, so the user's own password and MFA approval produce a valid session, and the proxy keeps the cookie. Replayed from attacker infrastructure, that cookie skips both factors, and a password reset alone may leave it working. Any user who reached the credential step of a phishing kit is credential-exposed until the password is reset, sessions are revoked and MFA methods are reviewed. Look for the same session used from a new IP or device, new MFA methods and new inbox rules. Phishing-resistant MFA (passkeys, certificate-based sign-in) is what defeats the technique."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="An alert shows a new 4 GB .7z archive in ProgramData on a file server, followed minutes later by rclone.exe making sustained outbound connections. What is most likely happening?"
    options={[
      { value: "backup", label: "A scheduled backup job: archiving first and then syncing to cloud storage is normal IT practice." },
      { value: "update", label: "A software update: the archive is an installer, and rclone is fetching the remaining parts." },
      { value: "exfil",  label: "Collection, then exfiltration: data is staged in an archive and uploaded to cloud storage." },
      { value: "c2",     label: "Command and control: the archive holds the attacker's toolkit, and rclone is the beacon to C2." },
    ]}
    answer="exfil"
    explanation="Archive Collected Data followed by Exfiltration to Cloud Storage is the standard pattern before data-theft extortion. rclone is a legitimate sync tool, which is exactly why attackers like it; on a server with no reason to run it, pointed at MEGA or an unfamiliar cloud account, it is the exfiltration. Establish what went into the archive and where the uploads went, and treat the case as possible data theft whether or not ransomware follows."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="A phishing domain resolves to a different set of IP addresses every few minutes. Why does blocking those IPs fail, and what works better?"
    options={[
      { value: "domain", label: "The IPs are compromised hosts that rotate constantly; block the domain and pivot on its name servers." },
      { value: "cdn",    label: "The domain sits behind a CDN, so the IPs are shared; ask the CDN to block the site and then wait." },
      { value: "dga",    label: "It is a DGA, so the IPs follow a formula; reverse the algorithm and block the next IP addresses." },
      { value: "tor",    label: "The IPs are Tor exit nodes; blocking every known Tor exit node at the perimeter makes the site unreachable." },
    ]}
    answer="domain"
    explanation="Fast flux puts a large pool of compromised machines or proxies behind one name and swaps the DNS answers every few minutes, so any IP you block is already stale. The stable things are the domain itself and, for double flux, its name servers and registration details. Block and sinkhole at the domain level, and pivot on registrar, name server and certificate data to find sibling domains."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="A user gets a call from someone claiming to be IT support. Minutes later Quick Assist, then AnyDesk, run on their laptop. Both are signed and allowed. What is the right read?"
    options={[
      { value: "context",   label: "Suspicious: an unrequested remote session after a call is RMM abuse until IT can account for it." },
      { value: "signed",    label: "Benign: signed and allowlisted remote tools cannot be abused, so this is routine IT support." },
      { value: "av",        label: "Wait for antivirus: if the binaries were malicious, the endpoint agent would have quarantined them." },
      { value: "uninstall", label: "Uninstall both tools right away and close the alert; removing the software ends the risk." },
    ]}
    answer="context"
    explanation="Help-desk impersonators, callback-phishing crews and ransomware affiliates use legitimate remote-access tools as their command channel precisely because the tools are signed and often allowed. Nothing malicious is in the binary; the evidence is the sequence: a call or chat claiming to be IT, a remote session the help desk did not open, then a second tool installed to keep access. Ask IT whether they opened a session, scope what the remote operator did, and preserve the session logs before anything is removed."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="A suspicious attachment does nothing in the sandbox: no network traffic, no child processes, no file writes. What should you conclude?"
    options={[
      { value: "benign",       label: "It is benign: the sandbox ran the real file, so no behavior there means no behavior on any endpoint either." },
      { value: "broken",       label: "The sample is corrupted, so it cannot run on any host and the alert can be closed as noise." },
      { value: "retry",        label: "The sandbox must be blocking it; resubmit the same file until a run finally shows behavior." },
      { value: "inconclusive", label: "Nothing yet: it may be detecting the sandbox or waiting it out, so silence is not innocence." },
    ]}
    answer="inconclusive"
    explanation="Malware routinely checks for virtual machines, analysis tools, a real user's mouse movement or a long uptime (Environment Checks), or simply sleeps past the sandbox's time limit (Delayed Execution). A silent run is inconclusive. Look at static indicators, check whether the sample queried VM artifacts or called long sleeps, and hunt for the same hash or behavior on real endpoints. Silent in the sandbox but active on the endpoint is evidence of evasion, not innocence."
  />

  <QuizItem
    prompt="An employee emails a spreadsheet of patient records to the wrong outside address by mistake. Is it a leak or a breach?"
    options={[
      { value: "leak",    label: "Only a leak: no attacker stole anything, so no breach process or notification duty can apply." },
      { value: "both",    label: "Possibly both: an accidental PHI leak can be a reportable breach, so escalate for a legal decision." },
      { value: "breach",  label: "Always a breach: any email sent outside the company is automatically a notifiable data breach." },
      { value: "neither", label: "Neither: a single misdirected email is too small to count, so close it with a reminder to the user." },
    ]}
    answer="both"
    explanation="Leak describes how the data got out; breach is a legal determination about what that exposure means. Under HIPAA, GDPR and most US state laws, an accidental disclosure of protected data can be a breach, and scale does not decide it. The analyst's job is to establish the facts (what data, how many people, who received it, whether it was retrieved or deleted) and escalate so privacy or legal counsel can decide whether a notification clock has started."
  />

</Quiz>

</section>

{/* Closing */}
<footer class="bad-foot">
  <p>The B.A.D. Glossary is a living reference, last reviewed October 2026 against MITRE ATT&amp;CK Enterprise v19.2. The vocabulary evolves with every campaign, every CVE, every shift in the threat landscape. Contributions and corrections are welcome on <a href="https://github.com/block/assured-methodology" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">GitHub</a>.</p>
</footer>

</div>

<GlossaryPageScript page="bad" />
